Localisation of the Third Way Theory
Dimitri Kanakaris Decavel

Abstract




Disclaimer

This version of my thesis has been reviewed for typos and errors for the purpose of uploading it on arXiv. However, the content itself hasn’t been meaningfully altered. If you’d happen to find another typo or error feel free to contact me.

Abstract

The following is a master thesis centered around the concept of localisation and the Third Way Theory.
This thesis discusses various aspects of supersymmetric localisation in one and three dimensions, and contains original results with regards to the Third Way Theory.
It starts off with the Witten index for a one-dimensional supersymmetric system and derives various aspects through localisation.
After this, the thesis moves on to the Third Way Theory. First, it offers a review of the Third Way Theory, a deformation of topologically massive Yang-Mills theory in three dimensions. Then it moves on to original results. These include a supersymmetrisation of the Third Way Theory and consequently a localisation of the Third Way Theory, which is to say, a method of deriving non-perturbative results.

Chapter 1
Introduction to Localisation

1.1
Introduction

The main goal of this master thesis is to develop a method of doing non-perturbative computations in the Third Way Theory. This is a quantum field theory developed by Arvanitakis, Sevrin and Townsend
[
1
]
.
The technique which will be used for this —and one of the main themes of this thesis— will be localisation.
Localisation is a technique in quantum field theory which borrows from the concept of equivariant localisation in differential geometry.
Equivariant localisation is a technique for performing integration over Riemannian manifolds with isometries. One deforms the integrant such that its contributions localise to a lower dimensional —if not discrete— locus, all the while leaving the integral unchanged. This happens when the integrant shares its symmetries with the underlying geometry, and will localise to the points left unchanged by this symmetry.
As it turns out, this procedure can be generalised to infinite-dimensional field space, the space over which one integrates in quantum field theory when working in the path integral formalism.
This is interesting because generally path integrals are extremely difficult to compute exactly and one is typically forced to use a perturbative approach, which for example only works for weak couplings. Particularly, we consider supersymmetric quantum field theories. In this context a localisation technique exists in which the path integral can be deformed in such a way that it will only receive contributions from a lower dimensional —if not finite-dimensional or even discrete— subspace of field space. This space will consist of BPS configurations. These are configurations in field space which one could consider to be supersymmetric. The observables for which one could then do exact quantum computations will be the space of BPS operators. That is, operators which are invariant under supersymmetry. A way to think of this in more physical terms is that the 1-loop approximation becomes exact
[
2
]
.

In this chapter we introduce the necessary background concepts for doing localisations. These will include some basic facts about supersymmetry as well a general description of the localisation argument.

In chapter 2 we will consider as a warm up exercise the so called Witten index. This is an integer defined for a superparticle moving in a superpotential which is closely related to the partition function. We will show that this object can be computed through a localisation. The standard approach to this topic involves the Hilbert space formalism as well as the path integral formalism. However, in this thesis previously known results which have to our knowledge only been derived in the Hilbert space formalism will be derived from the path integral formalism. These constitute new computations in that they weren’t performed directly following a different source and could be new altogether.

In chapter 3 we will move on to describing the Third Way Theory, the theory we want to localise in this thesis. Particularly, we present a detailed description of its original conception by Arvanitakis, Sevrin and Townsend in
[
1
]
.

Finally, in chapter 4 we will try to localise the Third Way Theory. We will start off by giving a brief review of
d=3+0
,
{\mathcal{N}}=2
gauge theory, starting from a superconnection in superspace and arriving at the vector multiplet in Wess-Zumino gauge with its gauge covariant supersymmetry transformation. This supermultiplet will then be used to construct various standard supersymmetric actions and their supersymmetric localisations. After this we will move on to a toy model version of the Third Way Theory, which we coined Proca-Chern-Simons theory, which is simply Chern-Simons theory with a Proca mass term. A new kind of supersymmetry was then constructed, which we named massive supersymmetry, which is a supersymmetry of super-Proca-Chern-Simons theory. Interestingly this supersymmetry forces the Proca mass term upon us if one were to try and construct a super-Chern-Simons theory with this supersymmetry. After this we will move on to describe localisation in this theory. Then we will move on to supersymmetrisations of the Third Way Theory. We start off by presenting previous results by Arvanitakis, resulting in a so-called
\delta
-localisation of the Third Way Theory. After this, the reader will be presented with our own results, consisting of a generalisation of Proca-Chern-Simons theory to the Third Way Theory. This then leads to its superanalogue coined the Super-Third Way Theory, which is invariant under so-called Third Way supersymmetries. We will then use these supersymmetries to localise the Third Way Theory.

1.2
Some Basics of Supersymmetry

1.2.1
A Brief Motivation

A supersymmetry is simply defined as a symmetry between bosons and fermions. Bosons are spin-integer particles which make up the forces and Higgs boson in the Standard Model where on the other hand, fermions are spin-half-odd particles which make up matter in the Standard Model
[
3
]
[
4
]
.

There are a couple of motivations for supersymmetry. Firstly, there’s the concept of renormalisation in quantum field theory. Renormalisation is a way to make sense of divergent integrals which appear in quantum field theory. In this regard supersymmetry is promising because bosons and fermions tend to contribute in opposite signs to transition amplitudes and for some supersymmetric theories these concellations could be so strong that the theory is finite. Another motivation is that it would explain various unanswered questions in the Standard Model, such as the Higgs mass. Furthermore, it is also a predictive hypothesis, namely predicting that there exist yet to be detected superpartners to the current known field content of the Standard Model, which we’re looking for at the LHC. On the theoretical front, a great motivation for supersymmetry is the so called Coleman-Mandula theorem. This theorem states that —under mild assumptions— the symmetry algebra of a quantum field theory is generally the direct sum of the Poincaré algebra and an internal symmetry algebra. This would mean that gauge theory is restricted to Yang-Mills type gauge fields and gravity. However, there exists a loophole in this theorem, being that the symmetry algebra is assumed to be a Lie algebra. If one assumes that fermionic symmetries could exist, this no-go theorem can be bypassed. It’s quite possible that supersymmetry is the only extension to the symmetry algebras of quantum field theory. Supersymmetry also plays a key role in string theory and M-theory
[
3
]
[
5
]
[
6
]
.

Finally, there are applications of supersymmetry in other domains of physics. This is where for example localisation comes in. As a matter of fact localisation can be used to do non-perturbative computations in quantum field theories which aren’t necessarily supersymmetric. Namely, if the supersymmetric extension of a theory is physically equivalent to it’s non-supersymmetric version, one could use the techniques of localisation to do non-perturbative computations in such theories. One example of this is Chern-Simons theory, and it will in fact be in the spirit of this example that we will attempt to localise the Third Way Theory without altering its physics
[
7
]
[
8
]
.

1.2.2
Algebraic Aspects of Supersymmetry

Non-supersymmetric symmetry algebra

We will introduce supersymmetries through their algebra. We start off by considering the general form of a supersymmetry algebra. For non-supersymmetric quantum field theories one has a symmetry algebra given by

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{iso}}(d_{-},d_{+})\oplus{{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{int}}}

(1.1)

where —in accordance with the Coleman-Mandula theorem—
{\mathfrak{iso}}
denotes the Poincaré algebra for some metric
\eta_{ab}
of signature
(d_{-},d_{+})
and
{\mathfrak{g}}
a Lie algebra corresponding to internal symmetries
[
3
]
[
9
]
. We denote the generators of
{\mathfrak{iso}}
by translations
P_{a}
and rotations
M_{ab}
. These satisfy an algebra

\displaystyle[P_{a},P_{b}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[P_{a},M_{bc}]

\displaystyle=2\eta_{a[b}P_{c]}

\displaystyle[M_{ab},M^{cd}]

\displaystyle=-4\eta_{[a}{{}^{[c}}M_{b]}{{}^{d]}}

(1.2)

\displaystyle[T_{I},T_{J}]

\displaystyle=f_{IJ}{{}^{K}}T_{K}

\displaystyle[T_{I},P_{a}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[T_{I},M_{ab}]

\displaystyle=0

(1.3)

where
f_{IJ}{{}^{K}}
denote the structure constants of the Lie group
{{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{int}}}
and indices for the Poincaré generators are raised and lowered using
\eta_{ab}

[
9
]
.

Supersymmetry algebra for
d=0+3
,
{\mathcal{N}}=2

We now consider a supersymmetric extension of a symmetry algebra. Particularly, the case we will consider will be
{\mathcal{N}}=2
,
d=0+3
.
{\mathcal{N}}
refers to the number of supercharges and
d
refers to the spacetime dimension, in this case describing Euclidean space
\eta={\mathbbm{1}}_{3\times 3}
. The reason for using Euclidean signature is that this thesis will mainly be concerned with quantum field theory and hence path integrals, which need to be defined on a Wick rotated space to be well-defined. In this case we introduce two spinorial supercharges
Q_{\alpha}
and
{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\alpha}
, with
\alpha,\beta,\dots=1,2
referring to 2-spinor indices. For conventions I refer the reader to appendix
A.2.1
. These introduce new (anti)commutators

\displaystyle\{Q_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle=-2i\gamma^{a}{{}_{\alpha\beta}}P_{a}

\displaystyle\{Q_{\alpha},Q_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle=\{{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\beta}\}=0

(1.4)

\displaystyle[Q_{\alpha},M_{ab}]

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\epsilon_{abc}\gamma^{c}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}}Q_{\beta}

\displaystyle[Q_{\alpha},T_{I}]

\displaystyle=[{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\alpha},T_{I}]=0

(1.5)

\displaystyle[{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\alpha},M_{ab}]

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\epsilon_{abc}\gamma^{c}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}}{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\beta}

(1.6)

The most remarkable of these relations is the first, which states that supersymmetries square to translations. This algebra is an example of a Lie superalgebra. This is a generalisation of a Lie algebra, which may also contain fermionic generators
[
10
]
.

1.2.3
Representations: The Chiral and Antichiral Multiplets

Component fields

In this section we consider an example of a representation of supersymmetry on field content. Field content in supersymmetric theories appear in supermultiplets. These are multiplets of fields on which a representation of supersymmetry is represented which closes on the fields. Supersymmetry transformations typically take on the form

\displaystyle\delta(\text{boson})

\displaystyle=\text{fermion}

(1.7)

\displaystyle\delta(\text{fermion})

\displaystyle={\partial}(\text{boson})+\text{auxiliary}

(1.8)

\displaystyle\delta(\text{auxiliary})

\displaystyle={\partial}(\text{auxiliary})

(1.9)

By ‘auxiliary’, one means that the field in question won’t be dynamical. As an example we consider a fairly simple representation of supersymmetry
[
3
]
[
11
]
. Namely, we consider the chiral and antichiral multiplet. These multiplets respectively have the following field content:

•

a real scalar
\phi
, resp.
{\accentset{\sim}{\phi}}

•

a complex spinor
\psi
, resp.
{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}

•

a real auxiliary scalar
F
, resp.
{\accentset{\sim}{F}}

Translations will simply be represented as
P_{\mu}={\partial}_{\mu}
. Supersymmetry is represented slightly more complicatedly. The supersymmetry transformations are
parametrised
by constant
bosonic
spinors
\zeta
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}
. Note, however, that the
spinorial field content
(e.g.
\psi
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}
) is
fermionic
. The supersymmetry transformations are represented on the multiplets
(\phi,\psi,F)
and
({\accentset{\sim}{\phi}},{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}},{\accentset{\sim}{F}})
as

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\phi

\displaystyle=2\zeta\psi

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\phi}}

\displaystyle=2{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}\Big.

(1.10)

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\psi

\displaystyle=\zeta F+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\phi

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{F}}+i\zeta{\not{{\partial}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\phi}}\Big.

(1.11)

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}F

\displaystyle=-2i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\psi

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{F}}

\displaystyle=-2i\zeta{\not{{\partial}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}\Big.

(1.12)

A direct computation indeed yields

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},\delta_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\begin{pmatrix}\phi\\
\psi\\
F\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=2i\zeta{\not{{\partial}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\begin{pmatrix}\phi\\
\psi\\
F\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},\delta_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\begin{pmatrix}{\accentset{\sim}{\phi}}\\
{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}\\
{\accentset{\sim}{F}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=2i\zeta{\not{{\partial}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\begin{pmatrix}{\accentset{\sim}{\phi}}\\
{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}\\
{\accentset{\sim}{F}}\end{pmatrix}

(1.13)

verifying that the algebra is indeed represented in these fields. The reason for introducing bosonic spinors might be slightly counterintuitive, as for fields spinors are typically taken to be fermionic. However, in the case of parameters for supersymmetry the more fundamental choice turns out to be bosonic spinors. This is important because as we will later see the choice of bosonic spinors is fundamental to the workings of localisation.
One reason for this is so that one could normalise by dividing by
|\zeta|^{2}={\zeta^{\dagger}}{{}^{\alpha}}\zeta_{\alpha}
. It should further be noted then that supersymmetry variations are Grassmann-odd instead of Grassmann-even, that is to say,

\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\big\{AB\big\}=(\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}A)B+(-)^{|A|}A(\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}B).

(1.14)

One can obtain Grassmann-even supersymmetry transformations parametrised by fermionic spinors simply by multiplying the variation by a Grassmann parameter
\epsilon
:

\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\to\epsilon\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}=\delta_{\epsilon\zeta,\epsilon{\tilde{\zeta}}}
. However since the bosonic spinors are fundamental to the localisation arguments we’ll keep working with bosonic supersymmetry parameters.

Superspace approach

This is a very interesting case of a supersymmetry representation. But one could wonder,
isn’t this a little ad hoc?
It would be better if we had a way of finding the supersymmetry transformations from first principles, instead of guessing them out of thin air. One way one could ‘geometrise’ supersymmetry is through the construct of superspace. In the superspace approach one extends spacetime with Grassmann coordinates
\theta^{\alpha}
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{\alpha}}
. A supermultiplet is then described through a superfield
\Phi(x,\theta,{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}})
on superspace. A superfield can be expanded in powers of its Grassmann coordinates and the corresponding field valued coefficients of each term will correspond to a component field
[
3
]
[
11
]
.

Supersymmetry generators are represented through the differential operators

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}

\displaystyle={\partial}_{\alpha}+i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}:={\partial}_{\alpha}+i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}_{\beta}

(1.15)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}+i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}\theta:={\accentset{\sim}{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}+i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}\theta_{\beta}

(1.16)

One notes that these satisfy an algebra

\{Q_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\beta}\}=i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\beta}{{}^{\gamma}}\{{\partial}_{\alpha},\theta_{\gamma}\}+i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}{{}^{\gamma}}\{{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}_{\gamma},{\accentset{\sim}{{\partial}}}_{\beta}\}=-2i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha\beta}

(1.17)

Another important construct in superspace will be be a notion of differentiation which preserves supersymmetry. That is, a form of differentiation which (anti)commutes with supersymmetry. We introduce covariant derivatives as

\displaystyle D_{\mu}

\displaystyle={\partial}_{\mu}

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}

\displaystyle={\partial}_{\alpha}-i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}-i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}\theta

(1.18)

These indeed preserve supersymmetry as

\displaystyle[Q_{\alpha},{\partial}_{\mu}]

\displaystyle=\{Q_{\alpha},D_{\beta}\}=\{Q_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\beta}\}=0

(1.19)

\displaystyle[{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\alpha},{\partial}_{\mu}]

\displaystyle=\{{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\alpha},D_{\beta}\}=\{{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\beta}\}=0

(1.20)

We further also find anticommutators

\displaystyle\{D_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle=2i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\{D_{\alpha},D_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle=\{{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\beta}\}=0.

(1.21)

These tools will now allow us to define the chiral and antichiral multiplets in superspace. We start off by noticing that an unconstrained superfield
\Phi(x,\theta,{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}})
will not define an irreducible representation of the supersymetry algebra. Indeed, since the covariant spinorial derivatives
D_{\alpha}
and
{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\alpha}
anticommute with the supersymmetry generators we can use them to contrain the chiral and antichiral superfields
\Phi
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\Phi}}
as

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\alpha}\Phi(x,\theta,{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\Phi}}(x,\theta,{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}})

\displaystyle=0.

(1.22)

Solving this constraint for a most general expression of these fields might seem a little tricky at first glance. However, this is greatly simplified if one notices that

\displaystyle[{\partial}_{\alpha},e^{-i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}]

\displaystyle=e^{-i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}[{\partial}_{\alpha},-i{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta]=e^{-i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}

(1.23)

\displaystyle[{\accentset{\sim}{{\partial}}}_{\alpha},e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}]

\displaystyle=e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}[{\accentset{\sim}{{\partial}}}_{\alpha},+i{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta]=e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}\theta

(1.24)

so that

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}e^{-i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}

\displaystyle=e^{-i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}{\partial}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}

\displaystyle=e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\big({\partial}_{\alpha}-2i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}\big)

(1.25)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\alpha}e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}

\displaystyle=e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}{\accentset{\sim}{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\alpha}e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}

\displaystyle=e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\big({\accentset{\sim}{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}-2i{\not{{\partial}}}_{\alpha}\theta\big)

(1.26)

The constraints for the chiral and antichiral superfields are now trivially solved as

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\Big[\phi+2\psi\theta+F\theta^{2}\Big]

(1.27)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\Phi}}

\displaystyle=e^{-i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\Big[{\accentset{\sim}{\phi}}+2{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+F{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}\Big]

(1.28)

Consequently one finds that

\begin{split}\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\Phi&=(\zeta Q+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{Q}})\Phi\\
&=e^{i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\big(\zeta{\partial}+2i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta\big)\Big[\phi+2\psi\theta+F\theta^{2}\Big]\\
&=e^{i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\Big[2\zeta\psi+2\big(\zeta F+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\phi\big)\theta-2i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\psi\theta^{2}\Big]\\
&=:e^{i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\Big[\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\phi+2\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\psi\theta+\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}F\theta^{2}\Big]\end{split}

(1.29)

and similarly that

\begin{split}\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\Phi}}&=e^{-i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\Big[2\zeta\psi+2\big({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{F}}+i\zeta{\not{{\partial}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\phi}}\big){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}-2i\zeta{\not{{\partial}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}\Big]\\
&=:e^{i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\Big[\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\phi}}+2\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\psi}}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{F}}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}\Big]\end{split}

(1.30)

which reproduces the results from equations
1.10
-
1.12

[
3
]
.

1.3
Equivariant Localisation

In this section we introduce a finite-dimensional toy model for localisation. After doing so we move on next section to do this for supersymmetric theories. Doing so we will mostly follow Cremonesi’s lecture notes on introducing localisation
[
2
]
.

1.3.1
Stationary Phase Approximation

We start off by introducing the localisation argument for the finite-dimensional case. To do so we consider an integral over some
2\ell
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
M
, given by

Z_{f}(t)=\int_{M}{\mathrm{d}}^{2\ell}x\sqrt{g}e^{itf(x)}

(1.31)

which functions as a toy model for a partition function.
f
is here taken to be a Morse function, that is, it has a discrete set of stationary points
x_{k}
such that
{\mathrm{d}}f(x_{k})=0
. In this context one can regard
t
as
t=1/\hbar
. The stationary phase approximation can then simply be given by

Z_{f}(t)=\bigg(\frac{2\pi i}{t}\bigg)^{\ell}\sum_{x_{k}:{\mathrm{d}}f(x_{k})=0}(-i)^{\lambda(x_{k})}\frac{e^{itf(x_{k})}}{\sqrt{\det g^{-1}H_{f}(x_{k})}}+{\mathcal{O}}(t^{-\ell-1})

(1.32)

where we defined
\lambda(x_{k})=\#(\text{negative eigenvalues of $g^{-1}H_{f}(x_{k})$})
known as the Morse index at
x_{k}
. This result —which follows simply from solving Gaussian integrals— is known as stationary phase approximation. The way it can be interpret is that as we take
t\to\infty
, or equivalently,
\hbar\to 0
the contributions to this integral become localised to the stationary points of the Morse function
f
. This is analogous to the semi-classical approach in quantum field theory, in which one integrates over field configurations which minimise the action weighted by the 1-loop determinant of the quadratic fluctuations around these configurations
[
2
]
.

Illustrating example

As an illustrating example we consider the height function on the 2-sphere
[
2
]
. That is, we take
M=S^{2}
with a metric

{\mathrm{d}}s^{2}={\mathrm{d}}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta{\mathrm{d}}\varphi^{2}

(1.33)

and the Morse function to be the height function

f(\theta,\varphi)=\cos\theta.

(1.34)

with stationary points
\theta=0,\pi
. The partition function is then simply given by

Z_{f}(t)=\int_{S^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta{\mathrm{d}}\varphi~\sin\theta e^{it\cos\theta}=\frac{2\pi i}{t}\big(-e^{it}+e^{-it}\big)

(1.35)

which
precisely
agrees with the stationary phase approximation! This now raises the question,
how do we interpret this result?
It turns out that the correct interpretation is due to the work of Duistermaat and Heckman. The key things to notice is that
(1)
the manifold
S^{2}
has a U(1) isometry,
(2)
this isometry is also a symmetry of the height function and that
(3)
it has fixed points, in this case corresponding to the poles of the 2-sphere.

1.3.2
Equivariant Cohomology

We now move on to describe the above mentioned principles in a formal manner. We consider a
2\ell
-dimensional compact Riemannian manifold
M
without boundary, which has an Abelian (for simplicity) isometry group
G
.

On this manifold we would like to define the appropriate kind of cohomology suited for essentially reducing the integral from the manifold
M
to the coset space
M/G
. We further consider a Killing vector
V
, that is, a vector satisfying

0={\mathcal{L}}_{V}g_{\mu\nu}=2\nabla_{(\mu}V_{\nu)}

(1.36)

assumed to generate a U(1) symmetry. Let us further denote
\Omega(M)
the space of polyforms on
M
. On this space we define the
V
-equivariant exterior derivative
{\mathrm{d}}_{V}
as

{\mathrm{d}}_{V}:={\mathrm{d}}-\iota_{V}:\Omega(M)\to\Omega(M)

(1.37)

where
{\mathrm{d}}:\Omega(M)\to\Omega(M)
the exterior derivative on polyforms and
\iota_{V}:\Omega(M)\to\Omega(M)
the interior product by
V
. These respectively incerase and decrease the differential degree by one. We note that the
V
-equivariant exterior derivative has the interesting property

{\mathrm{d}}^{2}_{V}=-\{{\mathrm{d}},\iota_{V}\}=-{\mathcal{L}}_{V}

(1.38)

of squaring to the Lie derivative with respect to
-V
. We now wish to restrict to the space of polyforms on which
{\mathrm{d}}_{V}
is nilpotent. This motivates us to define the space of
V
-equivariant polyforms
\Omega_{V}(M)=\{\alpha\in\Omega(M)~|~{\mathcal{L}}_{V}\alpha=0\}
. Let
\alpha\in\Omega_{V}(M)
, we define

\displaystyle\alpha\text{ $V$-equivariantly closed}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{Ker}{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\big|_{\Omega_{V}(M)}

(1.39)

\displaystyle\alpha\text{ $V$-equivariantly exact}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{Im}{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\big|_{\Omega_{V}(M)}

(1.40)

This in turn allows us to define the
V
-equivariant cohomology ring

H_{V}(M)=\frac{\operatorname{Ker}{\mathrm{d}}_{V}|_{\Omega_{V}(M)}}{\operatorname{Im}{\mathrm{d}}_{V}|_{\Omega_{V}(M)}}

(1.41)

consisting of
V
-equivariantly closed polyforms modulo
V
-equivariantly exact polyforms. We further recall that integration over polyforms is defined as integrating over the top form:

\int_{M}\alpha:=\int_{M}\alpha_{2\ell}

(1.42)

where
\alpha_{r}
denotes the
r
-form component of the polyform
\alpha
. Noting that
({\mathrm{d}}_{V}\alpha)_{2\ell}={\mathrm{d}}\alpha_{2\ell-1}
we find that

\int_{M}{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\alpha=\int_{M}{\mathrm{d}}\alpha_{2\ell-1}=\int_{{\partial}M}\alpha_{2\ell-1}=0.

(1.43)

Thus, we conclude that the integral of a
V
-equivariant polyform
\alpha
only depends on its
V
-equivariant cohomology class
[\alpha]

[
2
]
!

1.3.3
Localisation Arguments for Equivariant Integrals

Now we introduce two localisation arguments for equivariant integrals
[
2
]
. Namely, the claim is that the integral’s contributions are localised to the zero locus of
V
:

M_{V}=\{x\in M~|~V|_{x}=0\}.

(1.44)

We now give two arguments for this claim:

First localisation argument

This argument is centered around proving a version of Poincaré’s lemma. Let
\alpha
be
V
-equivariantly closed:
{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\alpha=0
. We wish to show that it is equivariantly exact on the complement
M\setminus M_{V}
of the zero locus. We can define the 1-form dual to the Killing vector as

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=V_{\mu}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\mu}\in\Omega^{1}(M)

\displaystyle\eta(X)

\displaystyle=g(V,X)

(1.45)

We note that
\eta
is a
V
-equivariant polyform since
V
is a Killing vector:

{\mathcal{L}}_{V}\eta_{\mu}=V^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}V_{\mu}+\nabla_{\mu}V^{\nu}V_{\nu}=2V^{\nu}\nabla_{(\mu}V_{\nu)}=0.

(1.46)

Hence we find that
\eta\in\Omega_{V}(M)
. We further note that

{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta={\mathrm{d}}\eta-\iota_{V}\eta=-|V|^{2}+{\mathrm{d}}\eta.

(1.47)

Interestingly, for polyforms there exists a notion of inversion, but this is only if the 0-form component is non-vanishing and is defined through the terminating Taylor expansion of the inverse in its form components. It thus follows that one can invert
{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta
on the complement
M\setminus M_{V}
of the zero locus as

\frac{1}{{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta}=-\frac{1}{|V|^{2}}\Big(1-\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\eta}{|V|^{2}}\Big)^{-1}=-\frac{1}{|V|^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\Big(\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\eta}{|V|^{2}}\Big)^{k}.

(1.48)

This polyform is well-defined and is furthermore
V
-equivariantly closed. Indeed, one finds that

{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\frac{1}{{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta}=-\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}_{V}\eta}{({\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta)^{2}}=\frac{{\mathcal{L}}_{V}\eta}{({\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta)^{2}}=0.

(1.49)

We now define the polyform

\Theta_{V}:=\frac{\eta}{{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta}.

(1.50)

This polyform satisfies the interesting identity

{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\Theta_{V}=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta}{{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta}-\eta{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\frac{1}{{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta}=1+0=1

(1.51)

where we applied the graded Leibniz rule. We now come back to the
V
-equivariantly closed polyform
\alpha
. Using the aforementioned results it directly follows that
\alpha
is
V
equivariantly exact on
M\setminus M_{V}
:

\alpha=1\cdot\alpha={\mathrm{d}}_{V}\Theta_{V}\cdot\alpha={\mathrm{d}}_{V}(\Theta_{V}\alpha).

(1.52)

Hence, it follows that the contributions to the equivariant integral have to localise to the zero locus
M_{V}

[
2
]
.

Second localisation argument

This argument will be more direct than the previous localisation argument. It will also be more directly related to the generalisation to path integrals. This argument hinges on the previous observation that equivariant integrals only depend on the respective equivariant cohomology class. Let us for example consider an equivariant polyforms
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega_{V}(M)
. We note that we can deform
\alpha
without changing its equivariant cohomology class as

\alpha_{t}:=\alpha e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta}

(1.53)

To see this, we start off by noting that

{\mathrm{d}}_{V}e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta}={\mathrm{d}}_{V}(t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta)e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta}=t{\mathrm{d}}^{2}_{V}\beta e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta}=-t{\mathcal{L}}_{V}\beta e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta}=0.

(1.54)

From this it follows that

\alpha_{t}-\alpha=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}t~\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\alpha_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}t~{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta\alpha e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta}={\mathrm{d}}_{V}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}t~\beta\alpha e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta}

(1.55)

hence we conclude that this kind of deformation doesn’t change the equivariant cohomology class of
\alpha
:

[\alpha]=[\alpha e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta}]

(1.56)

and thus,

\int_{M}\alpha=\int_{M}\alpha e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta}.

(1.57)

If we now take
\beta=\eta
we find that

\int_{M}\alpha=\int_{M}\alpha e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta}=\int_{M}\alpha e^{-t|V|^{2}}e^{t{\mathrm{d}}\eta}.

(1.58)

Taking the limit
t\to\infty
we thus find that

\int_{M}\alpha=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{M}\alpha e^{-t|V|^{2}}e^{t{\mathrm{d}}\eta}.

(1.59)

Note that for all points on the manifold where
V
is nonzero, the corresponding contributions will be suppressed by the deformation of the form. This leaves us with contributions from the infinitesimal regions around the zero locus
M_{V}

[
2
]
.

Atiyah-Bott-Berline-Vergne localisation formula

Let us now compute the equivariant integral in question. Around the loci we use the ‘inertial’ Cartesian coordinate system in which the metric becomes

{\mathrm{d}}s^{2}\approx\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\big[{\mathrm{d}}x_{i}^{2}+{\mathrm{d}}y_{i}^{2}\big]=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\big[{\mathrm{d}}r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\varphi_{i}^{2}\big].

(1.60)

Around these loci we can furthermore also write the Killing vector
V
as

V\approx\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\omega_{k,i}\bigg[-y_{i}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x_{i}}+x_{i}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}y_{i}}\bigg]=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\omega_{k,i}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\varphi_{i}}.

(1.61)

Here the index
k
enumerates the zero locus
M_{V}=\{x_{k}\}
. The Killing vector generates approximately a linear transformation on these ‘inertial’ Cartesian coordinates given by the block diagonal matrix

L_{V,k}=\operatorname{diag}\Bigg(\begin{pmatrix}0&\omega_{k,1}\\
-\omega_{k,1}&0\end{pmatrix},\dots,\begin{pmatrix}0&\omega_{k,\ell}\\
-\omega_{k,\ell}&0\end{pmatrix}\Bigg).

(1.62)

We further note that the dual 1-form
\eta
is given around the
k^{\text{th}}
locus by

\eta\approx\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\omega_{k,i}\Big[-y_{i}{\mathrm{d}}x_{i}+x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}y_{i}\Big]=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\omega_{k,i}r_{i}^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\varphi_{i}.

(1.63)

from which one obtains an equivariant differential

{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta\approx\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\bigg[2\omega_{k,i}{\mathrm{d}}x_{i}{\mathrm{d}}y_{i}-\omega_{k,i}^{2}(x_{i}^{2}+y_{i}^{2})\bigg]=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\bigg[\omega_{k,i}{\mathrm{d}}(r_{i}^{2}){\mathrm{d}}\varphi_{i}-\omega_{k,i}^{2}r_{i}^{2}\bigg].

(1.64)

We now find that the equivariant integral can be computed as

\begin{split}\int_{M}\alpha&=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{M}\alpha e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta}=\sum_{x_{k}}\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{U_{k}}\alpha e^{t{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\eta}\\
&=\sum_{x_{k}}\lim_{t\to\infty}\alpha_{0}(x_{k})\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\int{\mathrm{d}}(tr_{i}^{2}){\mathrm{d}}\varphi_{i}~e^{-t\omega_{k,i}^{2}r_{i}^{2}}\\
&=\sum_{x_{k}}\alpha_{0}(x_{k})\frac{(2\pi)^{\ell}}{\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\omega_{k,i}}\end{split}

(1.65)

where
U_{k}
are sufficiently small neighbourhoods around
x_{k}
. We note that we can rewrite the product in this expression as

\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\omega_{k,i}=\operatorname{Pf}(-L_{V}(x_{k})).

(1.66)

Here the Pfaffian is defined on antisymmetric
2\ell\times 2\ell
matrices as

\operatorname{Pf}M=\varepsilon^{i_{1}\dots i_{2\ell}}M_{i_{1}i_{2}}\dots M_{i_{2\ell-1}i_{2\ell}}.

(1.67)

We thus find the following powerful result:

Atiyah-Bott-Berline-Vergne localisation formula

Let
M
be a Riemannian manifold with Killing vector
V
which generates a U(1) action with discrete zero locus
M_{V}
. Let furthermore
\alpha
be a
V
-equivariantly closed polyform. Its integral over
M
is then given by

\int_{M}\alpha=(2\pi)^{\ell}\sum_{x_{k}}\frac{\alpha_{0}(x_{k})}{\operatorname{Pf}(-L_{V}(x_{k}))}

(1.68)

where we defined
L_{V}(x_{k})
as in
1.62

[
2
]
.

Duistermaat-Heckman localisation formula

As a corrolary to the Atiyah-Bott-Berline-Vergne localisation formula we will now also derive the Duistermaat-Heckman localisation formula. Let us consider a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
. For completeness, this is an even-dimensional manifold endowed with a
closed
non-degenerate 2-form
\omega
, called the symplectic form
[
2
]
.

Let us now consider a Hamiltonian
H
on this symplectic manifold with
V
its associated Hamiltonian vector field. That is,
V
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}H

\displaystyle=\iota_{V}\omega

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}_{V}(H+\omega)

\displaystyle=0

(1.69)

since
H
is a 0-form and
\omega
is closed. We can apply the Atiyah-Bott-Berline-Vergne localisation formula —assuming that
V
is also a Killing vector to some underlying metric— to solve oscillatory integrals of the form

Z_{H}(t)=\int_{M}\frac{\omega^{\ell}}{\ell!}e^{iHt}=\frac{1}{(it)^{\ell}}\int_{M}e^{it(H+\omega)}.

(1.70)

Indeed, applying the Atiyah-Bott-Berline-Vergne localisation formula we find the following celebrated result
[
2
]
:

Duistermaat-Heckman formula

Z_{H}(t)=\int_{M}\frac{\omega^{\ell}}{\ell!}e^{iHt}=\bigg(\frac{2\pi i}{t}\bigg)^{\ell}\sum_{x_{k}:{\mathrm{d}}H(x_{k})=0}\frac{e^{itH(x_{k})}}{\operatorname{Pf}L_{V}(x_{k})}

(1.71)

Illustrating example

Let us now go back to the example given in equation
1.35
of a case where the stationary phase approximation is exact. That is, the integral

Z(t)=\int_{S^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\varphi{\mathrm{d}}\theta~\sin\theta e^{it\cos\theta}.

(1.72)

In this case the U(1) action is generated by the Killing vector
{\partial}_{\varphi}
, which we note is also a symmetry of the integrand. We further note that this partition function can be rewritten as

Z(t)=\int_{S^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\varphi{\mathrm{d}}\cos\theta~e^{it\cos\theta}=\frac{1}{it}\int_{S^{2}}e^{it(\cos\theta+{\mathrm{d}}\varphi{\mathrm{d}}\cos\theta)}.

(1.73)

This lends the interesting interpretation of a Hamiltonian
H=\cos\theta
with corresponding symplectic form
\omega={\mathrm{d}}\varphi{\mathrm{d}}\cos\theta
with the Killing vector
{\partial}_{\varphi}
generating the Hamiltonian flow. And indeed, this interpretation is correct since

\iota_{{\partial}_{\varphi}}\omega=\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\mathrm{d}}\varphi}\big({\mathrm{d}}\varphi{\mathrm{d}}\cos\theta\big)={\mathrm{d}}\cos\theta={\mathrm{d}}H.

(1.74)

As such, application of the Duistermaat-Heckman localisation formula leads to

Z(t)=\frac{2\pi i}{t}\big(-e^{it}+e^{-it}\big).

(1.75)

in agreement with equation
1.35

[
2
]
.

1.4
Supersymmetric Localisation

1.4.1
Analogy between Equivariant Localisation and Supersymmetric Localisation

Now we ready to move on to path integrals. Again, we will for the most part follow Cremonesi’s lecture notes
[
2
]
in this section. In our context, we will assume that these path integrals are defined on compact, Riemannian manifolds. The reason for this is that we’d like to avoid infrared divergences for the path integral to be well-defined. Another more concrete reason is that we want the analogue of
-|V|^{2}
to have a definite signature in the upcoming field space generalisations of the localisation argument. In our context we will denote the field content collectively by
\Phi
, with
\Phi|_{\text{bos}}
denoting the collective bosonic field content and
\Phi|_{\text{fer}}
denoting the collective fermionic field content
[
2
]
.

We start off by noticing that the previous approach was a toy model for what we’re about to do now. In our previous example, even and odd forms were to be understood as corresponding to respectively bosonic and fermionic fields, with integration over a manifold being the toy version of integration over field space:

\displaystyle\alpha_{\text{even}}/\alpha_{\text{odd}}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

bosons/fermions

(1.76)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\alpha

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\int{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}e^{-S[\Phi]}\bigg.

(1.77)

Furthermore, the
V
-equivariant exterior derivative is to be understood as a supersymmetry transformation. Indeed, we recall that

({\mathrm{d}}_{V}\alpha)_{p}={\mathrm{d}}\alpha_{p-1}+\iota_{V}\alpha_{p+1}

(1.78)

where if
p
even/odd one has
p\pm 1
odd/even, and likewise for supersymmetry transformations one typically has the form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}(\text{boson})

\displaystyle=\text{fermion}

(1.79)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}(\text{fermion})

\displaystyle={\partial}(\text{boson})+\text{auxiliary}

(1.80)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}(\text{auxiliary})

\displaystyle={\partial}(\text{fermion})

(1.81)

Also the algebra shows a strong analogy. Indeed, we have

{\mathrm{d}}_{V}^{2}=-{\mathcal{L}}_{V}

(1.82)

and likewise

{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}={\mathcal{B}}=\text{diffeo}+\text{gauge}+\text{field equations}+\dots

(1.83)

where
{\mathcal{B}}
stands for a bosonic operator. The objects we were integrating over previous were
V
-equivariantly closed polyforms
\alpha
:
{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\alpha=0
. It is clear that its analogues in supersymmetric field theory will be operators which are
{\mathcal{Q}}
-closed:
{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}=0
, which we will refer to as BPS-operators. Similarly, we define
{\mathcal{Q}}
-exact operators as being of the form
{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{O}}
. In summary, we have the following analogies
[
2
]
:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}_{V}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}\Big.

(1.84)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}_{V}^{2}=-{\mathcal{L}}_{V}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}={\mathcal{B}}\Big.

(1.85)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\alpha

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}=0\Big.

(1.86)

\displaystyle\alpha={\mathrm{d}}_{V}\beta

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}\Big.

(1.87)

1.4.2
{\mathcal{Q}}
-cohomology and Path Integrals

We now move on to describe how cohomology works on field space and what its implications are for path integrals. We recall that Stokes’ theorem on manifolds is given by

\int_{M}{\mathrm{d}}\alpha=\int_{{\partial}M}\alpha.

(1.88)

One could now wonder how this generalises to field space. On field space, the analogues of derivatives
{\partial}_{\mu}
will simply be functional derivatives
\delta/\delta\Phi(x)
, where indices are replaced by points in spacetime and the Kronecker
\delta
-symbol between various indices by the Dirac
\delta
-distribution. However, a generalisation which won’t be straightforward is the field space analogue of a boundary. In our setting we will assume that a boundary is associated to fields which decay fast enough as they go to (a possible) infinity. In this case the field space analogue of Stokes’ theorem will be given by

\int{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~\frac{\delta}{\delta\Phi}\Big\{{\mathcal{O}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]}\Big\}=0.

(1.89)

To relate this result to its
V
-equivariant analogue, we further note that the supersymmetry operator
{\mathcal{Q}}
can be written as

{\mathcal{Q}}=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{d}x~\bigg[({\text{fer}})\frac{\delta}{\delta({\text{bos}})}+\Big({\partial}({\text{bos}})+{\text{aux}}\Big)\frac{\delta}{\delta({\text{fer}})}+{\partial}({\text{fer}})\frac{\delta}{\delta({\text{aux}})}\bigg]

(1.90)

As such, we arrive at the analogy

\displaystyle\int_{M}{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\alpha=0

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\int{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{Q}}\Big\{{\mathcal{O}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]}\Big\}=0

(1.91)

It should be noted that this analogue is equivalent to the invariance of the path integral measure with respect to supersymmetry. That is, throughout the rest of the thesis we will assume this is the case but generally this doesn’t have to be the case. Now, let’s suppose that we consider a supersymmetric theory, that is a theory with a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-closed action:

{\mathcal{Q}}S[\Phi]=0.

(1.92)

We consider the expectation value of various operators now. We recall that the expectation value is given by

\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}\right\rangle=\int{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]}.

(1.93)

Interestingly, as it turns out, this expectation value will only depend on the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-cohomology class of the operator. Indeed, we find that

\left\langle{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{O}}\right\rangle=\int{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{O}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]}=\int{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{Q}}\Big\{{\mathcal{O}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]}\Big\}=0

(1.94)

where the second step follows from the fact that the supersymmetry variation
{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfies the Leibniz rule and that the action is supersymmetric. We thus conclude

{\mathcal{Q}}
-cohomology

\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}+{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{O}}\right\rangle

(1.95)

In supersymmetric theories, the expectation value of a BPS operator is determined by its
{\mathcal{Q}}
-cohomology class.

Note that nowhere we required the operator inserted to be a BPS operator for the above identity to hold. However, this is necessary firstly for cohomology to be well defined, and this will also become relevant for the localisation arguments
[
2
]
.

1.4.3
Localisation Arguments for Supersymmetric Path Integrals

In this section we outline localisation arguments for supersymmetric path integrals. These will be analogous to the arguments made in previous section for equivariant localisation.

First localisation argument

This argument was due to Witten
[
12
]
. Suppose we have a fermionic symmetry group
G
which acts on field space
{\mathscr{F}}
, which has fixed points
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. It then follows that the symmetry group acts freely on
{\mathscr{F}}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, and hence we can ‘integrate along the fibers’ generated by the fermionic symmetry group
G
. This yields a path integral

\int_{{\mathscr{F}}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]}=\operatorname{Vol}(G)\int_{({\mathscr{F}}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}})/G}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]}.

(1.96)

However, since we’re dealing with a fermionic group, we find that the volume of this group will be of the form

\operatorname{Vol}(G)=\int{\mathrm{d}}\theta\cdot 1=0

(1.97)

for some Grassmann parameter
\theta
. This yields a result

\int_{{\mathscr{F}}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]}=0

(1.98)

hence showing that the path integral localises to the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-supersymmetric field configurations
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}

[
2
]
.

Second localisation argument

The second localisation argument follows in strong analogy to that for equivariant integrals. We note that we can deform the path integral continuously using a fermionic functional
{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]
which we require to be annihilated by the square of the supercharge:

{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]={\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]=0.

(1.99)

and for which we take its supersymmetry transformation to be
positive semi-definite
in its bosonic components:

{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]\Big|_{\text{bos}}\geq 0.

(1.100)

In this case it turns out that the path integral can be continuously deformed as

\begin{split}\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}\right\rangle&=\int_{\mathscr{F}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]}\\
&=\int_{\mathscr{F}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]-t{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]}\end{split}

(1.101)

for any value of
t
, without changing the expetation value, assuming that
{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}
is (as its name suggests) a
BPS operator
. Indeed, we find that

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{\mathscr{F}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]-t{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]}=-\int_{\mathscr{F}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi]{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]-t{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]}\\
=-\int_{\mathscr{F}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{Q}}\Big\{{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi]{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]-t{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]}\Big\}=0

(1.102)

where in the third step we used the Leibniz rule for variations and in the final step we used Stokes’ theorem on field space. Since the bosonic part of
{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}
is positive semi-definite, we are free to take the limit
t\to\infty
. Taking this limit we find that the parts for which the bosonic part of the localising action

S_{\text{loc}}[\Phi]={\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]

(1.103)

is strictly positive get exponentially supressed in the limit
t\to\infty
. Taking this limit we find that the path integral localises to

{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\Big\{\Phi\in{\mathscr{F}}~\Big|~S_{\text{loc}}[\Phi]\big|_{\text{bos}}=0,~~\Phi\big|_{\text{fer}}=0\Big\}.

(1.104)

The reason that we can localise around zero configurations of the fermionic component fields
\Phi|_{\text{fer}}
that due to their anticommutig nature we can expand them around any given point, since we don’t have to worry about things such as divergence for series
[
2
]
.

An example

A standard choice for localising actions is for example

S_{\text{loc}}[\Phi]\overset{\text{e.g.}}{=}{\mathcal{Q}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{d}x\sum_{\psi\in\Phi|_{\text{fer}}}({\mathcal{Q}}\psi)^{\dagger}\psi

(1.105)

This localising term has a bosonic part

S_{\text{loc}}[\Phi]\Big|_{\text{bos}}=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{d}x\sum_{\psi\in\Phi|_{\text{fer}}}|{\mathcal{Q}}\psi|^{2}

(1.106)

which is indeed positive semi-definite. We thus find that for this example the path integral localises to

{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\big\{\Phi\in{\mathscr{F}}~\big|~\forall\psi\in\Phi|_{\text{fer}}:{\mathcal{Q}}\psi=0,~\psi=0\big\}

(1.107)

consisting of so-called BPS configurations
[
2
]
.

Working out the localisation

Let us now work out how this localisation gives us results for supersymmetric path integrals. We expand the field content around the localisation locus
\Phi_{0}\in{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}
as

\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}\delta\Phi.

(1.108)

The localising action is then expanded as

tS_{\text{loc}}[\Phi]=tS_{\text{loc}}[\Phi_{0}]+\sqrt{t}\int\frac{\delta S_{\text{loc}}}{\delta\Phi}[\Phi_{0}]\delta\Phi+\frac{1}{2}\iint\frac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{loc}}}{\delta\Phi^{2}}[\Phi_{0}](\delta\Phi)^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(t^{-1/2})\\
=\frac{1}{2}\iint\frac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{loc}}}{\delta\Phi^{2}}[\Phi_{0}](\delta\Phi)^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(t^{-1/2})\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}\iint\frac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{loc}}}{\delta\Phi^{2}}[\Phi_{0}](\delta\Phi)^{2}

(1.109)

The first in the expansion vanishes due to the fact the defining quality of the localisation locus is
S_{\text{loc}}[\Phi_{0}]=0
. The second term vanishes due to the fact that since the localising term is positive definite its first order contributions around a local extremum vanish. This leaves us after taking the limit with only the second order contributions to the path integral. As for the action of the theory as well as the BPS operator, we find that they are simply expanded as

\displaystyle S[\Phi]

\displaystyle=S[\Phi_{0}]

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{O}}(t^{-1/2})\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}0

(1.110)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi]

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi_{0}]

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{O}}(t^{-1/2})\xrightarrow{t\to\infty}0

(1.111)

The aforementioned results allow us to solve the path integral as

\begin{split}\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}\right\rangle&=\lim_{t\to\infty}\int_{\mathscr{F}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi]e^{-S[\Phi]-tS_{\text{loc}}[\Phi]}\\
&=\int_{N{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi_{0}{\mathcal{D}}\delta\Phi~{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi_{0}]\exp\bigg\{-S[\Phi_{0}]-\frac{1}{2}\iint\frac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{loc}}}{\delta\Phi^{2}}[\Phi_{0}](\delta\Phi)^{2}\bigg\}\\
&=\int_{{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi_{0}~\frac{{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi_{0}]}{\operatorname{Ber}\left[\frac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{loc}}}{\delta\Phi^{2}}[\Phi_{0}]\right]}e^{-S[\Phi_{0}]}.\end{split}

(1.112)

where
\operatorname{Ber}
stands for the functional Berezinian, which we took of the kinetic operator of the localising action at the localisation locus.
N{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}
stands for the normal bundle of the localisation locus, which has field space coordinates
([\Phi_{0}],[\delta\Phi])
. In conclusion, we find that the localisation of the supersymmetric path integral is given by

Supersymmetic localisation formula

\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}\right\rangle=\int_{{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}}{\mathcal{D}}\Phi_{0}~\frac{{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}[\Phi_{0}]}{\operatorname{Ber}\left[\frac{\delta^{2}S_{\text{loc}}}{\delta\Phi^{2}}[\Phi_{0}]\right]}e^{-S[\Phi_{0}]}

(1.113)

the localisation formula for a BPS operator
{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}
, with the localising action given by

S_{\text{loc}}[\Phi]={\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]

(1.114)

for some fermionic functional
{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]
satisfying

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}{\mathcal{F}}[\Phi]

\displaystyle=0,

(1.115)

and the localisation locus given by

{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\Big\{\Phi_{0}\in{\mathscr{F}}~\Big|~S_{\text{loc}}[\Phi_{0}]\big|_{\text{bos}}=0,~~\Phi_{0}\big|_{\text{fer}}=0\Big\}

(1.116)

which may itself be another field space, or may be a finite-dimensional manifold or even a discrete set of points.

In conclusion, we have given a method which allows one to systematically reduce the dimension of a path integral. This in turn would allow one to do non-perturbative computations
[
2
]
.

It should be noted that this is not the only way one can localise. There are more ways of continuously deforming path integrand in such a way that the corresponding expectation value of a BPS operator is left unchanged. One such example will be treated next chapter in the Witten index. In the case of the Witten index, rather than adding a localising term which we scale to infinity, we will rescale certain parameters of the theory, resulting in an eventual localisation.

Chapter 2
The Witten Index

2.1
Introduction

In this chapter, we discuss the Witten index of a one-dimensional supersymmetric quantum mechanical system. This serves as a ‘warm up’ before we move on to the more challenging cases of supersymmetric quantum field theories.

We start off by introducing the Witten index through the Hamiltonian formalism. We discuss a Hilbert space expression for this object which involves periodic Euclidean time and also show the independence of the Witten index on this Euclidean time periodicity. We then move on to describe the Witten index through a Euclidean action formalism, in the language of path integrals. It’s in this context that we can connect this to localisation methods. This action can then also be described in superspace, allowing one to efficiently derive all desired properties of the Witten index.
These notes start off by formulating a topic of discussion in David Tong’s notes on supersymmetric quantum mechanics
[
13
]
but will quickly start diverging from these notes.
In fact, it is believed that we made a new contribution to this, because we described a procedure through the path intergal formalism which has previously only been described through the Hilbert space formalism.

2.2
Witten Index in the Hilbert Space Formalism

2.2.1
On-Shell Supersymmetric Point Particle

We start off by giving the on-shell description of the supersymmetric point particle. That is, we start off by considering the action of the supersymmetric point particle where potential auxiliary fields are taken to be on-shell. We take this description because
it’s this action that allows us to go to the Hilbert space formalism. We also immediately go to Euclidean time, since that’s all we’ll be making use of. This being said, the on-shell Euclidean action for
\beta
-periodic Euclidean time
\tau
is given by

S_{E}^{\text{on}}=\underset{S^{1}(\beta)}{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}}\left[\frac{1}{2}{\dot{x}}^{2}+{\psi^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}}+\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}(x)^{2}-h^{\prime\prime}(x){\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\right].

(2.1)

Let us take a look at the content of this action.
x(\tau)
is taken to be the position of the point particle in one-dimensional space.
\psi(\tau)
and
{\psi^{\dagger}}(\tau)
describe the
spin of this particle and are Grassmann-valued fields on the Euclidean time circle.
h(x)
is a real-valued Morse function of
x
,
known as the superpotential of the system. By Morse, we mean that the stationary points of this function are taken to be isolated.
S^{1}(\beta)
denotes the Euclidean time circle of period
\beta
over which we’re integrating. Finally, a dot denotes a time derivative with respect to Euclidean time.

This action is supersymmetric, with its supersymmetries given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\psi\frac{\delta}{\delta x}+(-{\dot{x}}+h^{\prime})\frac{\delta}{\delta{\psi^{\dagger}}}\right],

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}

\displaystyle={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[-{\psi^{\dagger}}\frac{\delta}{\delta x}+({\dot{x}}+h^{\prime})\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi}\right].

(2.2)

Indeed, a straightforward computation yields

{\mathcal{Q}}S_{E}^{\text{on}}={\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}S_{E}^{\text{on}}=0.

(2.3)

We can finally also introduce a ‘Eucidean Hamiltonian’

H_{E}=-L_{E}+\frac{{\partial}L_{E}}{{\partial}{\dot{x}}}{\dot{x}}+\frac{{\partial}L_{E}}{{\partial}{\dot{\psi}}}{\dot{\psi}}=\frac{1}{2}{\dot{x}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime 2}+h^{\prime\prime}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi=-H.

(2.4)

In the final step, it’s important to remember that
({\mathrm{d}}x/{\mathrm{d}}\tau)^{2}=-({\mathrm{d}}x/{\mathrm{d}}t)^{2}

[
13
]
.

2.2.2
Hilbert Space Formalism

Before moving on to the Witten index, we make the necessary commentary on the Hilbert space of this system. The system in consideration carries bosonic as well as fermionic degrees of freedom. Accordingly, its Hilbert space will be that of a one-dimensional point particle with a single spin, given by

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{F}\cong L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}.

(2.5)

In accordance with general properties of supersymmetry generators, the Euclidean Hamiltonian will be given by

H_{E}=-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{E},Q_{E}{{}^{\dagger}}\}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}}-h^{\prime 2}\right)\otimes\mathbbm{1}+\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime\prime}\otimes\sigma^{3}

(2.6)

where
\sigma^{3}=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)
the third Pauli matrix and
Q_{E}
,
Q_{E}{{}^{\dagger}}
the supersymmetry operators given by

\displaystyle Q_{E}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}x}+h^{\prime}\right)\otimes\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle Q_{E}{{}^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}x}+h^{\prime}\right)\otimes\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

(2.7)

They also satisfy the nilpotency conditions

(Q_{E})^{2}=(Q_{E}{{}^{\dagger}})^{2}=0.

(2.8)

These algebraic properties of the Hamiltonian and supersymmetry oparators have a number of consequences. Firstly, we note that the Euclidean energy is always negative, since for any state
{\left|\psi\right\rangle}

{\left\langle\psi\right|}H_{E}{\left|\psi\right\rangle}=-\frac{1}{2}|Q_{E}{\left|\psi\right\rangle}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|Q_{E}{{}^{\dagger}}{\left|\psi\right\rangle}|^{2}\leq 0

(2.9)

where one assumes the Hilbert space inner product to be positive definite, which is obviously the case for our system in consideration.
Secondly, we note that for a supersymmetric system positive energy states are 1:1. This can be seen as follows: Recall firstly that the fermion number operator is given by

F=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

(2.10)

highlighting that in the spin Hilbert space the components refer to respectively bosonic and fermionic states. We now consider the subspace of negative Euclidean energy states, spanned by

\displaystyle{\left|E\right\rangle},

s.t.

\displaystyle H_{E}{\left|E\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=-E{\left|E\right\rangle},

\displaystyle E>0.

(2.11)

Note however that negative Euclidean energy corresponds to positive energy, see equation
2.4
. This allows us now to define operators
c
and
c^{\dagger}
on this space by linearly extending

\displaystyle c{\left|E\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2E}}Q_{E}{\left|E\right\rangle},

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}{\left|E\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2E}}Q_{E}{{}^{\dagger}}{\left|E\right\rangle}.

(2.12)

These operators satisfy the algebra

\displaystyle\{c,c^{\dagger}\}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1},

\displaystyle c^{2}

\displaystyle=(c^{\dagger})^{2}=0

(2.13)

being the commutation relations characterizing a fermionic harmonic oscillator. This means that negative Euclidean energy states come in pairs which represent this algebra. We furthermore note that these operators preserve energy and change fermion number, due to the algebraic relations

\displaystyle[c,H_{E}]

\displaystyle=[c^{\dagger},H_{E}]=0,

\displaystyle[F,c]

\displaystyle=-c,

\displaystyle[F,c^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=+c^{\dagger}.

(2.14)

This confirms that bosonic and fermionic states negative Euclidean energy states come 1:1 with the same energy.
However, this needn’t be the case for zero energy states
[
13
]
.

2.2.3
Hilbert Space Witten Index Expression

We now introduce the Witten index as expressed in the Hilbert space formalism. In this formalism, the Witten index is given by

{\mathcal{I}}=\operatorname{Tr}(-1)^{F}e^{-\beta H}=\operatorname{Tr}(-1)^{F}e^{\beta H_{E}}.

(2.15)

where we assume a discrete energy spectrum. Let us think about how to compute this: Since bosonic and fermionic same positive energy states are
1:1
, their contributions will cancel in the above trace, due to the
(-1)^{F}
factor. We hence find that the Witten index in the Hilbert space formalism is given by

{\mathcal{I}}=\operatorname{Tr}(-1)^{F}e^{-\beta H}=\dim\ker H|_{\text{bos}}-\dim\ker H|_{\text{fer}}.

(2.16)

This can be regarded as a statement about the kernel of a differential operator —the Hamiltonian operator— on a manifold. This will later link this result to more general index theorems.
We furthermore note that the Witten index is independent of our choice of
\beta
. Indeed, differentiating with respect to
\beta
we find that

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\beta}=\operatorname{Tr}(-1)^{F}H_{E}e^{H_{E}}=0.

(2.17)

The vanishing in the final step is due to the fact that positive energy fermion-boson doublets again cancel, as well as the fact that for this trace zero energy states don’t contribute altogether.
From this we conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}(-1)^{F}e^{-\beta H}=\dim\ker H|_{\text{bos}}-\dim\ker H|_{\text{fer}},

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\beta}

\displaystyle=0

(2.18)

the Hilbert space expression and
\beta
-independence of the Witten index, derived from the Hilbert space formalism
[
13
]
.

2.3
The Witten index and the Path Integral

In this section, we will discuss the Witten index through the path integral formalism. We will start off by obtaining a path integral expression for the Witten index from its Hilbert space expression. Then, we will discuss the off-shell expression for the Witten index. To do so, we will start off by reintroducing a missing auxiliary field which in the on-shell expression is taken to be on-shell. We will then further generalize our description of the Witten index to superspace. After this, we will discuss symmetries of the Witten index. To do this, we will start off by motivating the systematics through geometrical arguments generalized to function spaces. We then combine this with our superspace description of the system to obtain manifest symmetries of the Witten index. When this is finished we will use these symmetries to show the
\beta
-independence of the Witten index through the path integral formalism, as well as obtain a simple expression for the Witten index also through the path integral formalism.

2.3.1
Path Integral Expression of the Witten Index

We start off this section by deriving a path integral expression of the Witten index. To do so, we note that the trace of some operator
\mathcal{O}
acting on our Hilbert space is given by

\begin{split}\operatorname{Tr}\mathcal{O}&=\int{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\psi e^{-{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi}~{\left\langle-\psi,x\right|}\mathcal{O}{\left|\psi,x\right\rangle}\\
&=\int{\mathrm{d}}x~\bigg[{\left\langle 0,x\right|}{\mathcal{O}}{\left|0,x\right\rangle}+{\left\langle 1,x\right|}{\mathcal{O}}{\left|1,x\right\rangle}\bigg]\end{split}

(2.19)

where we defined the states

\displaystyle{\left|0,x\right\rangle}

\displaystyle:={\left|x\right\rangle}\otimes{\left|0\right\rangle}

\displaystyle{\left|\psi,x\right\rangle}

\displaystyle:={\left|x\right\rangle}\otimes{\left|0\right\rangle}+{\left|x\right\rangle}\otimes{\left|1\right\rangle}\psi,

(2.20)

\displaystyle{\left|1,x\right\rangle}

\displaystyle:={\left|x\right\rangle}\otimes{\left|1\right\rangle}

\displaystyle{\left\langle\psi,x\right|}

\displaystyle:={\left\langle x\right|}\otimes{\left\langle 0\right|}+{\left\langle x\right|}\otimes{\psi^{\dagger}}{\left\langle 1\right|}

(2.21)

where
{\left|0\right\rangle}
,
{\left|1\right\rangle}
denote the bra-ket basis of the fermionic Hilbert space. The exponential factor in this trace might seem a little confusing but is in fact completely justified. The reason for this being that only terms in the integrand proportional to
{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi
survive Berezinian integration.

From this it follows that

(-1)^{F}{\left|\psi,x\right\rangle}={\left|x\right\rangle}\otimes\Big((-1)^{0}{\left|0\right\rangle}+(-1)^{1}{\left|1\right\rangle}\psi\Big)={\left|-\psi,x\right\rangle}.

(2.22)

Using this we now find that

\begin{split}{\mathcal{I}}=\operatorname{Tr}(-1)^{F}e^{-\beta H}&=\int{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\psi e^{-{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi}~{\left\langle-\psi,x\right|}(-1)^{F}e^{-\beta H}{\left|\psi,x\right\rangle}\\
&=\int{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\psi e^{-{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi}~{\left\langle\psi,x\right|}e^{-\beta H}{\left|\psi,x\right\rangle}.\end{split}

(2.23)

Furthermore noting the path integral identity

{\left\langle\psi,x\right|}e^{-\beta H}{\left|\eta,y\right\rangle}=\overset{x(\beta)=y,~\psi(\beta)=\eta,~\text{h.c.}}{\underset{x(0)=x,~\psi(0)=\psi,~\text{h.c.}}{\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi}}~e^{-S_{E}^{\text{on}}}

(2.24)

we conclude that

{\mathcal{I}}=\operatorname{Tr}(-1)^{F}e^{-\beta H}=\int\underbrace{{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi}_{\text{$\beta$-periodic}}~e^{-S^{\text{on}}}

(2.25)

the on-shell path integral expression of the Witten index. We dropped the subscript
E
in the Euclidean action and will keep doing so from now on.

Note that the additional factor
e^{-{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi}
in equation
2.23
gets absorbed into the path integral acting as an object which glues the two ends of the Euclidean time
[0,\beta]
into a circle
S^{1}(\beta)
of circumference
\beta

[
14
]
.

2.3.2
The Off-Shell Action

So far we have only considered the on-shell action
S^{\text{on}}
of the supersymmetric point particle.
This can be seen from the fact that while the bosonic and fermionic states seem to show matching behaviour, the field content doesn’t. Namely, there is one real bosonic degree of freedom but two real (i.e. one complex) fermionic degrees of freedom.
However, to properly study the Witten index from the path integral formalism we will find it to be necessary to include the remaining the off-shell degree of freedom in our discussion.
From this point on we diverge completely from Tong’s notes
[
13
]
, as the off-shell description isn’t mentioned in Tong’s notes whatsoever.

Reintroducing the auxiliary field

To match the degrees of freedom of the field content, we need one more bosonic degree of freedom, matching the bosonic and fermionic field content degrees of freedom two-to-two. This remaining degree of freedom will be a real bosonic auxiliary field
F(\tau)
, which is taken to be non-dynamical. We now want to find an action such that upon eliminating this non-dynamical field we obtain the on-shell action again. This action is given by

S^{\text{off}}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\frac{1}{2}{\dot{x}}^{2}+{\psi^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}}+\frac{1}{2}F^{2}+ih^{\prime}F-h^{\prime\prime}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\right]

(2.26)

the (Euclidean) off-shell action.

We note that this action can be regarded as a WZ model with interactions up to arbitrary order. Let us verify that on-shell this action indeed gives the us the on-shell action. The equation of motion of the auxiliary field is

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta S^{\text{off}}}{\delta F}=F+ih^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=-ih^{\prime}.

(2.27)

Substitution in the off-shell action yields

S^{\text{off}}[x,-ih^{\prime},\psi,{\psi^{\dagger}}]={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\frac{1}{2}{\dot{x}}^{2}+{\psi^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}}+\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime 2}-h^{\prime\prime}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\right]=S^{\text{on}}[x,\psi,{\psi^{\dagger}}]

(2.28)

giving us the on-shell action. This is all good and well, but this doesn’t guarantee that the physics will we the same for this system or that its supersymmetries are preserved. We hence have two goals: Show the existence of supersymmetries of the off-shell action and relate them to the on-shell action, as well as show that introducing these auxiliary fields doesn’t change the Witten index.

To tackle the first goal, we let us be inspired by the typical form of SUSY variations, being

\displaystyle\delta(\text{boson})

\displaystyle=\text{fermion},

(2.29)

\displaystyle\delta(\text{fermion})

\displaystyle=\partial(\text{boson})+\text{auxiliary},

\displaystyle\delta(\text{auxiliary})

\displaystyle=\partial(\text{fermion}).

Following this form we consider

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\psi\frac{\delta}{\delta x}+(-{\dot{x}}+iF)\frac{\delta}{\delta{\psi^{\dagger}}}-i{\dot{\psi}}\frac{\delta}{\delta F}\right]

(2.30)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}

\displaystyle={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[-{\psi^{\dagger}}\frac{\delta}{\delta x}+({\dot{x}}+iF)\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi}-i{\dot{\psi}{{}^{\dagger}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta F}\right]

(2.31)

the off-shell supersymmetry variations. We typically supress the superscript ‘off’ unless ambiguities arise.

Indeed, we note that

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\text{off}}|_{F=-ih^{\prime}}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\psi\frac{\delta}{\delta x}+(-{\dot{x}}+h^{\prime})\frac{\delta}{\delta{\psi^{\dagger}}}\right]={\mathcal{Q}}^{\text{on}}

(2.32)

and similarly
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\text{off}\dagger}|_{F=-ih^{\prime}}={\mathcal{Q}}^{\text{on}\dagger}
. Furthermore, we find that it also indeed is a symmetry of the off-shell action since

{\mathcal{Q}}S^{\text{off}}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\bigg[\psi(-\ddot{x}+ih^{\prime\prime}F)+(-{\dot{x}}+iF)({\dot{\psi}}-h^{\prime\prime}\psi)-i{\dot{\psi}}(F+ih^{\prime})\bigg]\\
={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\bigg[(-\ddot{x}\psi-{\dot{x}}{\dot{\psi}})+({\dot{x}}h^{\prime\prime}\psi+h^{\prime}{\dot{\psi}})\bigg]=0

(2.33)

and similarly
{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}S^{\text{off}}=0
.

Now we move on to our second goal of relating it to the on-shell Witten index. We claim that the on-shell Witten index agrees with the off-shell expression

{\mathcal{I}}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~e^{-S^{\text{off}}}.

(2.34)

This is indeed the case and can be shown by completing the square in the off-shell action as

S^{\text{off}}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\frac{1}{2}{\dot{x}}^{2}+{\psi^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}}+\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime 2}-h^{\prime\prime}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi+\frac{1}{2}(F+ih^{\prime})^{2}\right]=S^{\text{on}}+{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\frac{1}{2}(F+ih^{\prime})^{2}.

(2.35)

This hence yields

\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~e^{-S^{\text{off}}}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~\exp\left\{-S^{\text{on}}-\frac{1}{2}{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}(F+ih^{\prime})^{2}\right\}\\
=\operatorname{Det}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbbm{1}\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~e^{-S^{\text{on}}}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~e^{-S^{\text{on}}}.

(2.36)

This confirms that

{\mathcal{I}}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~e^{-S^{\text{off}}}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~e^{-S^{\text{on}}}

(2.37)

the off-shell and the on-shell expressions of the Witten index agree.

Going to superspace

This won’t fully conclude us extending the way we describe this system. We won’t introduce any more new fields, rather we will reformulate our theory in the superspace formalism, as this will make a lot of properties which are important later on for obtaining the right results become manifest.
We will promote the Euclidean time circle to a superspace. That is, instead of just having a coordinate
\tau
, superspace will have coordinates
(\tau,\theta,{\theta^{\dagger}})
with the latter two being Grassmann variables. In accordance with the periodicity of the fermionic fields we take these coordinates to be periodic instead of antiperiodic on the Euclidean time circle.
We will now interpret the fields involved on this circle to correspond to a scalar superfield
X
. That is, we take our superfield to be of the form

X(\tau,\theta,{\theta^{\dagger}})=x(\tau)+\theta{\psi^{\dagger}}(\tau)+{\theta^{\dagger}}\psi(\tau)+i\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}F(\tau).

(2.38)

Accordingly, we now introduce a ‘superspace Lie derivative’ representation of the supersymmetries
{\mathcal{Q}}
,
{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}
. We will denote these representations by
Q
,
{Q^{\dagger}}
respectively. These representations have to satisfy

\displaystyle QX

\displaystyle=Qx-\theta{\mathcal{Q}}{\psi^{\dagger}}-{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}}\psi+i\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}}F

\displaystyle{Q^{\dagger}}X

\displaystyle={\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}x-\theta{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}{\psi^{\dagger}}-{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}\psi+i\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}F

(2.39)

\displaystyle=\psi-\theta(-{\dot{x}}+iF)+i\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}(-i{\dot{\psi}})

\displaystyle=-{\psi^{\dagger}}-{\theta^{\dagger}}({\dot{x}}+iF)+i\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}(-i{\dot{\psi}{{}^{\dagger}}})

We can satisfy these conditions, namely through choosing

Superspace supersymmetry representation

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\theta^{\dagger}}}+\theta\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau},

\displaystyle{Q^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}-{\theta^{\dagger}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}

(2.40)

One of the advantages of using this representation is that it becomes much easier to study general algebraic properties of supersymmetry. For example, we find that

\{Q,{Q^{\dagger}}\}=-\{\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\theta^{\dagger}}},\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}\}-\{\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\theta^{\dagger}}},{\theta^{\dagger}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\}-\{\theta\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau},\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}\}-\{\theta\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau},{\theta^{\dagger}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\}=-2\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}

(2.41)

as well as
Q^{2}=({Q^{\dagger}})^{2}=0
which is in alignment with general properties of supersymmetric theories.

We now move on to constructing the Lagrangian in superspace. This Lagrangian
{\mathbb{L}}
has to be chosen such that the action is given by

S={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta~{\mathbb{L}}(X,DX,{D^{\dagger}}X)

(2.42)

where we demand that the superspace Lagrangian transforms as a density under supersymmetry transformations. The derivatives
D
and
{D^{\dagger}}
are covariant spinor derivatives. They’re covariant as in they are required to anticommute with the supersymmetries
Q
and
{Q^{\dagger}}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\{Q,D\}=\{{Q^{\dagger}},D\}=\{Q,D^{\dagger}\}=\{{Q^{\dagger}},D^{\dagger}\}=0.

(2.43)

These relations are satisfied if we choose

Covariant spinor derivatives

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\theta^{\dagger}}}-\theta\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau},

\displaystyle{D^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}+{\theta^{\dagger}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}

(2.44)

We note that these also satisfy

\displaystyle\{D,{D^{\dagger}}\}

\displaystyle=2\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau},

\displaystyle D^{2}

\displaystyle=({D^{\dagger}})^{2}=0.

(2.45)

These now allow us to construct the free part of the action. We compute

\displaystyle DX

\displaystyle=\psi-\theta({\dot{x}}+iF)-\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}},

\displaystyle{D^{\dagger}}X

\displaystyle=-{\psi^{\dagger}}+{\theta^{\dagger}}({\dot{x}}-iF)-\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}{{}^{\dagger}}}.

(2.46)

We hence find that

{D^{\dagger}}XDX=-{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi-\theta{\psi^{\dagger}}({\dot{x}}+iF)+{\theta^{\dagger}}\psi({\dot{x}}-iF)+\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}({\dot{x}}^{2}+F^{2}+{\psi^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}}-{\dot{\psi}{{}^{\dagger}}}\psi)

(2.47)

of which the highest Grassmann order term is proportional to the free sector of the off-shell action
S^{\text{off}}
given in equation
2.26
. As for the interaction part of the action, doing some combinatorics we note that

\begin{split}X^{n}&=x^{n}+nx^{n-1}(\theta{\psi^{\dagger}}+{\theta^{\dagger}}\psi)+\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}\Big(inx^{n-1}F-n(n-1)x^{n-2}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\Big)\\
&=\left[1+(\theta{\psi^{\dagger}}+{\theta^{\dagger}}\psi)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}x}+\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}\left(iF\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}x}-{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}}\right)\right]x^{n}.\end{split}

(2.48)

Hence, formally Taylor expanding the superpotential
h
in powers of the superfields
X
instead of
x
we find that

\begin{split}h(X)&\equiv\exp\left(X\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}x}\right)h(0)\\
&=\left[1+(\theta{\psi^{\dagger}}+{\theta^{\dagger}}\psi)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}x}+\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}\left(iF\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}x}-{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}}\right)\right]\exp\left(x\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}x}\right)h(0)\\
&=h(x)+h^{\prime}(x)(\theta{\psi^{\dagger}}+{\theta^{\dagger}}\psi)+\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}\Big(ih^{\prime}(x)F-h^{\prime\prime}(x){\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\Big)\end{split}

(2.49)

of which the highest Grassmann order term is the interaction part of the action. From this we conclude that

Superspace Lagrangian

\begin{split}{\mathbb{L}}&=\frac{1}{2}{D^{\dagger}}XDX+h(X)\\
&=\!\begin{multlined}\left(-\frac{1}{2}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi+h\right)+\theta{\psi^{\dagger}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}({\dot{x}}+iF)+h^{\prime}\right)+{\theta^{\dagger}}\psi\left(\frac{1}{2}({\dot{x}}-iF)+h^{\prime}\right)\\
+\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Big({\dot{x}}^{2}+{\psi^{\dagger}}\overleftrightarrow{\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}}\psi+F^{2}\Big)+ih^{\prime}F-h^{\prime\prime}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\bigg)\end{multlined}\end{split}

(2.50)

The reason we went ahead and wrote down the full expansion is because this expansion will become important later on.

2.3.3
Symmetries of the Witten Index

Systematics

In this section we will discuss symmetries of the Witten index. What we mean by symmetries is that we consider transformations of the off-shell action and verify whether or not they leave the Witten index unchanged.
