It is here that a connection is made with the previous chapter. Particularly, as explained in Cremonesi’s lecture notes
[
2
]
, the expectation value of a BPS operator depends only on its
Q
-cohomology class. In the current context, this is expressed as

\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{BPS}}+{\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{G}+{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}\mathcal{H}\right\rangle.

(2.51)

The BPS operator in consideration here is the
(-1)^{F}
operator. As such, if the Witten index path integrand is deformed by a
Q
-exact term the Witten index is left unchanged.
The fact that we can freely deform a path integral integrand by
Q
-exact functionals will be used deform the integrand in such a way that the path integral will in some limit only receive contributions from lower dimensional subspaces of the function space, if not finite-dimensional or even discrete contributions
[
2
]
.

\hbar
-rescalings

We will consider three symmetries in this section. The first symmetry we will consider is an

\hbar
-rescaling.
This is a symmetry given by
S^{\text{off}}\to\mu S^{\text{off}}
with
\mu>0

, essentially corresponding to a rescaling
\hbar\to\mu^{-1}\hbar
.
The Witten index then becomes

{\mathcal{I}}(\mu)=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~e^{-\mu S^{\text{off}}}.

(2.52)

This means that under changes of
\mu
the Witten index changes as

\frac{{\partial}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\partial}\mu}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~(-S^{\text{off}})e^{-\mu S^{\text{off}}}.

(2.53)

Note that these rescalings aren’t the same as on-shell

\hbar
-rescalings
, as you can’t integrate out the auxiliary fields without obtaining an additional functional detereminant factor! Following our discussion earlier this section it now suffices to show that
S^{\text{off}}
is
Q
-exact. This can be most easily seen in the superspace formalism. This is because the superspace Lagrangian represents the supersymmetry algebra as a scalar density. Expanding the superspace Lagrangian as

{\mathbb{L}}=\ell+\theta{\mathcal{L}}+{\theta^{\dagger}}{{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}+\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}L

(2.54)

this means that under
Q
and
{Q^{\dagger}}
it transforms as

\displaystyle Q{\mathbb{L}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{Q}}\ell-\theta{\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{L}}-{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}}{{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}+\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}}L+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}(\dots),

(2.55)

\displaystyle{Q^{\dagger}}{\mathbb{L}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}\ell-\theta{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}{\mathcal{L}}-{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}{{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}}+\theta{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}L+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}(\dots).

(2.56)

Further noting that at least for this system we’re allowed to write

{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta(\dots)={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{Q^{\dagger}}Q(\dots)

(2.57)

we find that

S^{\text{off}}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta~{\mathbb{L}}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~{Q^{\dagger}}Q{\mathbb{L}}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}{\mathcal{Q}}\ell={\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}{\mathcal{Q}}\left\{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~\ell\right\}.

(2.58)

Comparing this to equation
2.50
we then find

S^{\text{off}}={\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}{\mathcal{Q}}\left\{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left(-\frac{1}{2}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi+h\right)\right\}.

(2.59)

By a direct computation one could verify that this indeed gives the right result. Using the
Q
-closure of the action as well as the graded Leibniz rule we now find that

\frac{{\partial}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\partial}\mu}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~{\mathcal{Q}{{}^{\dagger}}}{\mathcal{Q}}\left\{-{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left(-\frac{1}{2}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi+h\right)e^{-\mu S^{\text{off}}}\right\}=0

(2.60)

showing the invariance of the Witten index under

\hbar
-rescalings.
We conclude

\hbar
-rescaling symmetry

\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\partial}\mu}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle S^{\text{off}}\to\mu S^{\text{off}}

(2.61)

the Witten index is invariant under

\hbar
-rescalings.

Kinetic and superpotential rescalings

We now consider the other two symmetries. These are kinetic and superpotential rescalings. Denoting
{\mathcal{X}}=(x,\psi,{\psi^{\dagger}},F)
the collective fields this gives a transformation

S^{\text{off}}[{\mathcal{X}}]\to S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}[{\mathcal{X}}]={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~L({\mathcal{X}},\kappa\dot{{\mathcal{X}}})|_{h\to\lambda h}

(2.62)

with
\kappa,\lambda>0
. In
x
-space this gives

S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\frac{1}{2}\kappa^{2}{\dot{x}}^{2}+\kappa{\psi^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}}+\frac{1}{2}F^{2}+i\lambda h^{\prime}F-\lambda h^{\prime\prime}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\right]

(2.63)

with augmented supersymmetries

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\psi\frac{\delta}{\delta x}+(-\kappa{\dot{x}}+iF)\frac{\delta}{\delta{\psi^{\dagger}}}-i\kappa{\dot{\psi}}\frac{\delta}{\delta F}\right],

(2.64)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[-{\psi^{\dagger}}\frac{\delta}{\delta x}+(\kappa{\dot{x}}+iF)\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi}-i\kappa{\dot{\psi}{{}^{\dagger}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta F}\right].

(2.65)

Indeed, we find for example that

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\bigg[\psi(-\kappa^{2}\ddot{x}+i\lambda h^{\prime\prime}F)+(-\kappa{\dot{x}}+iF)(\kappa{\dot{\psi}}-\lambda h^{\prime\prime}\psi)-i\kappa{\dot{\psi}}(F+i\lambda h^{\prime})\bigg]\\
={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\bigg[-\kappa^{2}(\ddot{x}\psi+{\dot{x}}{\dot{\psi}})+\lambda(1-1)ih^{\prime\prime}F\psi+\kappa(1-1)iF{\dot{\psi}}+\kappa\lambda({\dot{x}}h^{\prime\prime}\psi+h^{\prime}{\dot{\psi}})\bigg]=0

(2.66)

and analogously
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}=0
. It hence follows that to show the invariance of the Witten index under these rescalings we have to show that the corresponding deformation of the integrand is exact with respect to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}
or
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}
.

However, let us first out of practical considerations also formulate these transformations in superspace. In superspace we redefine the supersymmetry representation and covariant spinor derivatives as

\displaystyle Q_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\theta^{\dagger}}}+\kappa\theta\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}

\displaystyle D_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\theta^{\dagger}}}-\kappa\theta\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}

(2.67)

\displaystyle Q_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}-\kappa{\theta^{\dagger}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}

\displaystyle D_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}+\kappa{\theta^{\dagger}}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}

(2.68)

The transformed superspace Lagrangian
{\mathbb{L}}_{\kappa,\lambda}
is now given by

{\mathbb{L}}_{\kappa,\lambda}=\frac{1}{2}D_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}XD_{\kappa}X+\lambda h(X).

(2.69)

We are now set to show that kinetic and superpotential rescales are indeed symmetries of the Witten index. Starting off with superpotential rescalings we find that the Witten index changes as

\frac{{\partial}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\partial}\lambda}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~\left(-\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\lambda}S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}\right)e^{-S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}}

(2.70)

where

\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\lambda}S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta~h(X).

(2.71)

Noticing that
h(X)
transforms as a scalar under supersymmetry transformations we find in analogy to the

\hbar
-rescalings
that

\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\lambda}S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}={\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}\left\{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~h(X)|_{\theta={\theta^{\dagger}}=0}\right\}={\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}\left\{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~h(x)\right\}

(2.72)

by the same reasoning as the

\hbar
-rescalings
it now follows that the Witten index is invariant under superpotential rescalings.

We now move on to kinetic rescalings. These will prove to be slightly more tricky to deal with, but can nevertheless be shown to give
Q
-exact changes to the path integrand of the Witten index. As can be expected by now we find that the Witten index changes as

\frac{{\partial}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\partial}\kappa}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~\left(-\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\kappa}S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}\right)e^{-S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}}

(2.73)

under kinetic rescalings. In this case we find

\begin{split}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\kappa}S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}&={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta~\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\kappa}D_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}X\right)D_{\kappa}X+D_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}X\left(\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\kappa}D_{\kappa}X\right)\right]\\
&={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta~\frac{1}{2}\bigg[{\theta^{\dagger}}\dot{X}D_{\kappa}X+\theta D_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}X\dot{X}\bigg].\end{split}

(2.74)

To show that these are
Q
-exact, we’d have to show that the integrand of the above expression corresponds to a supersymmetry multiplet. To see this, we note that the Euclidean time derivative is covariant with respect to supersymmetry. We further also note that

\displaystyle{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta~\theta(\dots)

\displaystyle={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}\left[1-\theta\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\theta}\right](\dots)={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}(\dots)\Big|_{\theta=0},

(2.75)

\displaystyle{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta~{\theta^{\dagger}}(\dots)

\displaystyle=-{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}\theta\left[1-{\theta^{\dagger}}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}{\theta^{\dagger}}}\right](\dots)=-{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}\theta(\dots)\Big|_{{\theta^{\dagger}}=0}.

(2.76)

This follows from the fact that the integrals on the left hand side can’t be
\theta
,
{\theta^{\dagger}}
-graded. We hence find that

\begin{split}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\kappa}S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}&=-\frac{1}{2}{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}\theta~\dot{X}D_{\kappa}X+\frac{1}{2}{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\mathrm{d}}{\theta^{\dagger}}~D_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}X\dot{X}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}Q_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}\Big\{\dot{X}D_{\kappa}X\Big\}\Big|_{{\theta^{\dagger}}=0}+\frac{1}{2}{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}Q_{\kappa}\Big\{D_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}X\dot{X}\Big\}\Big|_{\theta=0}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}\left\{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~\dot{X}D_{\kappa}X\Big|_{\theta={\theta^{\dagger}}=0}\right\}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}\left\{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~D_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}X\dot{X}\Big|_{\theta={\theta^{\dagger}}=0}\right\}.\end{split}

(2.77)

The lowest Grassmann order components are given by

\displaystyle\dot{X}D_{\kappa}X\Big|_{\theta={\theta^{\dagger}}=0}

\displaystyle={\dot{x}}\psi,

\displaystyle D_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}X\dot{X}\Big|_{\theta={\theta^{\dagger}}=0}

\displaystyle=-{\dot{x}}{\psi^{\dagger}}.

(2.78)

We thus find that

\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}\kappa}S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}{{}^{\dagger}}\left\{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~{\dot{x}}\psi\right\}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\kappa}\left\{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~{\dot{x}}{\psi^{\dagger}}\right\}.

(2.79)

Again, by the same reasoning as we had for the

\hbar
-rescalings
we now find that the Witten index is invariant under kinetic rescalings. We hence conclude

Kinetic and superpotential rescaling symmetry

\displaystyle\frac{{\partial}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\partial}\kappa}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\partial}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\partial}\lambda}=0,

\displaystyle S^{\text{off}}\to S^{\text{off}}_{\kappa,\lambda}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}~L({\mathcal{X}},\kappa\dot{{\mathcal{X}}})|_{h\to\lambda h}

(2.80)

the Witten index is invariant under kinetic and superpotential rescalings.

2.3.4
\beta
-independence of the Witten Index

We now use the previously derived symmetries to show the
\beta
-independence of the Witten index through the path integral formalism. We do this in the typical fashion of the previous section by taking a derivative with respect to
\beta
and showing that it vanishes. Taking a
\beta
-derivative of the Witten index isn’t entirely straightforward though, since the
\beta
-dependence is carried in the path integral measure and the integration domain of the action. We can get around this by setting the circumference of the circle to unity, but in return rescaling the Euclidean time parameter as
\tau=\beta\sigma
. Again denoting
{\mathcal{X}}=(x,\psi,{\psi^{\dagger}},F)
the collective fields, we then find that

\begin{split}{\mathcal{I}}(\beta)&=\int\underbrace{{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{X}}(\tau)}_{\text{per. $\beta$}}\exp\bigg\{-\underset{S^{1}(\beta)}{{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}}~L\left({\mathcal{X}},\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{X}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\right)\bigg\}\\
&=\int\underbrace{{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{X}}(\sigma)}_{\text{per. $1$}}\exp\bigg\{-\underset{S^{1}(1)}{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\sigma}~\beta L\left({\mathcal{X}},\beta^{-1}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{X}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\sigma}\right)\bigg\}\end{split}

(2.81)

which is nothing but a combination of an

\hbar
-rescaling
and a kinetic rescaling! Indeed, we find that

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\beta}=\int{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{X}}\left(-\frac{1}{\beta}S^{\text{off}}+\oint\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{\beta}\frac{{\partial}L}{{\partial}\dot{{\mathcal{X}}}}\dot{{\mathcal{X}}}\right)e^{-S^{\text{off}}}=\frac{1}{\beta}\left.\frac{{\partial}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\partial}\mu}\right|_{\mu=1}-\frac{1}{\beta}\left.\frac{{\partial}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\partial}\kappa}\right|_{\kappa=1}=0.

(2.82)

We hence conclude

\beta
-independence

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\beta}=0

(2.83)

the Witten index is also
\beta
-independent in the path integral formalism.

2.3.5
Localising the Witten Index

Finally, we compute the Witten index through the path integral formalism. This will be achieved through a localisation. This goes as follows: We make use of the freedom to deform the path integrand of the Witten index by a
Q
-exact functional in such a way that the contributions to the path integral get localised to a subspace of the function space. This can still be an infinite-dimensional space, but can also reduce to finite-dimensional subspaces or even discrete critical points.

The deformation we consider consists of diagonal kinetic-superpotential rescalings we recall are given by

\begin{split}S^{\text{off}}\to S^{\text{off}}_{\lambda,\lambda}&={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}{\dot{x}}^{2}+\lambda{\psi^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}}+\frac{1}{2}F^{2}+i\lambda h^{\prime}F-\lambda h^{\prime\prime}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\right]\\
&={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\left[\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}{\dot{x}}^{2}+\lambda{\psi^{\dagger}}{\dot{\psi}}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}h^{\prime 2}-\lambda h^{\prime\prime}{\psi^{\dagger}}\psi\right]+{\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\frac{1}{2}(F+i\lambda h^{\prime})^{2}.\end{split}

(2.84)

From this it is clear that in the
\lambda\to\infty
limit the path integral localises to the critical points
x_{0}\in\ker{\mathrm{d}}h
. These points are discrete since we assumed the superpotential
h
to be Morse. Accordingly, we expand around these points as

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=x_{0}+\frac{y}{\lambda},

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi}{\sqrt{\lambda}},

\displaystyle{\psi^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\chi^{\dagger}}}{\sqrt{\lambda}}

(2.85)

yielding an expanded action

S^{\text{off}}_{\lambda,\lambda}={\oint{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\!\begin{multlined}\bigg[\overbrace{\bigg.\frac{1}{2}{\dot{y}}^{2}+{\chi^{\dagger}}{\dot{\chi}}+\frac{1}{2}F^{2}}^{\text{free terms}}+\overbrace{\bigg.h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\Big(iFy-{\chi^{\dagger}}\chi\Big)}^{\text{harmonic terms}}\\
+\underbrace{\sum_{n\geq 3}\frac{1}{n!}\lambda^{2-n}h^{(n)}(x_{0})\Big[iF\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}y}-{\chi^{\dagger}}\chi\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}y^{2}}\Big]y^{n}}_{\text{${\mathcal{O}}(\lambda^{-1})$ non-linear interactions}}\bigg]\equiv S^{\text{off}}_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(\lambda^{-1}).\end{multlined}

(2.86)

Under this reparametrisation the path integral measure transforms as

{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi={\mathcal{D}}y{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\chi~\operatorname{Ber}\frac{\delta(x,\psi,{\psi^{\dagger}},F)}{\delta(y,\chi,{\chi^{\dagger}},F)}

(2.87)

where the functional Berezinian is given by

\operatorname{Ber}\frac{\delta(x,\psi,{\psi^{\dagger}},F)}{\delta(y,\chi,{\chi^{\dagger}},F)}=\operatorname{Det}\frac{\delta x}{\delta y}\operatorname{Det}^{-1}\frac{\delta{\psi^{\dagger}}}{\delta{\chi^{\dagger}}}\operatorname{Det}^{-1}\frac{\delta\psi}{\delta\chi}=\frac{\operatorname{Det}\lambda^{-1}}{\operatorname{Det}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}\operatorname{Det}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}}=1

(2.88)

where ‘
\operatorname{Det}
’ denotes the formal functional determinant of the field rescalings, hence yielding

{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi={\mathcal{D}}y{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\chi.

(2.89)

We also note that these are local reparametrisations on the regions around the critical points in field space.
The Witten index will be computed through a loop expansion around these critical points. Accordingly, we need to define expectation values. We define the expectation value of some functional
G
as

\left\langle G\right\rangle=\mathcal{N}^{-1}\int{\mathcal{D}}y{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\chi~G[y,\chi,{\chi^{\dagger}},F]e^{-S^{\text{off}}_{0}}

(2.90)

where we defined the normalisation to be

\mathcal{N}=\int{\mathcal{D}}y{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\chi~e^{-S^{\text{off}}_{0}}=\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}\frac{\delta^{2}S^{\text{off}}_{0}}{\delta(y,\chi,{\chi^{\dagger}},F)^{2}}.

(2.91)

This functional Berezinian is somewhat nontrivial, since the free action
S^{\text{off}}_{0}
contains nondiagonal terms. One way to get past this is to eliminate the auxiliary field first and then to compute the functional Berezinian of the on-shell free action. Alternatively, it can also be computed as

\begin{split}\operatorname{Ber}\frac{\delta^{2}S^{\text{off}}_{0}}{\delta(y,\chi,{\chi^{\dagger}},F)^{2}}&=\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}-{\mathrm{d}}^{2}/{\mathrm{d}}\tau^{2}&ih^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\\
ih^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})&\mathbbm{1}\end{pmatrix}\operatorname{Det}^{-1}\left(\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}+h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\right)\\
&=\operatorname{Det}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(-\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau^{2}}+h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})^{2}\right)\operatorname{Det}^{-1}\left(\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}+h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\right)\\
&=\frac{\sinh(\beta|h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})|/2)}{\sinh(\beta h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})/2)}=\operatorname{sign}~h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\end{split}

(2.92)

thus yielding a normalisation

\mathcal{N}=\operatorname{sign}~h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0}).

(2.93)

Combining these results we are now ready to compute the Witten index. We find

{\mathcal{I}}=\int{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi~e^{-S^{\text{off}}_{\lambda,\lambda}}=\sum_{x_{0}}\int{\mathcal{D}}y{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\chi~e^{-S^{\text{off}}+{\mathcal{O}}(\lambda^{-1})}\\
=\sum_{x_{0}}\operatorname{sign}~h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\left\langle e^{{\mathcal{O}}(\lambda^{-1})}\right\rangle\xrightarrow{\lambda\to\infty}\sum_{x_{0}}\operatorname{sign}~h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0}).

(2.94)

We hence conclude that

Localisation of the Witten index

{\mathcal{I}}=\int\underbrace{{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi}_{\text{period $\beta$}}~e^{-S^{\text{off}}}=\sum_{x_{0}}\operatorname{sign}~h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})

(2.95)

the Witten index in the path integral formalism.

2.4
Conclusion

In conclusion, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}(-1)^{F}e^{-\beta H}

\displaystyle=\dim\ker H|_{\text{bos}}-\dim\ker H|_{\text{fer}}

(2.96)

\displaystyle=\int\underbrace{{\mathcal{D}}x{\mathcal{D}}F{\mathcal{D}}{\psi^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{D}}\psi}_{\text{period $\beta$}}~e^{-S^{\text{off}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x_{0}}\operatorname{sign}~h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})

the Witten index given in both the Hilbert space formalism and the path integral formalism. The Witten index further also satisfies

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{I}}}{{\mathrm{d}}\beta}=0.

(2.97)

That is, it is indepedent of the Euclidean periodicity
\beta
.

Let us comment on this. We consider an analytic interpretation of the Hilbert space. As was mentioned in the second section, the Hilbert space can be regarded as
\mathcal{H}\cong L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
. One way to look at this is as position space constituting a manifold, with the wavefunctions being
\mathbb{C}^{2}
-valued fields living on this manifold. The supercharges then constitute an operator complex and the Hamiltonian its associated Laplacian. With this interpretation, the Hilbert space expression of the Witten index becomes an analytical statement about the Hamiltonian operator. On the other hand, the expression of the Wi tten index in terms of a sum over signs of Hessians of the superpotential
h
can be regarded as a statement of a topological nature
[
13
]
. We hence find that our computations show a relation between analytical properties of an operator acting on some space and its topology. More general cases could include for example the Atiyah-Singer index theorem, which generally relates analytical properties of complexes on manifolds to topological properties of a corresponding principal bundle
[
13
]
[
14
]
.

Chapter 3
The Third Way Theory

3.1
Introduction

In this chapter, we introduce the Third Way Theory, originally studied by Arvanitakis, Sevrin and Townsend in
[
1
]
. The Third Way Theory is an example of what’s called a third way consistent field theory. These are theories in which ‘auxiliary’ field content is added which however can’t be consistently integrated out of the action. This results in field equations which can’t be the result of any gauge invariant action in only one set of gauge fields, hence constituting an interesting and new contribution to classical field theory. These were originally developed in the context of ‘minimally massive gravity’ (MMG)
[
15
]
[
16
]
[
17
]
by —among others— Arvanitakis and Townsend, which is a modification of topologically massive gravity
[
18
]
. The field theory which is central to this thesis is a third way consistent analogue of topologically massive Yang-Mills theory (TMYM). Its field equation was originally postulated by Sevrin, and the corresponding action was constructed by Arvanitakis
[
1
]
. In this chapter we will go in close detail over the contents of their original paper, as to set the ground for the goal of this thesis, namely to localise this theory.

3.2
Topologically Massive Yang-Mills Theory

We start off by describing the theory on which the Third Way theory is based: Topologically Massive Yang-Mills theory. Throughout these notes I will work in the form-formalism for differential geometry. For conventions and basic identities, see appendix
A.1
.

3.2.1
Yang-Mills Theory

Let us start off by describing Yang-Mills theory. The field content consists of a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued spin-1 gauge vector
A_{\mu}
. The corresponding action is given by

S_{\text{YM}}[A]=\frac{1}{2g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\big[F\ast F\big]=\frac{1}{4g^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~e\kappa_{IJ}F^{I}_{\mu\nu}F^{J\mu\nu}=:\frac{1}{2g^{2}}(F|F)

(3.1)

Its field equations are computed to give

\delta S_{\text{YM}}[A]=\frac{1}{g^{2}}(\delta F|F)=\frac{1}{g^{2}}({\mathcal{D}}\delta A|F)=\frac{1}{g^{2}}(\delta A|{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}F)

(3.2)

hence by the non-degeneracy of the inner product
(\bullet|\bullet)
yielding field equations

\displaystyle\delta S_{\text{YM}}[A]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}F

\displaystyle=0.

(3.3)

These field equations are the
d=3
non-Abelian analogue of sourced Maxwell equations, whereas the Bianchi identity
{\mathcal{D}}F\equiv 0
is the analogue of the unsourced Maxwell equations
[
14
]
.

3.2.2
Chern-Simons Theory

The classical theory

Let us now move on to the second component of topologically massive Yang-Mills theory: The Chern-Simons action. This action warrants a little more detail since there are a lot of interesting things to say about this slightly more obscure action. The action of Chern-Simons theory is given by

\begin{split}S_{\text{CS}}[A]&=\int\Omega_{\text{CS}}(A,F)=\frac{1}{2}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[A{\mathrm{d}}A+\frac{2}{3}A^{3}\bigg]\\
&=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\kappa_{IJ}\Big(A^{I}_{\mu}{\partial}_{\nu}A^{J}_{\rho}+\frac{1}{3}f_{KL}{{}^{J}}A^{I}_{\mu}A^{K}_{\nu}A^{L}_{\rho}\Big)\end{split}

(3.4)

with the Chern-Simons 3-form
\Omega_{\text{CS}}(A,F)
given by

\Omega_{\text{CS}}(A,F)=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\bigg[AF-\frac{1}{3}A^{3}\bigg]=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\bigg[A{\mathrm{d}}A+\frac{2}{3}A^{3}\bigg].

(3.5)

This theory has the special property of not being coupled to gravity. Indeed, the action contains no dependence on the metric whatsoever. Theories like this are called topological field theories. The geometric origins of the Chern-Simons form are rooted in characteristic classes. These are polyforms which contain topological information about the principal bundle to which a particular connection belongs
[
14
]
. Particularly, the characteristic class connected to the Chern-Simons form is the 4
th
Chern character

\operatorname{ch}_{4}(F)=\frac{1}{2!}\operatorname{tr}\bigg(\frac{iF}{2\pi}\bigg)^{2}=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\operatorname{tr}F^{2}

(3.6)

being the 4-form component of the Chern character

\operatorname{ch}(F)=\operatorname{tr}\exp\frac{iF}{2\pi}=:\operatorname{ch}_{0}(F)+\operatorname{ch}_{2}(F)+\operatorname{ch}_{4}(F)+\dots

(3.7)

The Chern character —and in fact any characteristic class— has the property of being closed, that is,

{\mathrm{d}}\operatorname{ch}(F)=0.

(3.8)

From Poincaré’s lemma
[
14
]
it therefore follows that the Chern character is
locally
exact. Particularly, one finds that locally the 4
th
Chern character can be written as

\operatorname{ch}_{4}(A)=\bigg(\frac{i}{2\pi}\bigg)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\Omega_{\text{CS}}(A,F)

(3.9)

where
\Omega_{\text{CS}}
the aforementioned Chern-Simons form. Since the Chern character is gauge invariant, it follows then that
locally
the Chern-Simons form changes by a total derivative under gauge transformations. This is very interesting, because it means that while the Chern-Simons action may not actually be gauge invariant the way it changes under gauge transformations doesn’t actually change the classical dynamics! Varying the Chern-Simons action we find that

\delta S_{\text{CS}}[A]=\frac{1}{2}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\delta A{\mathrm{d}}A+A{\mathrm{d}}\delta A+2\delta AA^{2}\bigg]=\int\operatorname{tr}\Big[\delta AF-{\mathrm{d}}(A\delta A)\Big].

(3.10)

Assuming we work on boundariless spaces we then find field equations

\displaystyle\delta S_{\text{CS}}[A]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=0.

(3.11)

That is, the classical solutions consist of flat connections. We note that under an infinitesimal gauge transformation
\delta_{\theta}A=-{\mathcal{D}}\theta
with parameter
\theta
the Chern-Simons action transforms as

\delta_{\theta}S_{\text{CS}}[A]=\int\operatorname{tr}\big[\delta_{\theta}AF\big]=\int\operatorname{tr}\big[-{\mathcal{D}}(\theta F)\big]=\int{\mathrm{d}}\operatorname{tr}\big[-\theta F\big]

(3.12)

where we used the Bianchi identity
{\mathcal{D}}F\equiv 0
. This is indeed a total derivative as we expected
[
14
]
.

The quantum theory

Let us now move on to make a few comments on the quantum theory. Since in the quantum theory we are interested in computing expectation values

\left\langle{\mathcal{O}}\right\rangle=\int{\mathcal{D}}A~{\mathcal{O}}[A]e^{-S[A]}

(3.13)

it becomes now important that the Chern-Simons action isn’t gauge invariant! Let us sketch the solution to this problem without getting into too much technical details.
In the quantum theory, the parameters defining the theory are

•

a simple gauge group
G
,

•

a bilinear form of
G
(the Killing form) of level
k
.

The level of the bilinear form manifests itself in the trace as
\operatorname{tr}_{k}=k\operatorname{tr}
where
\operatorname{tr}
the trace of the fundamental representation. The action is then given by

S_{\text{CS}}^{k}[A]:=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\operatorname{tr}_{k}\bigg[A{\mathrm{d}}A+\frac{2}{3}A^{3}\bigg]=\frac{k}{4\pi}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[A{\mathrm{d}}A+\frac{2}{3}A^{3}\bigg]=\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{CS}}[A].

(3.14)

This action is still not gauge invariant. However, now it has the interesting property of being
gauge invariant modulo
2\pi i
, hence, leaving the expectation values unchanged and thus making the quantum theory gauge invariant. These integer multiples of
2\pi
by which the action changes can be related to the fact that the third homotopy group for simple gauge groups is given by
\pi_{3}(G)=\mathbb{Z}

[
11
]
.

3.2.3
Topologically Massive Yang-Mills Theory

Now that we’ve treated both the Yang-Mills and Chern-Simons actions we are ready to move on to the topologically massive Yang-Mills (TMYM) action which combines both these theories. The action is given by

S_{\text{TMYM}}[A]=\frac{1}{g^{2}}S_{\text{YM}}[A]+\frac{\mu}{g^{2}}S_{\text{CS}}[A]

(3.15)

where
g^{2}
and
\mu
are couplings which have the dimension of mass. The name of this action will become clear when we work out the corresponding field equations. Varying the action one finds

g^{2}\delta S_{\text{TMYM}}[A]=\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\delta A\ast{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}F+\mu\delta AF\bigg]=\left(\delta A\middle|{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}F+\mu{\ast^{-1}}F\right)

(3.16)

We thus arrive at field equations

\displaystyle\delta S_{\text{TMYM}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\mu F

\displaystyle=0

(3.17)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mu\rho\sigma}{\mathcal{D}}^{\rho}({\ast}F)^{\sigma}+\mu({\ast}F)_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0

(3.18)

Using the field equations we then find that

\displaystyle({\mathcal{D}}\ast)^{2}F-\mu^{2}F

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\nu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}(\ast F)_{\mu}+[F_{\mu\nu},(\ast F)^{\nu}]+\eta\mu^{2}(\ast F)_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0

(3.19)

which are nothing but the field equations for a vector field
\ast F_{\mu}
of mass
\mu
. Here we defined
\eta=\det(\eta_{ab})
the determinant of the flat index metric, as is outlined in the appendix section
A.1.1
.

3.3
Third Way to Source TMYM Theory

In this section we will discuss several ways in which to add a source TMYM theory. We will start off by going over the standard two ways one could add a source to TMYM theory, and then finally we will discuss the so called ‘third way’ to add a source to this theory, with interesting consequences.

3.3.1
Consistency Condition

Adding a source current
j
to TMYM theory is simply achieved by adding it to the field equation as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\mu F

\displaystyle={\ast^{-1}}j

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mu\rho\sigma}{\mathcal{D}}^{\rho}({\ast}F)^{\sigma}+\mu({\ast}F)_{\mu}

\displaystyle=j_{\mu}.

(3.20)

However, we can’t just pick any 1-form to be a source for these equations. Namely, these currents have to satisfy consistency conditions. Particularly, we find that

{\mathcal{D}}\big({\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\mu F\big)=[F,\ast F]+\mu{\mathcal{D}}F\equiv 0

(3.21)

where we used the Bianchi identity as well as
D^{2}=\operatorname{ad}F
. The commutator in the above expression vanishes because it’s antisymmetric in its Lie algebra components but symmetric in its form components under an interchange of the two field strengths.
1
1

1

This result may seem a bit surprising but in fact it’s a generic consistency condition for gauge invariant theories, as we will see shortly.
Because of this, the source current
j
has to satisfy the consistency condition

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}\ast j

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}j^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0.

(3.22)

That is, it has to be covariantly conserved. There are two standard ways of enforcing this result:

•

One could have the source emerge through additional interaction terms in the gauge fields. The source would then be of the form
j^{\mu}=e^{-1}\delta I[A]/\delta A_{\mu}
, for
I[A]
some gauge invariant functional. This source would be conserved off-shell.

•

As a Noether current for some lower spin matter content
\Phi
. In this case the source
j(\Phi)
is conserved is the matter field equations are enforced. However, the gauge fields may be off-shell.

3.3.2
Noether’s Second Theorem

Let us now take a look at Noether’s second theorem. Not only will this be important for understanding how the two aforementioned currents are conserved, but it will also highlight just how special the third way of getting a consistent source current is! We consider a functional
S[A,\Phi]
of the gauge fields and matter content which is both Lorentz and gauge invariant.

From the gauge invariance of this action it follows that under infinitesimal gauge transformations with local parameter
\theta^{I}
we have

0\equiv\delta_{\theta}S[A,\Phi]=\int\ast\bigg[\delta_{\theta}A_{\mu}^{I}\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S}{\delta A_{\mu}^{I}}+\delta_{\theta}\Phi^{i}\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\Phi^{i}}\bigg]=\int\ast\bigg[-{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\theta^{I}\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S}{\delta A_{\mu}^{I}}+\delta_{\theta}\Phi^{i}\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\Phi^{i}}\bigg]\\
=\int\ast\bigg[\theta^{I}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\bigg(\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S}{\delta A_{\mu}^{I}}\bigg)+\delta_{\theta}\Phi^{i}\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\Phi^{i}}\bigg].

(3.23)

Since this holds for any local parameter
\theta^{I}
we find the much celebrated

Noether’s second theorem

Let
S[A,\Phi]
be a functional which is gauge and Lorentz invariant. It then satisfies the conservation law

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\bigg(\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S}{\delta A_{\mu}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Phi&\text{on-shell}\\
A_{\mu}&\text{off-shell}\end{cases}

(3.24)

For example, if we take
S=S_{\text{TMYM}}
we arrive at the consistency condition
3.21
. The two source currents can be achieved as follows: For a source current through coupling to lower spin matter we consider a total action

S[A,\Phi]=S_{\text{TMYM}}[A]+S_{0,\frac{1}{2}}[\Phi,A]

(3.25)

where
S_{0,\frac{1}{2}}
a matter Lagrangian. In this case we find a conserved current

\displaystyle j^{\mu}(\Phi)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta S_{0,\frac{1}{2}}}{\delta A_{\mu}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}j^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Phi&\text{on-shell}\\
A_{\mu}&\text{off-shell}\end{cases}

(3.26)

yielding the first kind of conserved current. The second can be found by taking an action

S[A]=S_{\text{TMYM}}[A]-I[A]

(3.27)

yielding an identically conserved current

\displaystyle j^{\mu}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{e}\frac{\delta I}{\delta A_{\mu}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}j^{\mu}

\displaystyle\equiv 0.

(3.28)

Note, however, that the most general gauge invariant action which is (up to) second order derivatives in the gauge fields is exactly the TMYM action
[
17
]
. As such, sourcing the action this way would lead to higher order derivative terms.

3.3.3
Third Way Source Term

We now move on to discuss a third way to source TMYM theory. This way was due to Sevrin and borrows from the methodologies of MMG, instead now applied to TMYM theory. In this approach, the conserved current will be of the form

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle\propto\ast[\ast F,\ast F]

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle j_{\mu}

\displaystyle\propto[F_{\mu\nu},({\ast}F)^{\nu}].

(3.29)

And it is here that comes the catch to the story: The reason that this current isn’t produced by the aforementioned methods of obtaining a source,
is that it makes use of the field equations for the gauge fields!
We find that using the TMYM field equations

{\mathcal{D}}\ast j\propto[{\mathcal{D}}\ast F,\ast F]\propto[F,\ast F]=0.

(3.30)

However, this by itself isn’t enough because we used unsourced TMYM field equations. If we use the sourced TMYM field equations with this source we find

{\mathcal{D}}\ast j\propto[{\mathcal{D}}\ast F,\ast F]=-\mu[F,\ast F]+[{\ast^{-1}}j,\ast F]\propto[[\ast F,\ast F],\ast F]

(3.31)

Using the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Jacobi identity we can then show that the RHS vanishes:

[[\ast F,\ast F],\ast F]=\frac{1}{3}[[\ast F,\ast F],\ast F]-\frac{1}{3}\Big([\ast F,[\ast F,\ast F]]-[[\ast F,\ast F],\ast F]\Big)\\
\overset{\text{\ref{Z2 graded Jacobi identity}}}{=}\frac{1}{3}[[\ast F,\ast F],\ast F]-\frac{1}{3}[[\ast F,\ast F],\ast F]=0.

(3.32)

From this, it follows that the aformentioned current can be consistently used to source the TMYM field equations. For dimensional reasons we now normalise the current as

j=-\frac{1}{2m}\ast[\ast F,\ast F]

(3.33)

introducing a new dimensionful parameter
m
with the dimension of mass. In conclusion we now have

Third Way sourced TMYM field equations

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\mu F

\displaystyle={\ast^{-1}}j

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2m}\ast[\ast F,\ast F]

(3.34)

satisfying the consistency condition

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}j^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0

A_{\mu}

on
-shell

(3.35)

which —unlike Noether charges— only holds on-shell.

3.4
The Third Way Theory

We have now constructed a new way to source TMYM theory. However, as far as we have explained it isn’t yet obvious how to translate this into an action formalism, mainly due to the fact that this approach is distinct from the usual ways one sources TMYM theory. Before moving on to an action description, we take a closer look at the field equations of the presumed Third Way Theory:

Third Way field equations

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\frac{1}{2m}[\ast F,\ast F]+\mu F=0

(3.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{m}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mu\rho\sigma}\Big({\mathcal{D}}^{\rho}{\ast}F^{\sigma}+\frac{1}{2m}[{\ast}F^{\rho},{\ast}F^{\sigma}]\Big)+\mu{\ast}F_{\mu}=0

(3.37)

the field equations of the presumed Third Way Theory, with dimensionful constants
m
and
\mu
of mass dimension 1.

We will now go over various aspects of the conjectured Third Way Theory: It’s parity, the way it couples to 3D gravity and the way it couples to lower spin matter fields. In all of these regards we will see that the Third Way Theory exhibits quite remarkable and very unorthodox properties.

3.4.1
Parity

Let us start off by studying whether or not the on-shell Third Way theory is parity invariant. By note that if we take
A
to be parity-even, the field equations are not parity invariant, even if
\mu=0
. Indeed, if
A
is parity-even then so is
F
from which it follows that
\ast F
is parity-odd. This is in conflict with the
[\ast F,\ast F]
term which has to be parity-even. To solve this issue one introduces the ad hoc parity transformations

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}A+\frac{1}{m}\ast F

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{P}F+\underbrace{\frac{1}{m}\bigg[{\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\frac{1}{2m}[\ast F,\ast F]\bigg]}_{\text{$\propto$ $\mu=0$ field equations}}

(3.38)

One thus finds that for
\mu=0
on-shell,
F
is parity-even,
\ast F
is parity-odd and
[\ast F,\ast F]
. However, in contrast to the previous approach
{\mathcal{D}}\ast F
will be neither. The way it transforms non-trivially will make it so that the field equations are parity invariant on-shell. Indeed, one finds that

{\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\frac{1}{2m}[\ast F,\ast F]\xrightarrow{\text{on-shell $P$}}\big({\mathcal{D}}+\frac{1}{m}\operatorname{ad}{\ast}F\big)\big(-{\ast}F\big)+\frac{1}{2m}[-{\ast}F,-{\ast}F]\\
=-\bigg({\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\frac{1}{2m}[\ast F,\ast F]\bigg).

(3.39)

For these transformations, parity is thus conserved on-shell. This feature which we now have introduced ad hoc will become manifest through the off-shell formulation of the Third Way Theory.

3.4.2
Coupling to Lower Spin Fields

Coupling the Third Way Theory to lower spin matter will in the on-shell formalism also turn out to be a non-trivial task. Let us start off by trying to naively source the Third Way field equations as

{\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\frac{1}{2m}[\ast F,\ast F]+\mu F={\ast^{-1}}j

(3.40)

where we assume
j^{\mu}
some Noether current for lower spin content. We then find using previous results that

{\mathcal{D}}{\ast^{-1}}j\overset{\text{\ref{consistency condition 1}}}{=}\frac{1}{m}[{\mathcal{D}}\ast F,\ast F]\overset{\text{\ref{naive sourcing}}}{=}-\frac{1}{2m^{2}}[[\ast F,\ast F],\ast F]-\frac{\mu}{m}[F,\ast F]+\frac{1}{m}[{\ast^{-1}}j,\ast F]\\
\overset{\text{\ref{vanishing 1}}}{=}\frac{1}{m}[{\ast^{-1}}j,\ast F].

(3.41)

We hence arrive at the on-shell sourcing consistency condition

\displaystyle\Big({\mathcal{D}}+\frac{1}{m}\operatorname{ad}{\ast}F\Big){\ast^{-1}}j

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}j_{\mu}+\frac{1}{m}[{\ast}F^{\mu},j_{\mu}]

\displaystyle=0

(3.42)

which is clearly in conflict with ordinary sourcing, since
j
can only be consistently interpret as a Noether current in the limit
m^{-1}\to 0
. Before offering a solution to this problem let us first simplify the notation. We note that we can simplify expression
3.42
as

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{D}}^{\dagger}}j

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}

\displaystyle:={\mathcal{D}}+\frac{1}{m}\operatorname{ad}{\ast}F

(3.43)

It turns out, however, that there is a way to solve this problem. For this we assume that
m\neq\mu
and that we’re given a Noether current
j
, i.e.
{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}j=0
. Then we can construct a consistent sourcing of the on-shell Third Way field equation by using a current

{\mathcal{J}}:=j-\frac{1}{m-\mu}\ast\bigg[{\mathbb{D}}j+\frac{1}{2m(m-\mu)}[j,j]\bigg].

(3.44)

Indeed, we start off by noting that

\begin{split}{\mathbb{D}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}[{\mathbb{D}},{\mathbb{D}}]&=\frac{1}{2}[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}]+\frac{1}{m}[{\mathcal{D}},\operatorname{ad}\ast F]+\frac{1}{2m^{2}}[\operatorname{ad}\ast F,\operatorname{ad}\ast F]\\
&=\operatorname{ad}\bigg[F+\frac{1}{m}{\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\frac{1}{2m^{2}}[\ast F,\ast F]\bigg]\\
&=\operatorname{ad}\bigg[\frac{m-\mu}{m}F+\frac{1}{m}\bigg({\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\frac{1}{2m}[\ast F,\ast F]+\mu F\bigg)\bigg]\\
&\overset{\text{on-shell}}{=}\operatorname{ad}\bigg[\frac{m-\mu}{m}F+\frac{1}{m}{\ast^{-1}}{\mathcal{J}}\bigg]\end{split}

(3.45)

It thus follows that

\begin{split}{\ast^{-1}}{{\mathbb{D}}^{\dagger}}{\mathcal{J}}&=-{\mathbb{D}}{\ast^{-1}}{\mathcal{J}}=-{\mathbb{D}}{\ast^{-1}}j+\frac{1}{m-\mu}\operatorname{ad}\bigg[{\mathbb{D}}^{2}+\frac{1}{m(m-\mu)}{\mathbb{D}}j\bigg]j\\
&\overset{\text{\ref{Dbb^2}}}{=}\operatorname{ad}\bigg[-\frac{1}{m}F+\frac{1}{m-\mu}\bigg(\frac{m-\mu}{m}F+\frac{1}{m}{\ast^{-1}}{\mathcal{J}}+\frac{1}{m(m-\mu)}{\mathbb{D}}j\bigg)\bigg]j\\
&=\frac{1}{m(m-\mu)}\operatorname{ad}\bigg[{\ast^{-1}}{\mathcal{J}}+\frac{1}{m-\mu}{\mathbb{D}}j\bigg]j\\
&\overset{\text{\ref{Jg}}}{=}\frac{1}{m(m-\mu)}\bigg[[{\ast^{-1}}j,j]+\frac{1}{2m(m-\mu)}[[j,j],j]\bigg]\\
&\equiv 0\end{split}

(3.46)

where the final step follows in analogy to equations
3.30
and
3.32
. We thus conclude that

Sourced Third Way field equations

The Third Way Theory is consistently coupled to lower spin fields as

{\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\frac{1}{2m}[\ast F,\ast F]+\mu F={\ast^{-1}}{\mathcal{J}}

(3.47)

where we defined the Third Way current
{\mathcal{J}}
in terms of a Noether current
j
, satisfying
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}j^{\mu}=0
as

{\mathcal{J}}:=j-\frac{1}{m-\mu}\ast\bigg[{\mathcal{D}}j+\frac{1}{m}[\ast F,j]+\frac{1}{2m(m-\mu)}[j,j]\bigg].

(3.48)

3.5
The Third Way Theory: Off-Shell

3.5.1
Third Way Action

Noether’s second theorem

We now move on to describing the off-shell formalism of the Third Way theory. We start off by making the important observation that the Third Way field equations
cannot
come from a local gauge invariant action. Indeed, we recall from section
3.3.2
that a consequence of Noether’s second theorem is that local gauge invariant theories have field equations which identically satisfy

{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\frac{\delta S}{\delta A_{\mu}}\equiv 0.

(3.49)

However, in the case of the Third Way field equation we find that

{\mathcal{D}}\bigg({\mathcal{D}}\ast F+\frac{1}{2m}[\ast F,\ast F]+\mu F\bigg)=\frac{1}{m}[{\mathcal{D}}\ast F,\ast F]~\not{\equiv}~0.

(3.50)

Indeed, the whole point of the Third Way being a new consistent way of doing gauge theory in three dimensions was that the consistency of the field equations invoked the on-shell conditions for the gauge fields.

Third Way Action

As it turns out, the way to construct an action by introducing an ‘auxiliary’ 1-form
G
of mass dimension 2 and taking the dimensionful parameters to satisfy
m\neq\mu
. The action which gives the Third Way field equations is then given by

S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}[A,G]=\begin{multlined}\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[FG-\frac{m-\mu}{2m}G{\ast^{-1}}G+\frac{1}{2m}\bigg(G{\mathcal{D}}G+\frac{2}{3m}G^{3}\bigg)\bigg]\\
+\frac{\mu}{g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[A{\mathrm{d}}A+\frac{2}{3}A^{3}\bigg].\end{multlined}

(3.51)

We note that the ‘auxilary’ field
G
is not auxiliary in the typical sense we mean in the context of supersymmetry, since it appears with derivatives in the action.
Because of this, we can’t consistently eliminate it from the action.
To see this, let us work out the field equations: Varying the action yields

\displaystyle\delta S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}=\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\big(\delta A+\frac{1}{m}\delta G\big)\bigg({\mathcal{D}}G+\frac{1}{2m}[G,G]+\mu F\bigg)

(3.52)

\displaystyle+\frac{m-\mu}{m}\delta G\big(F-{\ast^{-1}}G\big)\bigg]

resulting in field equations

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}G+\frac{1}{2m}[G,G]+\mu F

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\ast F.

(3.53)

If we substitute the latter in the former we arrive at the Third Way field equation.

Parameter constraints

We can constrain the parameters
\mu
and
m
by noting that the stress tensor will be that of Yang-Mills theory (at least using
G=\ast F
), multiplied by a factor
(m-\mu)/m
. By demanding positive energy we can constrain the parameters by the condition

m(m-\mu)>0.

(3.54)

Sourcing the Third Way theory

Sourcing the Third Way theory now becomes a straightforward procedure, and will also connect to the on-shell formalism. One simply adds the standard source term

S[A,G]=S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}[A,G]-\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\big[A\ast^{-1}j\big]

(3.55)

where
j
is taken to be some Noether current, satisfying
{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}j=0
. In this case varying the action yields

\displaystyle\delta S=\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\big(\delta A+\frac{1}{m}\delta G\big)\bigg({\mathcal{D}}G+\frac{1}{2m}[G,G]+\mu F-{\ast^{-1}}j\bigg)

(3.56)

\displaystyle+\frac{m-\mu}{m}\delta G\bigg(F+\frac{1}{m-\mu}{\ast^{-1}}j-{\ast^{-1}}G\bigg)\bigg]

resulting in field equations

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}G+\frac{1}{2m}[G,G]+\mu F

\displaystyle={\ast^{-1}}j,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\ast F+\frac{1}{m-\mu}j.

(3.57)

One notes now that on-shell
G
gains
j
dependence. It is exactly this which after substituting the latter equation into the former produces the unusual source
{\mathcal{J}}
introduced into the on-shell Third Way field equations
3.47
.

3.5.2
Manifest Parity

So far, we have discussed all aspects of the Third Way theory discussed in the on-shell approach, except for the way parity is preserved for
\mu=0
. To see how parity is preserved we will have to consider an alternate way to parametrise the Third Way action. We introduce a second connection
{\bar{A}}
for the gauge group which transforms
diagonally
with
A
under gauge transformations. That is, their gauge transformations are parametrised by the same local parameter. The 1-form
G
which is an adjoint tensor of the gauge group will then be related to the connections
A
and
{\bar{A}}
as

G=m({\bar{A}}-A)

(3.58)

which is still an adjoint tensor of the gauge group since differences of connections are tensors. In this case the action will take on the much more elegant form

Parity manifest Third Way action

\displaystyle S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}[A,{\bar{A}}]={}

\displaystyle\frac{m}{g^{2}}S_{\text{CS}}[{\bar{A}}]-\frac{{\bar{m}}}{g^{2}}S_{\text{CS}}[A]-\frac{m{\bar{m}}}{2g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\Big[({\bar{A}}-A){\ast^{-1}}({\bar{A}}-A)\Big]

(3.59)

the Third Way action in terms of
A
and
{\bar{A}}
, with field equations

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=F-m{\ast^{-1}}({\bar{A}}-A)

(3.60)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={\bar{F}}-{\bar{m}}{\ast^{-1}}({\bar{A}}-A)

\displaystyle{\bar{m}}

\displaystyle:=m-\mu

(3.61)

and parity transformations

A\overset{P}{\longleftrightarrow}{\bar{A}}

(3.62)

Let us verify that this indeed agrees with previous results. We start off by noting that the barred field strength can be rewritten as

{\bar{F}}={\mathrm{d}}{\bar{A}}+{\bar{A}}{{}^{2}}\overset{\text{\ref{G(A,Ab)}}}{=}F+\frac{1}{m}{\mathcal{D}}G+\frac{1}{m^{2}}G^{2}.

(3.63)

With this the Chern-Simons term in
{\bar{A}}
becomes

\begin{split}\frac{m}{g^{2}}S_{\text{CS}}[{\bar{A}}]&=\frac{m}{2g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[{\bar{A}}{\bar{F}}-\frac{1}{3}{\bar{A}}{{}^{3}}\bigg]\\
&\overset{\text{\ref{tussenstap Fb}}}{=}\frac{m}{g^{2}}S_{\text{CS}}[A]+\frac{1}{2g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\big(A{\mathcal{D}}G+FG-A^{2}G\big)+\frac{1}{m}G{\mathcal{D}}G+\frac{2}{3m^{2}}G^{3}\bigg]\\
&=\frac{m}{g^{2}}S_{\text{CS}}[A]+\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[FG+\frac{1}{2m}\bigg(G{\mathcal{D}}G+\frac{2}{3m}G^{3}\bigg)\bigg]\end{split}

(3.64)

where in the final step we integrated by parts and used the fact that
{\mathrm{d}}A=F-A^{2}
.

Connection to previous parity transformations

We now show that these parity transformations also yield the previous parity transformations as in equation
3.38
. This follows from field equation
3.60
as

A\overset{P}{\longrightarrow}{\bar{A}}\overset{\text{\ref{field equation F}}}{=}A+\frac{1}{m}\ast F

(3.65)

indeed agreeing with the on-shell results, when the fields are taken to be on-shell.

Further constraints on the parameters in the quantum theory

Let us also briefly discuss how the parameters of the Third Way are constrained in the quantum theory. In accordance with the results of quantum Chern-Simons theory we find constraints for gauge invariance given by

\displaystyle\frac{m}{g^{2}}

\displaystyle=:\frac{k}{2\pi},

\displaystyle\frac{{\bar{m}}}{g^{2}}

\displaystyle=:\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{m{\bar{m}}}{2g^{2}}

\displaystyle=g^{2}\frac{k{\bar{k}}}{8\pi^{2}}.

(3.66)

with
k,{\bar{k}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{0}
. This in turn allows us to rewrite the Third Way action as

S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}=\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{CS}}[{\bar{A}}]-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi}S_{\text{CS}}[A]-\frac{k{\bar{k}}}{4\pi^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2\ell}({\bar{A}}-A)\ast^{-1}({\bar{A}}-A)\bigg]

(3.67)

where we defined the parameter
\ell:=g^{-2}
with the dimension of length.

3.5.3
The Bifundamental Scalar and the Brout-Englert-Higgs Mechanism

To close off this chapter we discuss a context in which the Third Way Theory emerges through the BEH mechanism. This was originally discovered in M-theory, particularly for multi M2-brane dynamics in
[
19
]
by Mukhi and Papageorgakis in 2008. Later in 2011 Mukhi studied the symmetry breaking of the bifundamental scalar in greater detail in
[
20
]
. Here we will briefly outline how the Third Way Theory (together with interactions with a BEH scalar) emerges in this context.

The bifundamental scalar and connection

We start off by describing the gauge group and representations of this field theory. The gauge group of this theory will be of the form
G\times G
, where
G
a simple Lie group. A connection
A^{\text{bi}}
of the gauge group
G\times G
will then be of the form

A^{\text{bi}}=A\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}+{\mathbbm{1}}\otimes{\bar{A}}

(3.68)

transforming under gauge transformations as

\displaystyle A^{\text{bi}}

\displaystyle\to e^{\theta^{\text{bi}}}\Big(A^{\text{bi}}+{\mathrm{d}}\Big)e^{-\theta^{\text{bi}}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\begin{cases}A\to e^{\theta}(A+{\mathrm{d}})e^{-\theta}\\
{\bar{A}}\to e^{\bar{\theta}}({\bar{A}}+{\mathrm{d}})e^{-{\bar{\theta}}}\end{cases}

(3.69)

\displaystyle\delta_{\theta^{\text{bi}}}A^{\text{bi}}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\theta^{\text{bi}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\delta_{\theta}A=-{\mathcal{D}}\theta\\
\delta_{\bar{\theta}}{\bar{A}}=-{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}{\bar{\theta}}\end{cases}

(3.70)

where we defined the local gauge parameter

\displaystyle\theta^{\text{bi}}

\displaystyle:=\theta\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}+{\mathbbm{1}}\otimes{\bar{\theta}}

\displaystyle e^{\theta^{\text{bi}}}

\displaystyle=e^{\theta}\otimes e^{\bar{\theta}}

(3.71)

It is thus clear that
A
and
{\bar{A}}
transform like
indepedent
gauge connections of
G
. The scalar field we consider lies in the so-called bifundamental representation. That is, the bifundamental scalar
\Phi
is a matrix of the fundamental representation of
G
(which for all clarity doesn’t have to lie in the Lie algebra) which transforms under
G\times G
as

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\to e^{\theta}\Phi e^{-{\bar{\theta}}},

\displaystyle\delta_{\theta,{\bar{\theta}}}\Phi

\displaystyle=\theta\Phi-\Phi{\bar{\theta}}.

(3.72)

In this representation the bifundamental covariant derivative thus acts on it as

{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Phi={\mathrm{d}}\Phi+A\Phi-\Phi{\bar{A}}.

(3.73)

Symmetry breaking

Let us now move on to the theory of interest. This theory is given by an action

S[A^{\text{bi}},\Phi]=\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{CS}}[{\bar{A}}]-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi}S_{\text{CS}}[A]-\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Phi\ast^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Phi\bigg]+\int\ast^{-1}V(\Phi).

(3.74)

Here
V(\Phi)
is a potential such that
\Phi
has a vacuum expectation value
\left\langle\Phi\right\rangle
of the form

\displaystyle\left\langle\Phi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=v{\mathbbm{1}}

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=v{\mathbbm{1}}+\Sigma

(3.75)

where by
\Sigma
we denote the fluctuations around the vacuum expectation value
\left\langle\Phi\right\rangle
. We then find that

{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Phi=v({\bar{A}}-A)+{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Sigma

(3.76)

so that the kinetic term for
\Phi
becomes

\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Phi\ast^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Phi\bigg]=\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}v^{2}({\bar{A}}-A)\ast^{-1}({\bar{A}}-A)+v({\bar{A}}-A)\ast^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Sigma\\
+\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Sigma\ast^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Sigma\bigg].

(3.77)

If we now choose to redefine the vacuum expectation value
v
to be

v^{2}=:\frac{k{\bar{k}}}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\ell}

(3.78)

we find that the action reduces to

\displaystyle S[A,{\bar{A}},\Sigma]=\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{CS}}[{\bar{A}}]-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi}S_{\text{CS}}[A]-\frac{k{\bar{k}}}{4\pi^{2}}\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2\ell}({\bar{A}}-A)\ast^{-1}({\bar{A}}-A)\bigg]

(3.79)

\displaystyle-\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Sigma\ast^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Sigma-\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\frac{k{\bar{k}}}{\ell}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}({\bar{A}}-A)\ast^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}^{\text{bi}}\Sigma-\hat{V}(\Sigma)\bigg]

which indeed yields the Third Way Theory along with some interaction terms. Let us now more closely look at the way the gauge group
G\times G
reduces under this symmetry breaking. We find that our choice of the vacuum expectation value
\left\langle\Phi\right\rangle
transforms under gauge transformations as

\displaystyle\delta_{\theta,{\bar{\theta}}}\left\langle\Phi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=v({\bar{\theta}}-\theta)=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle={\bar{\theta}}.

(3.80)

We thus find that the gauge group
G\times G
breaks to its diagonal subgroup as

G\times G\to\big(G\times G\big)\big|_{\text{diag}}\cong G.

(3.81)

We hence arrive back at the case of the Third Way Theory where we demanded the gauge fields to transform diagonally under gauge transformations.

Chapter 4
Localisation of the Third Way Theory

4.1
Introduction

In this chapter we move on to the main goal of this thesis: The localisation of the Third Way Theory. We will start off by getting deeper into the specifics of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry in Euclidean
d=3
, continuing the discussion we started in section
1.2
. Particularly, we will start off by taking a closer look at the gauge vector multiplet. We will discuss this object starting from superconnections in superspace and derive how the vector multiplet and its gauge covariant supersymmetry transformations naturally arise from this formalism, as well as how gauge transformations as we know and love them are to be understood in this formalism, that is, to relate ‘supergauge transformations’ to ‘gauge transformations’. We then move on to describe some theories in this formalism. We will in particular take a closer look at super-Chern-Simons theory and super-Yang-Mills theory. After this, we move on to describe the way to localise these theories.

Having done these things, we have finally set the stage to describe progress on the localisation of the Third Way Theory. We start off by treating a toy model of the Third Way Theory which we coined Proca-Chern-Simons theory, which contains only a single gauge field. To localise it, we have to supersymmetrise this theory. Interestingly, this will be in complete analogy to super-Chern-Simons theory, but where we deform the supersymmetry transformations of the gauginos to accomodate for the mass term, yielding new supersymmetry transformations which we will coin ‘massive supersymmetry transformations’. These are somewhat analogous to a central charge but not quite the same. As we will see, these will localise Proca-Chern-Simons theory not on its field equations, but rather on sourced field equations. After having dealt with Proca-Chern-Simons theory we move on to the Third Way Theory. We start off by describing a first attempt by Arvanitakis to localise this theory. This will successfully localise the Third Way Theory on one of its
diagonal
field equations, again sourced by the auxiliary fields. This achieves half a localisation of the Third Way Theory. Instead of trying to localise on the antidiagonal field equations, we take a slightly different approach and try to localise on the
A
and
{\bar{A}}
field equations
3.60
and
3.61
. This will be achieved by extending the results of Proca-Chern-Simons theory to the Third Way Theory, resulting in Third Way supersymmetries, which are deformations of standard supersymmetry transformations. These will then be used to localise the Third Way Theory on its sourced field equations.

4.2
Superspace Gauge Theory

In this section, we will for the most part follow Sohnius’ “Introducing supersymmetry”
[
3
]
, which instead of dealing with
d=3+0
,
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry deals with
d=3+1
,
{\mathcal{N}}=1
supersymmetry. However, this isn’t a bad thing as the former can actually be regarded as a dimensional reduction of the latter. From this viewpoint one of the directions is compactified to a circle and the dependence along this direction is ignored. The four dimensional Dirac spinor Grassmann coordinate in
d=3+1
then breaks up into two 2-spinors in
d=3+0
resulting in
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry. If one were to consider modes along this circle it would manifest itself as a central charge
[
3
]
[
11
]
.

4.2.1
The Superconnection

Defining the superconnection

We give a superspace geometric derivation of the field content and supersymmetries of supersymmetric gauge theories. This is achieved by considering the superspace analogue of a connection 1-form. Before proceiding through this section we encourage the reader to take another look at section
1.2
and appendix
A.2.1
. The superfield in consideration here is the superconnection

{\mathcal{A}}_{A}(x,\theta,{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}})=\big\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu},~{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha},~{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\alpha}\big\}

(4.1)

for which each component is a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued general complex superfield with overall Lorentz index
\mu
or
\alpha
.
1
1

1

Since we’re working in flat space here we don’t bother to distinguish between flat and curved indices in our notation.

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}
is taken to be Grassmann-even and
{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\alpha}
are taken to be Grassmann-odd. Under so-called
super
gauge transformations this superconnection transforms as

{\mathcal{A}}_{A}\to e^{X}\big({\mathcal{A}}_{A}+D_{A}\big)e^{-X},

(4.2)

with
X
a general
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued superfield and
D_{A}
the covariant superspace derivatives introduced in equation
1.18
. Infinitesimally this gives

\delta_{X}{\mathcal{A}}_{A}=-\nabla_{A}X:=-D_{A}X-[{\mathcal{A}}_{A},X\}

(4.3)

in analogy to connection 1-forms as introduced for non-supersymmetric theories. The bracket
[\bullet,\bullet\}
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded commutator given by

\displaystyle[A,B\}

\displaystyle:=AB-(-)^{|A||B|}BA

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle[A,B\}^{I}

\displaystyle:=f_{JK}{{}^{I}}A^{J}B^{K}

(4.4)

which satisfies
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Jacobi identities

[A,[B,C\}\}=[[A,B\},C\}+(-)^{|A||B|}[B,[A,C\}\}.

(4.5)

Here
|\bullet|
is used to denote the Grassmann parity
[
3
]
.

Defining the superfield strength

We now move on to describe the superanalogue of the field strength tensor. We define the superfield strength tensor
{\mathcal{F}}_{AB}
along with the supertorsion tensor
{\mathcal{T}}_{AB}{{}^{C}}
through the relations

\displaystyle[D_{A},D_{B}\}

\displaystyle=:{\mathcal{T}}_{AB}{{}^{C}}D_{C}

(4.6)

\displaystyle[\nabla_{A},\nabla_{B}\}

\displaystyle=:{\mathcal{T}}_{AB}{{}^{C}}\nabla_{C}+{\mathcal{F}}_{AB}

(4.7)

From this definition and equation
1.21
one can immediately derive that the supertorsion tensor is given by its only non-vanishing component

\displaystyle{\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{T}}}_{\alpha\beta}{{}^{\mu}}

\displaystyle=2i\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha\beta}},

\displaystyle\{D_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle={\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{T}}}_{\alpha\beta}{{}^{\mu}}{\partial}_{\mu}.

(4.8)

We further note that an explicit expression for the superfield strength tensor can be derived from working out

[\nabla_{A},\nabla_{B}\}=\big[D_{A}+{\mathcal{A}}_{A},~D_{B}+{\mathcal{A}}_{B}\big\}={\mathcal{T}}_{AB}{{}^{C}}D_{C}+2D_{[A}{\mathcal{A}}_{B\}}+[{\mathcal{A}}_{A},{\mathcal{A}}_{B}\}\\
={\mathcal{T}}_{AB}{{}^{C}}\nabla_{C}+\Big(2D_{[A}{\mathcal{A}}_{B\}}+[{\mathcal{A}}_{A},{\mathcal{A}}_{B}\}-{\mathcal{T}}_{AB}{{}^{C}}{\mathcal{A}}_{C}\Big).

(4.9)

where
[…}
denotes a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded antisymmetrisation, with vector indices regarded as ‘even’ and spinor indices regarded as ‘odd’.
2
2

2

\mu\leftrightarrow\nu
and
\mu\leftrightarrow\alpha
are antisymmetric and
\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta
is symmetric
This gives us an explicit expression

{\mathcal{F}}_{AB}=2D_{[A}{\mathcal{A}}_{B\}}+[{\mathcal{A}}_{A},{\mathcal{A}}_{B}\}-{\mathcal{T}}_{AB}{{}^{C}}{\mathcal{A}}_{C}

(4.10)

for the superfield strength tensor
[
3
]
. The superconnection satisfies Bianchi identities

\displaystyle[[\nabla_{[A},[\nabla_{B},\nabla_{C\}}\}\}

\displaystyle\equiv 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\nabla_{[A}{\mathcal{F}}_{BC\}}-{\mathcal{T}}_{[AB|}{{}^{D}}{\mathcal{F}}_{D|C\}}

\displaystyle\equiv 0.

(4.11)

4.2.2
Constraints

We recall from the case of the chiral and antichiral multiplets that a general superfield is not an irreducible representation of supersymmetry. What we do then is to impose constraints on these superfields which preferably (anti)commute with supersymmetry to reduce the field content. For the vector multiplet we will introduce two kinds of constraints: The so-called conventional constraints and the representation preserving constraints
[
3
]
.
Before moving on to describe these two kinds of constraints we quickly introduce the necessary notation for the field strength components:

\displaystyle[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\nu}]

\displaystyle={\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\{\nabla_{\alpha},\nabla_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{F}}_{\alpha\beta}\Big.

(4.12)

\displaystyle[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\alpha}]

\displaystyle={\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\alpha}

\displaystyle\{\nabla_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle=2i{\not{\nabla}}_{\alpha\beta}+{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}\Big.

(4.13)

\displaystyle[\nabla_{\mu},{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\alpha}]

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\alpha}

\displaystyle\{{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\beta}\}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}\Big.

(4.14)

Conventional constraints

The conventional constraint arises from the fact that a connection 1-form can be arbitrarily shifted by an adjoint tensor of the gauge group without ceasing to be a connection 1-form. We thus have the freedom to redefine the superconnection as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{A}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\text{redef}}{\mathcal{A}}_{A}+{\mathcal{X}}_{A}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}{\mathcal{A}}_{A}\to e^{X}({\mathcal{A}}_{A}+D_{A})e^{-X}\\
{\mathcal{X}}_{A}\to e^{X}{\mathcal{X}}_{A}e^{-X}\end{cases}

(4.15)

From this it follows that the superfield strength transforms as

{\mathcal{F}}_{AB}\xrightarrow{\text{redef}}{\mathcal{F}}_{AB}+2\nabla_{[A}{\mathcal{X}}_{B\}}+[{\mathcal{X}}_{A},{\mathcal{X}}_{B}\}-{\mathcal{T}}_{AB}{{}^{C}}{\mathcal{X}}_{C}

(4.16)

If we now choose

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{4}\gamma_{\mu}{{}^{\alpha\beta}}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{X}}}_{\alpha}=0

(4.17)

we find that

{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}\xrightarrow{\text{redef}}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha\beta}}\gamma_{\mu}{{}^{\gamma\delta}}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\gamma\delta}\overset{\text{\ref{Lemma symmetric}}}{=}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}-{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{(\alpha\beta)}={\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{[\alpha\beta]}.

(4.18)

That is, we can generally impose the constraint

\displaystyle{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{(\alpha\beta)}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{[\alpha\beta]}=:\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}.

(4.19)

This constraint which can always be imposed is referred to as the
conventional constraint

[
3
]
. A direct consequence of this is that we can express
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}
in terms of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\alpha}
. Indeed, we see that

0={\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}_{(\alpha\beta)}=D_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\beta}+{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\beta}{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}+\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\beta}\}-2i\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha\beta}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}

(4.20)

Again making use of equation
A.47
we find that

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}=\frac{i}{4}\gamma_{\mu}{{}^{\alpha\beta}}\Big(D_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\beta}+{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\beta}{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}+\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha},{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\beta}\}\Big).

(4.21)

Representation preserving constraint

Now we move on to the second kind of constraint. These constraints are motivated by integrability conditions for so-called gauge-chiral and gauge-antichiral superfields
\Phi
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\Phi}}
which are constrained by

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}\Phi

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\Phi}}

\displaystyle=0.

(4.22)

The representation preserving constraints are now given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=0.

(4.23)

Indeed, if these weren’t vanishing and one would take covariant derivatives of the equations above arive at conditions which overconstrain the gauge-chiral and gauge-antichiral multiplets
\Phi
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\Phi}}

[
3
]
.

4.2.3
Bianchi Identities

We will now go over Bianchi identities as they were described in equation
4.6
. These will result in some new information because the constraints we imposed weren’t taking account in any way with the superconnection origins of the superfield strengths
[
21
]
.

•

\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\gamma}
and
\nabla_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\beta}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\gamma}
Bianchi identities: These respectively yield

\displaystyle\nabla_{(\alpha}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}\varepsilon_{\beta)\gamma}

\displaystyle=+2i{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu(\alpha}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\beta)\gamma}}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}

\displaystyle=+\frac{2i}{3}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\beta}

(4.24)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{(\alpha}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}\varepsilon_{\beta)\gamma}

\displaystyle=-2i{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu(\alpha}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\beta)\gamma}}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\alpha}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2i}{3}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\beta}

(4.25)

Using these equations as well as the Fierz identity
A.46
one then finds explicit expressions

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\alpha,\beta\gamma}

\displaystyle:={\mathcal{F}}_{\alpha\mu}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\beta\gamma}}=+\frac{3i}{4}\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\gamma}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}+\frac{i}{2}\nabla_{(\alpha}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}\varepsilon_{\beta)\gamma}

(4.26)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha,\beta\gamma}

\displaystyle:={\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\mu}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\beta\gamma}}=-\frac{3i}{4}\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\gamma}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}-\frac{i}{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{(\alpha}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}\varepsilon_{\beta)\gamma}

(4.27)

•

\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}
and
\nabla_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\beta}
Bianchi identities: These respectively yield

\displaystyle\nabla_{(\alpha}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta)\mu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{Bianchi sst}}{\Longrightarrow}

\displaystyle\nabla^{\alpha}\nabla_{\alpha}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}

\displaystyle=0

(4.28)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{(\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\beta)\mu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{Bianchi stt}}{\Longrightarrow}

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}{{}^{\alpha}}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\alpha}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}

\displaystyle=0

(4.29)

•

\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\beta}
Bianchi identity: This identity yields

2i\gamma^{\nu}{{}_{\alpha\beta}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}+\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\mu}{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}={\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\beta}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\alpha}+\nabla_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\beta}

(4.30)

Tracing over
αβ
and contracting with the Levi-Civita tensor then yields

\displaystyle 4i{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}={\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}{{}^{\alpha}}\gamma_{\nu\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\beta}+\nabla{{}^{\alpha}}\gamma_{\nu\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\beta}=:{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}\gamma_{\nu}{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu}+\nabla\gamma_{\nu}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu}\Big.

(4.31)

\displaystyle\Updownarrow\Big.

\displaystyle 8i{\ast}{\mathcal{F}}^{\mu}=\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\Big({\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}\gamma_{\sigma}{\mathcal{F}}_{\rho}+\nabla\gamma_{\sigma}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\rho}\Big).\Big.

(4.32)

Contracting
4.32
with
\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha\beta}}
and using equation
A.50
as well as expressions
4.26
and
4.27
finally results in

\displaystyle{\ast}{\cancel{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{16}[{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{(\alpha},\nabla_{\beta)}]{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\ref{Lemma symmetric}}}{\Leftrightarrow}

\displaystyle{\ast}{\mathcal{F}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{32}\gamma^{\mu\alpha\beta}[{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\alpha},\nabla_{\beta}]{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}.

(4.33)

On the other hand, contracting
μν
in
4.31
and using previous Bianchi identities
4.24
and
4.25
results in

\big(\nabla^{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\alpha}-{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}{{}^{\alpha}}\nabla_{\alpha}\big){\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}=0.

(4.34)

These sum up the most interesting constraints placed on
{\accentset{\circ}{\mathcal{F}}}
by using the Bianchi identities.

4.2.4
Solving the Constraints

We now wish to formulate an explicit solution to the constraints placed on the connection. To this end we recall that in the case of gauge theories a flat connection —that is, a vanishing field strength— corresponds to what’s called a ‘pure gauge’ connection 1-form

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\exists\theta:A

\displaystyle=e^{-\theta}{\mathrm{d}}e^{\theta}

(4.35)

which can obviously always be gauged away by choosing a gauge transformation with parameter
\theta

[
14
]
. In the spirit of this fact we choose to write the spinorial superconnection components
{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\alpha}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=e^{-2{\mathcal{V}}}D_{\alpha}e^{2{\mathcal{V}}}

(4.36)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{F}}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=e^{-2{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}{\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\alpha}e^{2{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}

(4.37)

which is referred to as a
spinorially flat connection
.
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}
are
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued superfields which we refer to as the
prepotentials

[
3
]
[
21
]
. One could now wonder whether this means that the superconnection is flat and thus contains no physical degrees of freedom. However it isn’t since
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}
aren’t generally the same and thus can’t be generally gauged away simultaneously.

Pregauge and supergauge transformations

Now we move on to describe how gauge symmetries work in this propotential formalism, and particularly how the prepotentials transform under gauge transformations. Due to the graded Leibniz property of the spinorial covariant derivatives we find that the prepotentials transform under supergauge transformations as

\displaystyle e^{2{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle\to e^{2{\mathcal{V}}}e^{-X}

\displaystyle e^{2{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}

\displaystyle\to e^{2{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}e^{-X}

(4.38)

More concretely using the BCH formulas outlined in the appendix section
A.1.4
we find that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{2}(2{\mathcal{V}})\star(-X)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{2}(2{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}})\star(-X)

(4.39)

as defined in equation
A.33
of the appendix.
3
3

3

The five-pointed star
\star
of the BCH formula is not to be confused with the six-pointed star
\ast
of the Hodge operator.
Following equation
A.36
we find infinitesimal supergauge transformations

\displaystyle\delta_{X}{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}{1-e^{-2\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}}X,

\displaystyle\delta_{X}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\operatorname{ad}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}}{1-e^{-2\operatorname{ad}{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}}X.

(4.40)

Another kind of ‘gauge’ symmetry is one which arises as a redundancy of the way we write down the spinorially flat solution to the constraints we imposed. Namely, we note that the spinorial superconnections
{\mathcal{A}}_{\alpha}
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\alpha}
are left invariant under transformations

\displaystyle e^{2{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle\to e^{\tilde{\Lambda}}e^{2{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{2}\big({\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}\star(2{\mathcal{V}})\big)

(4.41)

\displaystyle e^{2{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}

\displaystyle\to e^{\Lambda}e^{2{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{2}\big(\Lambda\star(2{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}})\big)

(4.42)

where
\Lambda
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}
are respectively taken to be
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued chiral and antichiral superfields. These transformations are referred to as
pregauge transformations
. Similarly one finds following the results of equation
A.36
that the pregauge transformations are infinitesimally given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\tilde{\Lambda}}{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}{1-e^{2\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}},

\displaystyle\delta_{\Lambda}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\operatorname{ad}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}}{1-e^{2\operatorname{ad}{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}}\Lambda.

(4.43)

In summary, we find that:

Gauge transformations in the prepotential formalism

The prepotentials
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}
contain gauge transformations

\displaystyle e^{2{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle\to e^{\tilde{\Lambda}}e^{2{\mathcal{V}}}e^{-X}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{2}\big({\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}\star(2{\mathcal{V}})\star(-X)\big)

(4.44)

\displaystyle e^{2{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}

\displaystyle\to e^{\Lambda}e^{2{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}}e^{-X}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{2}\big(\Lambda\star(2{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}})\star(-X)\big)

(4.45)
