infinitesimally given by

\displaystyle\delta_{X,{\tilde{\Lambda}}}{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}{1-e^{-2\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}}X+\frac{-\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}{1-e^{2\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}

(4.46)

\displaystyle\delta_{X,\Lambda}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\operatorname{ad}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}}{1-e^{-2\operatorname{ad}{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}}X+\frac{-\operatorname{ad}{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}}{1-e^{2\operatorname{ad}{\tilde{\mathcal{V}}}}}\Lambda

(4.47)

where
X
is a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued general superfield parameter for the supergauge transformations and
{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}
and
\Lambda
are respectively
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued antichiral and chiral superfield parameters of the pregauge transformations.

4.2.5
Wess-Zumino Gauge

We now move on to make a supergauge choice. This kind of gauge fixing is different from the kind we’re used to in gauge theory. Usually when we think of gauge fixing this corresponds to imposing constraints on the field components of the connection 1-form. However, in the case of supergauge fixing the components which will be constrained will be the component fields of the supermultiplet.

The chiral representation

We start off with a gauge consition which is generally preserved by supersymmetry: We note that by supergauge transformations we have enough freedom to set one of the spinorial gauge superfields to zero. Choosing
X=2{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}
we set

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\nabla}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{D}}_{\alpha},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{4}{\accentset{\sim}{D}}\gamma_{\mu}{\mathcal{A}}.

(4.48)

However taking into account the pregauge transformation freedom of
{\accentset{\sim}{\mathcal{V}}}
we see that this doesn’t fully use all the freedom of
X
. We’re left with the freedom to take
X
to be chiral. That is, the residual gauge symmetries of
{\mathcal{V}}
are

\displaystyle e^{2{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle\to e^{\tilde{\Lambda}}e^{2{\mathcal{V}}}e^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{2}\big({\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}\star(2{\mathcal{V}})\star\Lambda\big)

(4.49)

where
\Lambda
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}
are taken to be respectively
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued chiral and antichiral superfields. This is the so-called chiral representation.

Wess-Zumino gauge

As it turns out, these residual supergauge transformations are in their turn enough to set to zero the chiral and the antichiral components of
{\mathcal{V}}
. It follows then that we are left with

{\mathcal{V}}=-\theta(i{\not{A}}+\sigma){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+i\lambda\theta{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}\theta^{2}-\frac{1}{2}D\theta^{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}.

(4.50)

We hence have the following field content (which are all
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued):

•

A real 1-form
A_{\mu}
of dimension 1

•

A real scalar
\sigma
of dimension 1

•

Two
independent
complex spinors
\lambda
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}
of dimensions
\frac{3}{2}

•

A real scalar
D
of dimension 2

One could now wonder wether taking the bosonic field content to be real is consistent with the supersymmetry transformations. This issue was addressed in
[
22
]
[
23
]
. There the action is understood as analytically continued to the space of complexified fields. The path integral is then understood as integrating a holomorphic functional over a half-dimensional contour in complexified field space.

4.2.6
Residual Gauge Symmetries

We have now derived the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued component fields for the vector multiplet. However, we haven’t yet derived if and how their transformational properties relate to those of non-supersymmetric gauge theories.
As it turns out, these are related to the residual gauge symmetries of the Wess-Zumino gauge, which only partially fixes the gauge
[
3
]
[
24
]
. We note that the choices of
\Lambda
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}
in equation
4.49
which preserve the Wess-Zumino gauge
4.50
are parametrised by a real
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued local parameter
\vartheta
(
not
a superfield) and given by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\text{res}}(\vartheta)

\displaystyle=e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}(+\vartheta)=+\vartheta-i\theta({\not{{\partial}}}\vartheta){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}-\frac{1}{4}\square\vartheta\theta^{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}

(4.51)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}_{\text{res}}(\vartheta)

\displaystyle=e^{-i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}(-\vartheta)=-\vartheta-i\theta({\not{{\partial}}}\vartheta){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+\frac{1}{4}\square\vartheta\theta^{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}

(4.52)

One finds that the prepotential
{\mathcal{V}}
transforms under these residual gauge transformations as

\begin{split}\delta_{\vartheta}{\mathcal{V}}&=\frac{\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}{1-e^{-2\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}}\Lambda_{\text{res}}(\vartheta)+\frac{-\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}{1-e^{2\operatorname{ad}{\mathcal{V}}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}_{\text{res}}(\vartheta)\\
&\overset{\text{\ref{BCH infinitesimal expansion}}}{=}-\frac{1}{2}\big({\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}_{\text{res}}(\vartheta)+\Lambda_{\text{res}}(\vartheta)\big)+\frac{1}{2}\big[{\mathcal{V}},~{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}_{\text{res}}(\vartheta)-\Lambda_{\text{res}}(\vartheta)\big]\end{split}

(4.53)

where higher order terms vanish identically due to the anticommuting nature of Grassmann numbers. Working this out we find that

\begin{split}\delta_{\vartheta}{\mathcal{V}}&=-\theta\Big(-i({\not{{\partial}}}+\operatorname{ad}{\not{A}})\vartheta+[\vartheta,\sigma]\Big){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+i[\vartheta,\lambda]\theta{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}+i[\vartheta,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}]{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}\theta^{2}-\frac{1}{2}[\vartheta,D]\theta^{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}\\
&=:-\theta(i\delta_{\vartheta}{\not{A}}+\delta_{\vartheta}\sigma){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+i\delta_{\vartheta}\lambda\theta{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}+i\delta_{\vartheta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}\theta^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\vartheta}D\theta^{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}\end{split}

(4.54)

In conclusion, we find that

Residual gauge symmetries of the Wess-Zumino gauge

\displaystyle\delta_{\vartheta}A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\vartheta

\displaystyle\delta_{\vartheta}\sigma

\displaystyle=[\vartheta,\sigma]

\displaystyle\delta_{\vartheta}D

\displaystyle=[\vartheta,D]\Big.

(4.55)

\displaystyle\delta_{\vartheta}\lambda

\displaystyle=[\vartheta,\lambda]

\displaystyle\delta_{\vartheta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=[\vartheta,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}]\Big.

(4.56)

That is, the residual gauge transformations parametrised by
\vartheta
constitute ordinary gauge transformations, for which
A_{\mu}
is a
G
-connection 1-form and
\sigma
,
\lambda
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}
and
D
lie in the adjoint reperesentation of
G
.

4.2.7
Supersymmetries in the WZ Gauge

We now move on to discuss supersymmetry transformations in the Wess-Zumino gauge. These are generated in the same way as for the chiral multiplet, only now we have to include compensating gauge transformations which ensure the Wess-Zumino gauge is upheld. These compensating gauge transfromations can themselves be taken to be residual gauge transformations
4.39
of the chiral representation since this condition is supersymmetric. Hence, we find that supersymmetry transformations are covariantly taken to be of the form

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=\zeta Q+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}+\delta_{\Lambda,{\tilde{\Lambda}}},

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=\Lambda(\zeta,{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}},{\mathcal{V}}),

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}(\zeta,{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}},{\mathcal{V}})

(4.57)

where the gauge symmetry
\delta_{\Lambda,{\tilde{\Lambda}}}
is included to ensure the preservation of the Wess-Zumino gauge.

Compensating gauge transformations

We start off by working out the compensating gauge transformations of the Wess-Zumino gauge. To this end we start off by noting that the out of gauge terms are given by

\displaystyle(\zeta Q){\mathcal{V}}\big|_{\text{out of gauge}}

\displaystyle=-\zeta(+i{\not{A}}+\sigma){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+i(\zeta\lambda){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}

(4.58)

\displaystyle({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}){\mathcal{V}}\big|_{\text{out of gauge}}

\displaystyle=-{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}(-i{\not{A}}+\sigma)\theta+i({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})\theta^{2}

(4.59)

From this we can read off that the parameters for the compensating gauge transformations are given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}&=2e^{+i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\Big[-\zeta(+i{\not{A}}+\sigma){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+i(\zeta\lambda){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}\Big]\Big.\\
&=-2\zeta(+i{\not{A}}+\sigma){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+2i(\zeta\lambda){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}+i\zeta(+i{\not{A}}+\sigma){\accentset{\leftarrow}{{\not{{\partial}}}}}\theta{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}\Big.\end{aligned}

(4.60)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Lambda&=2e^{-i{\tilde{\theta}}{\not{{\partial}}}\theta}\Big[-{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}(-i{\not{A}}+\sigma)\theta+i({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})\theta^{2}\Big]\Big.\\
&=-2{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}(-i{\not{A}}+\sigma)\theta+2i({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})\theta^{2}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}(-i{\not{A}}+\sigma){\accentset{\leftarrow}{{\not{{\partial}}}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}\theta^{2}\Big.\end{aligned}

(4.61)

In analogy to the case of the residual gauge symmetries we find that the compensating gauge transformations simplify to

\delta_{\Lambda,{\tilde{\Lambda}}}{\mathcal{V}}=-\frac{1}{2}\big(\Lambda+{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\big[\Lambda-{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}},~{\mathcal{V}}\big]

(4.62)

again due to the anticommuting nature of Grassmann numbers.

Computing the SUSY transformations

We are now ready to compute the gauge covariant supersymmetry transformations. Inserting the aforementioned results we find (after some tedious computations) that

\begin{split}\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\mathcal{V}}&=\big(\zeta Q+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}+\delta_{\Lambda,{\tilde{\Lambda}}}\big){\mathcal{V}}\\
&=\big(\zeta Q-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{ad}{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}\big){\mathcal{V}}-\frac{1}{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\Lambda}}+\big({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{Q}}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{ad}\Lambda\big){\mathcal{V}}-\frac{1}{2}\Lambda\\
&=\begin{aligned} &+\theta\bigg[i\big(-\zeta\gamma_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\gamma_{\mu}\lambda\big)+\big(i\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\lambda\big)\bigg]{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}\\
&+i\zeta\bigg[iD-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\bigg]\theta{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\bigg[iD+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\bigg]{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}\theta^{2}\\
&-\frac{i}{2}\zeta\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}+\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}-\frac{i}{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big)\lambda\end{aligned}\\
&=:+\theta\Big(i\gamma^{\mu}\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}A_{\mu}+\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\sigma\Big){\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}+i\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\lambda\theta{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}+i\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}\theta^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}D\theta^{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\theta}}{{}^{2}}\end{split}

(4.63)

From this, we conclude:

Supersymmetry transformations of the WZ gauge vector multiplet

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\zeta\gamma_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\gamma_{\mu}\lambda\bigg.

(4.64)

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\sigma

\displaystyle=i\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\lambda\bigg.

(4.65)

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}\lambda

\displaystyle=\zeta\bigg[iD+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\bigg]\bigg.

(4.66)

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\bigg[iD-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\bigg]\bigg.

(4.67)

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}D

\displaystyle=i\zeta\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}+\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big)\lambda\bigg.

(4.68)

the gauge covariantised supersymmetry transformations, where henceforth we shall denote

\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}=\delta_{\zeta}+{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}

(4.69)

as this will become convenient notation for localisations.

4.2.8
Commentary on the Algebra

Let us comment a bit on this algebra. We recall that the supersymmetry algebra is characterised by the anticommutation relation

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}

\displaystyle=-2i(\zeta\gamma^{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}){\partial}_{\mu}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\text{SUSY}^{2}

\displaystyle=\text{transl}

(4.70)

That is, supersymmetry transformations square to translations. However, this doesn’t take into account the fact that the Wess-Zumino gauge is not supersymmetric and has to be complemented by gauge transformations to stay in this gauge. Taking this into account the algebra takes on the form

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}=-2iK^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}+\delta_{\text{gauge}}(-2iK^{\mu}A_{\mu}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\sigma)

(4.71)

\displaystyle\Updownarrow

\displaystyle\text{SUSY}^{2}=\text{transl}+\text{gauge}

(4.72)

where we defined the Killing vector
K^{\mu}=\zeta\gamma^{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}
. That is, the algebra now becomes accompanied by a field dependent gauge transformation. Indeed, a direct computation yields generalisations of the nilpotency

\{\delta_{\zeta},\delta_{\eta}\}=\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\eta}}\}=0

(4.73)

as well as non-trivial relations

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\nu}F_{\nu\mu}+2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma

(4.74)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\sigma

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma

(4.75)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\lambda^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\lambda^{\alpha}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,\lambda^{\alpha}]

(4.76)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}]

(4.77)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}D

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}D-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,D]

(4.78)

in agreement with the algebra
4.71
.

An aside on odd derivatives

A source of some confusion could the question of whether to take the (Grassmann-odd!) supersymmetry transformation
\delta
to be a left or a right derivative. As it turns out despite us using left derivatives it are right derivatives which are more fundamental
[
25
]
. However, the difference will be at most an overall minus sign if the object acted on isn’t the sum of objects with differing Grassmann parities. Hence, for our purposes the distinction won’t be relevant. A way to translate between left and right acting derivatives is through

\displaystyle(\text{even}){\accentset{\leftarrow}{\delta}}

\displaystyle=+\delta(\text{even}),

\displaystyle(\text{odd}){\accentset{\leftarrow}{\delta}}

\displaystyle=-\delta(\text{odd}).

(4.79)

4.3
Supersymmetric Actions and Localisation

In this section we’ll go over two kinds of supersymmetric gauge theories: super-Yang-Mills theory and super-Chern-Simons theory. Particularly, as it turns out, it is in super-Yang-Mills theory that we find the clue to localising supersymmetric gauge theories.

4.3.1
Super-Yang-Mills Theory

We start off by giving a description of super-Yang-Mills theory. We start off by noting a very interesting and relevant fact to this thesis. The action of super-Yang-Mills theory is
Q
-exact
[
7
]
[
8
]
! The action of super-Yang-Mills theory is given by

S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big)\lambda\bigg]

(4.80)

where
g^{2}
a constant of mass dimension 1 and where we used
{\mathcal{V}}
to denote the collective field content of the vector multiplet in WZ gauge. Showing this action is supersymmetric will be done through its
Q
-exactness. Particularly, we note that this action can be written in different ways as

\displaystyle g^{2}S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\delta_{\zeta}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda\Big]\bigg.

(4.81)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[({\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\Big]\bigg.

(4.82)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4|\zeta|^{2}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda+({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\bigg]\bigg.

(4.83)

where we defined

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}:=\delta_{\zeta}+{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}

(4.84)

where now we take the two conjugate SUSY trasformations but with the same non-zero constant bosonic spinor
\zeta
. For conventions regarding the norm
|\bullet|^{2}
and Hermitian conjugation as it relates to the index formalism for spinors we refer the reader to section
A.2.3
of the appendix. Let us verify this fact a little more explicitly:

Expressions
4.81
and
4.82

We start off by taking a look at the first description, that is, expression
4.81
. We find that

g^{2}S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[|\delta_{\zeta}\lambda|^{2}+\delta_{\zeta}(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda\bigg]

(4.85)

This gives us respectively bosonic and fermionic sectors

\displaystyle g^{2}S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{bos}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}|\delta_{\zeta}\lambda|^{2},

(4.86)

\displaystyle g^{2}S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{fer}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\delta_{\zeta}(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda

(4.87)

Working out the bosonic part in some closer detail will be important since due to it’s exactness it will function as a localising term for which the bosonic part will be responsible for the Gaussian damping central to localisations. Noting that

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta}\lambda

\displaystyle=\Big[iD-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}-{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\Big]\zeta

(4.88)

\displaystyle(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle={\zeta^{\dagger}}\Big[iD+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\Big]

(4.89)

we find that

\begin{split}\operatorname{tr}|\delta_{\zeta}\lambda|^{2}&=\operatorname{tr}{\zeta^{\dagger}}\bigg[-D^{2}-\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\Big)^{2}\bigg]\zeta\\
&=|\zeta|^{2}\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-D^{2}-\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\rho\sigma}F^{\rho\sigma}+{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}+{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma\Big)\bigg]\\
&=|\zeta|^{2}\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-D^{2}-\frac{1}{2}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma-{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\big(\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}\sigma\big)\bigg]\end{split}

(4.90)

where we used the Bianchi identity
{\mathcal{D}}F\equiv 0
to turn the final term into a total derivative. This yields a bosonic sector

S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{bos}}=\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}\bigg]

(4.91)

which is indeed in agreement with super-Yang-Mills action
4.80
. Doing the fermionic sector is straightforward but tedious and not particularly insightful to any future discussion of the localisation of the Third Way Theory. Hence we will simply give the answer which is

S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{fer}}=\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big)\lambda\bigg]

(4.92)

also in agreement with the super-Yang-Mills action
4.80
. This verifies that the expression
4.81
agrees with the super-Yang-Mills action
4.80
. The agreement of expresion
4.82
follows similarly.

Expression
4.83

As for the third expression for the super-Yang-Mills action, equation
4.83
, there are a couple of interesting remarks to make which will become relevant later on when looking at the Third Way Theory. For this expression we find that

g^{2}S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=g^{2}S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{bos}}+g^{2}S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{fer}}

(4.93)

with bosonic and fermionic sectors

\displaystyle g^{2}S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{bos}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[|{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda|^{2}+|{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}|^{2}\bigg]

(4.94)

\displaystyle g^{2}S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{fer}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda+{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\bigg]

(4.95)

In close analogy to the computations for expression
4.81
we find for the bosonic sector that

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}|{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda|^{2}

\displaystyle=|\zeta|^{2}\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-D^{2}-\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\rho\sigma}F^{\rho\sigma}+{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}+{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma\Big)\bigg]

(4.96)

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}|{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}|^{2}

\displaystyle=|\zeta|^{2}\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-D^{2}-\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\rho\sigma}F^{\rho\sigma}-{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}-{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma\Big)\bigg]

(4.97)

where the reader is encouraged to note the relative minus signs between the two expressions. Because of these relative minus signs one finds that

\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\Big[|{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda|^{2}+|{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}|^{2}\Big]=\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-D^{2}-\frac{1}{2}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma\bigg]

(4.98)

without invoking the Bianchi identities!
Now, one may wonder why this fact could possibly be useful, since it just yields the same result as previous cases modulo a total derivative. The reason that this is interesting is that the localisation of the Third Way Theory will be attempted through a deformation of the supersymmetry transformations. With these deformations it will be the case that we can’t use the Bianchi identities as we did for expressions
4.81
and
4.82
, and hence an analogue of expression
4.83
will be our starting point for localising the Third Way Theory.

To continue our discussion, we find that

S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{bos}}=\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}\bigg]

(4.99)

nicely in agreement with the super-Yang-Mills action
4.80
and without having to care about boundary terms. As for the fermionic sector, we start off by noting that

\displaystyle(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle={\zeta^{\dagger}}\Big[iD+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\Big]=\delta_{\zeta^{\dagger}}\lambda

(4.100)

with in analogy following

\displaystyle(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\zeta^{\dagger}}\lambda,

\displaystyle({\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta^{\dagger}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}},

\displaystyle({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta^{\dagger}}\lambda,

\displaystyle({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta^{\dagger}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}.

(4.101)

We thus find that

\begin{split}&{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda+{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\\
&=(\delta_{\zeta}+{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta})\delta_{\zeta^{\dagger}}\lambda\lambda+(\delta_{\zeta}+{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}){\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta^{\dagger}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\\
&=\big(\delta_{\zeta}\delta_{\zeta^{\dagger}}\lambda\big)\lambda+\big(\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta},\delta_{\zeta^{\dagger}}\}\lambda\big)\lambda+\big({\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta^{\dagger}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+\big(\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta^{\dagger}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\\
&=\underbrace{\Big[\delta_{\zeta}(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda+{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}({\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\Big]}_{\text{compare to \ref{SYM exact 1} and \ref{SYM exact 2}}}+\underbrace{\Big[\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta},\delta_{\zeta^{\dagger}}\}(\lambda^{2})+\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta^{\dagger}}\}({\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{2}})\Big]}_{\text{transl + gauge}}\end{split}

(4.102)

Recalling the algebra as well as the results from expressions
4.81
and
4.82
as well as the fact that
\int\operatorname{tr}(\text{transl + gauge})=0
we find that

S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{fer}}=\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big)\lambda\bigg]

(4.103)

again nicely agreeing with the super-Yang-Mills action
4.80
.

Supersymmetry of SYM theory

It now follows almost trivially that the super-Yang-Mills action is invariant under supersymmetry. Indeed, we find that

\displaystyle g^{2}\delta_{\zeta}S_{\text{SYM}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\delta^{2}_{\zeta}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda\Big]=0

(4.104)

\displaystyle g^{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}S_{\text{SYM}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}{{}^{2}_{\zeta}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[({\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\Big]=0

(4.105)

proving that super-Yang-Mills theory is indeed supersymmetric.

4.3.2
Super-Chern-Simons Theory

We now move on to describe the superanalogue of Chern-Simons theory
[
7
]
[
11
]
[
21
]
[
26
]
. Super-Chern-Simons theory at level
k
is described through an action

S^{k}_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{CS}}[A]+\frac{k}{2\pi}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[\lambda{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}-\sigma D\Big].

(4.106)

This theory has a very interesting property which will become relevant when studying the Third Way Theory: Chern-Simons theory and its superanalogue are physically equivalent! Indeed, the additional field content
\sigma
,
\lambda
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}
,
D
can all be integrated out and the dynamics of the connection 1-form are left unchanged. This will be interesting in the case of the Third Way Theory because if it is physically equivalent to its conjectured superanalogue and it can be localised then we’ve achieved essentially a localisation of the Third Way Theory as is without having to change its physics. These kind of circumstance is exceedingly rare and being able to achieve this would prove significant theoretical progress.

Supersymmetry of SCS theory

Let us now verify that this theory is supersymmetric. This is a relatively short computation but we will do so explicitly because this is also instructive to understanding progress made towards localising the Third Way Theory.

Now to perform this computation: Varying this action yields

\delta S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\delta A_{\rho}+\delta\lambda{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+\delta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\lambda-D\delta\sigma-\sigma\delta D\bigg]

(4.107)

We hence find that the different contributions from supersymmetry transformations are given as follows:
We start off by writing out the most complicated contribution to this transformation which comes from the gaugino fields
\lambda
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}
. Here we find that

\begin{multlined}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[\delta\lambda{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+\delta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\lambda\Big]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\zeta\Big(iD+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\Big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\\
+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\Big(iD-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma\Big)\lambda\bigg]\end{multlined}

(4.108)

This consists of three pairs of terms. We find that the
D
terms cancel against

\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[-D\delta\sigma\Big]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[\zeta(-iD){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}(-iD)\lambda\Big].

(4.109)

We furthermore find that the
{\mathcal{D}}\sigma
terms cancel against

\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[-\sigma\delta D\Big]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[-i\sigma\zeta\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}+\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}-i\sigma{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big)\lambda\Big]\\
=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[\zeta(-{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}(-{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma)\lambda\Big]

(4.110)

where we used the Leibniz rule as well as the fact that
[\sigma,\sigma]=0
. Finally we move on to the
F
terms. Interestingly, these cancel because they are proportional to the Chern-Simons field equations. Indeed, one finds that

\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\delta A_{\rho}\bigg]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-\zeta\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}\Big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+{\zeta^{\dagger}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}\Big)\lambda\bigg]

(4.111)

indeed cancelling against the
F
terms in the variation of the gauginos. We this conclude that

\delta_{\zeta}S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]={\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=0.

(4.112)

That is, super-Chern-Simons theory is indeed supersymmetric.

4.3.3
Localisation of SYM and SCS Theory

Let us now move on to describe the way supersymmetric gauge theories are localised. As already mentioned, this will be done through the super-Yang-Mills action. Particularly, through the observation that expectation value of BPS operators in a SYM theory is indepedent on the coupling strength
g^{2}
of the theory. As such, we can take the limit
t:=g^{-2}\to\infty
localising the theory. Here we will discuss two differing localisation schemes and discuss their equivalence and their meaning in the context of the Third Way Theory.

\delta
-localisation scheme

This localisation scheme will be centered around the localising supersymmetry
\delta_{\zeta}
(the
{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}
case follows similarly). To rephrase in the language of section
1.4.3
, we have a fermionic functional given by

{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda\Big].

(4.113)

The corresponding localising action is then given by

tS_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=t\delta_{\zeta}{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{t=g^{-2}}.

(4.114)

The BPS operators then consist of operators which are annihilated by
\delta_{\zeta}
and the BPS configurations on which the path integral localises are given by

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=0

(4.115)

all in accordance to the discussion of section
1.4.3
. For this localisation argument to make sense we need the fermionic localising functional to be annihilated by
\delta^{2}_{\zeta}
. However we recall from our discussion in section
4.2.8
that

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\{\delta_{\zeta},\delta_{\zeta}\}\equiv 0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\delta^{2}_{\zeta}{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]

\displaystyle=0

(4.116)

thus verifying that this is indeed the case.

{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation scheme

We now move on to the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation scheme which is based on the localising supersymmetry transformation
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}
. In this case the fermionic localising functional is given by

{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\frac{1}{4|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda+({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\Big]

(4.117)

which again yields a localising action

tS_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=t{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=S_{\text{SYM}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{t=g^{-2}}.

(4.118)

In this scheme the BPS operators will consist of those annihilated by
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}
and the localisation locus will again consist of BPS configurations

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=0.

(4.119)

Most seems to be the same for this localisation argument compared to the previous case. However, now the condition for this localisation argument to make sense is somewhat different. Now, we need the fermionic localising functional to be annihilated by
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}^{2}
. In accordance to our discussion in previous sections we recall that this is indeed the case since

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta},{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\}=-2iK^{\mu}{\partial}_{\mu}+\delta_{\text{gauge}}(-2iK^{\mu}A_{\mu})

(4.120)

where we recall that we defined the Killing vector
K^{\mu}=\zeta\gamma^{\mu}\zeta
as a spinor bilinear from the Killing spinor
\zeta
. From this it follows that

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}^{2}{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}^{2}\big\{\dots\big\}\Big]=0.

(4.121)

We thus find that the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation scheme also works.

Comparison between the two schemes

Let us now compare these two schemes. We note that

•

The
\delta
-localisation scheme uses the Bianchi identity while the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation scheme doesn’t. If one considers different supersymmetry algebras (e.g. include a central charge) the Bianchi identity might not be sufficient anymore to seperate the
A_{\mu}
and
\sigma
loci. Furthermore if this isn’t the case anymore the resulting locus might not be on-shell anymore.

•

The
\delta
-localisation scheme uses the nilpotency of
\delta_{\zeta}
while the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-scheme uses the fact that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}^{2}=\text{transl}+\text{gauge}
. If one changes to algebra the nilpotency remains generically unaffected while the nontrivial relation becomes augmented, possibly in a way which could obstruct the localisation argument.

We hence find that both schemes in more general contexts have strong and weak points. For more general supersymmetry representations the
\delta
-localisation scheme is more likely to work but might localise to looser, possibly off-shell configurations. On the other hand, the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation scheme usually will localise on-shell but has stronger requirements to work. In more general contexts —particularly the Third Way Theory— these issues will become glaringly clear.

4.4
Proca-Chern-Simons Theory

4.4.1
Non-Supersymmetric Formulation

Before moving on to discussing how to localise the Third Way Theory, we will discuss a toy model, namely a model which we coin Proca-Chern-Simons theory. As the name suggests, this theory is simply given by combining a Proca term with Chern-Simons theory. That is, we consider a theory given by an action

Proca-Chern-Simons Theory

S_{\text{PCS}}[A]=S_{\text{CS}}[A]-\int\operatorname{tr}\Big[\frac{m}{2}A\ast A\Big]

(4.122)

with
A
a
G
-connection 1-form, where for simplicity we assume Euclidean signature, and where
m
is a parameter of mass dimension 1. This theory has field equations

F-m\ast A=0.

(4.123)

As is generally the case the case for Proca terms, they break gauge symmetry. The gauge group is broken down to

G\to\{1\}.

(4.124)

Let us now take a closer look at the field equations
4.123
of this theory. Taking the covariant exterior derivative of these field equations one finds

0={\mathcal{D}}\big(F-m\ast A\big)\equiv-m{\mathrm{d}}\ast A

(4.125)

where we used the Bianchi identity as well as the fact that
[A,\ast A]=0
. This is very interesting, because the Lorenz gauge
{\mathrm{d}}\ast A=0
is implied by the field equations themselves. This can be regarded as a consequence of the fact that the Proca mass term breaks gauge invariance.

4.4.2
Super-Proca-Chern-Simons Theory

Let us now supersymmetrise this theory. The way we supersymmetrise this theory will be by deforming the supersymmetry transformations of super-Chern-Simons theory in such a way that we’re
forced
to include a mass term. That is to say, in strong analogy to the action
4.106
of super-Chern-Simons theory we have:

Super-Proca-Chern-Simons Action

S_{\text{SPCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]-\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{m}{2}A\ast A+\frac{m}{2}\sigma\ast\sigma\bigg]\\
=S_{\text{CS}}[A]+\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\lambda{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}-\sigma D-\frac{m}{2}A_{\mu}A^{\mu}-\frac{m}{2}\sigma^{2}\bigg]

(4.126)

where
{\mathcal{V}}=(A_{\mu},\sigma,\lambda,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}},D)
denotes the field content of the WZ gauge vector multiplet. The field equations are given by

\displaystyle F-m\ast A

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle D+m\sigma

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=0.

(4.127)

The supersymmetries of this theory are deformed supersymmetry transformations of the vector multiplet
{\mathcal{V}}
, chosen to satisfy three purposes:

•

supersymmetrise Proca-Chern-Simons theory,

•

yield nilpotent supersymmetry transformations, i.e.
\delta_{\zeta}^{2}={\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}{{}^{2}_{\tilde{\zeta}}}=0
,

•

localise Proca-Chern-Simons theory to on-shell field configurations.

The supersymmetry transformations we arrived at are then given by:

Massive Supersymmetry Transformations

\displaystyle\delta A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\zeta\gamma_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\gamma_{\mu}\lambda\bigg.

(4.128)

\displaystyle\delta\sigma

\displaystyle=i\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\lambda\bigg.

(4.129)

\displaystyle\delta\lambda

\displaystyle=\zeta\bigg[iD+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-m{\not{A}}+im\sigma}\bigg]\bigg.

(4.130)

\displaystyle\delta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\bigg[iD-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+m{\not{A}}+im\sigma}\bigg]\bigg.

(4.131)

\displaystyle\delta D

\displaystyle=i\zeta\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}+\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big)\lambda\bigg.

(4.132)

where the highlighted terms are the ‘deformations’ of the usual supersymmetry transformations
4.64
-
4.68
. Note that these are proportional to the mass parameter
m
of Proca-Chern-Simons theory. Again we denote

\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}}}=\delta_{\zeta}+{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}

(4.133)

from now on.

The supersymmetry of the action follows in complete analogy to the computations
4.107
-
4.111
. To highlight why this action still is supersymmetric, we note that the new contributions to
4.108
are countered by the SUSY transformations of the mass terms in the SPCS action:

\begin{split}\delta S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{new}}}&=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[\delta\lambda{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+\delta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\lambda\Big]\Big|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{new}}}\\
&=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[\zeta\big(-m{\not{A}}+im\sigma\big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\big(m{\not{A}}+im\sigma\big)\lambda\Big]\\
&=-\delta\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-\frac{m}{2}A\ast A-\frac{m}{2}\sigma\ast\sigma\bigg].\end{split}

(4.134)

As for the nilpotency of the supersymmetry transformations, this follows from the results in section
4.2.8
as well as the observation that

\delta(i{\not{A}}\pm\sigma)_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}=\begin{cases}-2i\zeta_{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\beta}}+2i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{{}^{\beta}}\lambda_{\alpha}&\uparrow\\
-2i\zeta^{\beta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}_{\alpha}+2i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}_{\alpha}\lambda^{\beta}&\downarrow\end{cases}

(4.135)

which follows as a direct consequence of the Fierz identity
A.46
. The nilpotency of the SUSY transformations on
A_{\mu}
,
\sigma
and
D
follows directly from previous results and for the gauginos
\lambda
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}
we find

\displaystyle\delta^{2}_{\zeta}\lambda^{\alpha}\big|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{new}}}

\displaystyle=+im\zeta^{\beta}\delta_{\zeta}(i{\not{A}}+\sigma)_{\beta}{{}^{\alpha}}\overset{\text{\ref{tussenstap 3}}}{=}2m\zeta^{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}=0

(4.136)

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}{{}^{2}_{\tilde{\zeta}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}\big|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{new}}}

\displaystyle=-im{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{{}^{\beta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}(i{\not{A}}-\sigma)_{\beta}{{}^{\alpha}}\overset{\text{\ref{tussenstap 3}}}{=}2m{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{{}^{2}}\lambda^{\alpha}=0

(4.137)

proving that indeed

\delta^{2}_{\zeta}={\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}{{}^{2}_{\tilde{\zeta}}}=0

(4.138)

the massive SUSY transformations are nilpotent.

4.4.3
The Algebra of Massive SUSY

Let us now more closely work out the algebra associated to these massive SUSY transformations. A straightforward if not somewhat tedious computation yields

\{\delta_{\zeta},\delta_{\eta}\}=\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\eta}}\}=0.

(4.139)

As for the remaining anticommutators, we find

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\nu}(F_{\nu\mu}-m\epsilon_{\nu\mu\rho}A^{\rho})+2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma

(4.140)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\sigma

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma

(4.141)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\lambda^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\lambda^{\alpha}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,\lambda^{\alpha}]-2m{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{{}^{\alpha}}(\zeta\lambda)

(4.142)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}]-2m\zeta^{\alpha}({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})

(4.143)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}D

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}(D+im\sigma)-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,D]+2m\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\partial}_{\mu}A^{\mu}

(4.144)

Interestingly, the algebra now takes on the form

\text{SUSY}^{2}=\text{transl}+\text{gauge}+\text{mass}

(4.145)

where ‘mass’ refers to the inclusion of terms proportional to the mass
m
. Interestingly, this
does not
have the interpretation of a central charge. Originally it was attempted to realise the mass term in Proca-Chern-Simons theory as arising through a central charge. However, the relative signs between the new terms in
\delta\lambda
and
\delta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}
in equations
4.130
and
4.131
this can’t be the case (compare to e.g.
[
11
]
). Furthermore, such relative signs to our knowledge wouldn’t allow for the construction of a supersymmetric Proca-Chern-Simons action.

4.4.4
Localisation of Proca-Chern-Simons Theory

In this section we will study the localisation of Proca-Chern-Simons theory. It should be noted that due to the fact that all additional field content of SPCS being auciliary a localisation of PCS theory and SPCS theory would be equivalent.

\delta
-Localisation Scheme

Let us start off by studying the
\delta
-localisation scheme. In this scheme the fermionic localising function will be given by

{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda\Big].

(4.146)

We now work out the corresponding localising action

S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\delta_{\zeta}{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[|\delta_{\zeta}\lambda|^{2}+\delta_{\zeta}(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda\Big].

(4.147)

Starting off with the bosonic part we find in analogy to computation
4.90
that

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}|\delta_{\zeta}\lambda|^{2}&=\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{2}(D+m\sigma)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\rho\sigma}F^{\rho\sigma}-mA_{\mu}+{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma\Big)\\
&\times\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}-mA^{\mu}+{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma\Big)\bigg]\end{aligned}\\
&=\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{2}(D+m\sigma)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma\\
&-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}\sigma-mA^{\mu}\sigma\Big)+m{\partial}_{\mu}A^{\mu}\sigma\bigg].\end{aligned}\end{split}

(4.148)

It is here that our remarks with regards to the comparison of the
\delta
- and
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation schemes become important. Particularly, we find that an obstruction to the seperation of the two field equations in the
\delta
-localisation scheme is proportional to
{\partial}_{\mu}A^{\mu}
. Interestingly, if we had a good reason for assuming a Lorenz gauge
{\partial}_{\mu}A^{\mu}=0
this would seperate the two field equations. We hence arrive at a bosonic localising action

\begin{split}S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{bos}}&=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{4}\Big(F_{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}(A+{\mathcal{D}}\sigma)^{\rho}\Big)\Big(F^{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(A+{\mathcal{D}}\sigma)_{\rho}\Big)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}(D+m\sigma)^{2}\bigg]\end{aligned}\\
&=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{2}(D+m\sigma)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\big(F_{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}A^{\rho}\big)\big(F^{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}A^{\rho}\big)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma+m{\partial}_{\mu}A^{\mu}\sigma\bigg]\end{aligned}\end{split}

(4.149)

As for the fermionic part of the localising action, we find new contributions

\operatorname{tr}\Big[\delta_{\zeta}(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda\Big]\Big|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{new}}}=\operatorname{tr}\bigg[im{\zeta^{\dagger}}{{}^{\beta}}\delta_{\zeta}(i{\not{A}}+\sigma)_{\beta}{{}^{\alpha}}\lambda_{\alpha}\bigg]\overset{\text{\ref{tussenstap 3}}}{=}\operatorname{tr}\Big[2m|\zeta|^{2}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\lambda\Big]

(4.150)

Comparing this to equation
4.92
we hence find find that the fermionic part of the localising functional is given by

S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{fer}}=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma-m\big)\lambda\bigg].

(4.151)

In conclusion, we find that the localising action is given by

\delta
-Localisation of Proca-Chern-Simons Theory

S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{4}\Big(F_{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}A^{\rho}+\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}{\mathcal{D}}^{\rho}\sigma)\Big)\\
&\times\Big(F^{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}A_{\rho}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}{\mathcal{D}}_{\rho}\sigma)\Big)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}(D+m\sigma)^{2}-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma-m\big)\lambda\bigg]\end{aligned}

(4.152)

which yields a localisation locus

\displaystyle D+m\sigma

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle F-m\ast A

\displaystyle=-\ast{\mathcal{D}}\sigma.

(4.153)

localising the action on
sourced
Proca-Chern-Simons
A_{\mu}
field equations as well as the
\sigma
field equations.

We note that for the covariant flatness condition
{\mathcal{D}}\sigma=0
to seperate from the
A_{\mu}
field equations and hence become its own localisation locus the condition
{\partial}_{\mu}A^{\mu}=0
has to be satisfied.
A perhaps meaningful way of looking at this is that this would be the case if Noether’s second theorem were to hold somehow. However, due to the fact that PCS theory violates gauge symmetry it doesn’t have to. That is to say,

\displaystyle\delta_{\theta}S_{\text{PCS}}[A]

\displaystyle~\not{\equiv}~0,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\frac{\delta S_{\text{PCS}}}{\delta A_{\mu}}

\displaystyle~\not{\equiv}~0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\partial}_{\mu}A^{\mu}

\displaystyle~\not{\equiv}~0.

(4.154)

What we note though is that if the gauge field
A_{\mu}
is taken to be on-shell, it does hold:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{\text{PCS}}}{\delta A_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}A^{\rho}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\partial}_{\mu}A^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0.

(4.155)

This is an interesting fact, since terms appearing in supersymmetry which vanish on-shell are often symptomatic of the elimination of auxiliary field content. Perhaps this suggests that we need to introduce additional field content for the Proca-Chern-Simons localisation to make sense off-shell.

{\mathcal{Q}}
-Localisation Scheme

As an attempt to get rid of the terms which appear in the
\delta
-localisation scheme, we will now perform the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation scheme, which isn’t equivalent to the former with these supersymmetry transformations.
In analogy to section
4.3.3
we have a fermionic localising functional

{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\frac{1}{4|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda+({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\Big]

(4.156)

In complete analogy to the computations
4.96
-
4.98
we find

\begin{split}S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{bos}}&=\frac{1}{4|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[|{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda|^{2}+|{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}|^{2}\Big]\\
&=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{4}\big(F_{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}A^{\rho}\big)\big(F^{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}A_{\rho}\big)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma-\frac{1}{2}(D+m\sigma)^{2}\bigg]\end{aligned}\end{split}

(4.157)

which does
not
involve the term proportional to
{\partial}_{\mu}A^{\mu}
. If the total action is then supersymmetric, that is to say,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}S_{\text{loc}}=0
, then the localisation argument makes sense. However, before moving on to this let us discuss the fermionic part of this localising action. In this case the fermionic part of the action
won’t
agree with that of tha
\delta
-localisation scheme. We find after some brief computations that we arrive at a fermionic sector

\begin{split}S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\big|_{\text{fer}}&=\frac{1}{4|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda+{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\Big]\\
&=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma-m\big)\lambda-\frac{m}{2}\big(\lambda^{2}+{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{2}}\big)\bigg].\end{split}

(4.158)

This action interestingly is augmented by non-standard mass terms. We thus arrive at a total localising action

S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{4}\big(F_{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}A^{\rho}\big)\big(F^{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}A_{\rho}\big)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma{\mathcal{D}}^{\mu}\sigma-\frac{1}{2}(D+m\sigma)^{2}\\
&-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma-m\big)\lambda-\frac{m}{2}\big(\lambda^{2}+{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{2}}\big)\bigg].\end{aligned}

(4.159)

If
this action is supersymmetric,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]=0
, this localises on the localisation locus

\displaystyle D+m\sigma

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}-m\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}A^{\rho}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma

\displaystyle=0.

(4.160)

consisting of the
\sigma
field equation,
A_{\mu}
field equation and the
\sigma
field being covariantly constant. However, as it turns out, this action is
not
supersymmetric under
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}
:

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\neq 0.

(4.161)

In conclusion, we find that the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation scheme, which aimed at removing the problems which involve using the Bianchi identities in the derivation of the
\delta
-localisation, does not work for localising Proca-Chern-Simons theory, despite its initial appeal. Having set up this toy case we are now finally ready to move on to the supersymmetrisation and localisation of the Third Way Theory.

4.5
Localisation of the Third Way Theory

4.5.1
Motivation

Having treated Proca-Chern-Simons theory as a toy model we are now ready to move on to the localisation of the Third Way Theory. Let us motivate again why this localisation —if successful— is so interesting:

As we saw in previous chapters, the Third Way Theory originated as a new kind of consistent sourcing of the Yang-Mills field equations, with a source which is conserved under the remarkable condition that the gauge fields are taken to be on-shell. Due to this rather peculiar fact it followed that no gauge invariant action could yield the Third Way field equations. The way to solve this issue was then to introduce auxiliary gauge fields which transform diagonally with the original gauge fields and which can’t be integrated out of the action, but for which the combined field equations yield the Third Way field equation. This action, rather than taking on the form of some augmented TMYM action, took on the form of two Chern-Simons actions connected with each other through a peculiar gauge invariant mass term.

This then leads us to our results in previous sections on localisation: One of the remarkable facts of the supersymmetrisation of Chern-Simons theory is that the field content added to supersymmetrise the theory is all auxiliary. This means that Chern-Simons theory and its supersymmetrisation are physically equivalent. This then in turn means that results derived from localising super-Chern-Simons theory yield results in Chern-Simons theory. Due to the Chern-Simons-like action of the Third Way Theory, this then leads to the hypothesis that this theory can be supersymmetrised, and that its supersymmetrisation is such that in analogy to Chern-Simons theory it is physically equivalent to the original theory. The results from a conjectured localisation of the super-Third Way Theory would then lead to exact results in the Third Way Theory. That is to say, if we can localise the Third Way Theory what we have localised is a deformation of topologically massive Yang-Mills theory with a highly non-trivial source term and which by its own formulation isn’t inherently supersymmetric.

4.5.2
Arvanitakis
\delta
-Localisation Scheme

We start off by briefly describing a localisation scheme originally suggested by Arvanitakis. This scheme does not closely resemble the localisation scheme developed for Proca-Chern-Simons Theory which was developed throughout this thesis. For example, rather than forcing the mass turn upon us through deformed supersymmetry transformations it seeks to eliminate it through parity properties of the transformations. It further also assumes that
\mu=0\Leftrightarrow m={\bar{m}}\Leftrightarrow k={\bar{k}}
. Instead of trying to localise on the
A
and
{\bar{A}}
field equations this scheme tries to localise on the diagonal and antidiagonal field equations. That is,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F-m\ast({\bar{A}}-A)=0\\
{\bar{F}}-m\ast({\bar{A}}-A)=0\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F-{\bar{F}}=0\\
F+{\bar{F}}=2m\ast({\bar{A}}-A)\end{cases}

(4.162)

Supersymmetric Action and Supersymmetries

In this scheme we have a supersymmetric action which depends on field content
(A,{\bar{A}},\sigma,\lambda,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}},D)
. That is, the field content of a vector multiplet
{\mathcal{V}}
alongside the ‘auxiliary’ gauge field
{\bar{A}}
. This is a little strange because one would typically expect that supersymmetric theories have matching fermionic and bosonic degrees of freedom. However, this isn’t a problem since in terms of field content this isn’t a hard rule, as we can always just add auxiliary fields to a theory indefinitely. The corresponding action is now given by

S[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{A}}]=\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{CS}}[{\bar{A}}]-\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]-\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{mass}}[{\bar{A}}-A]

(4.163)

with the Third Way mass term given by

S_{\text{mass}}[{\bar{A}}-A]=\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2\ell}({\bar{A}}-A)\ast({\bar{A}}-A)\bigg].

(4.164)

As is the case for Chern-Simons theory the additional field content
(\sigma,\lambda,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}},D)
is all auxiliary. The proposed supersymmetries of this action satisfy the property
\delta A=\delta{\bar{A}}
, thus automatically annihilating the mass term. These are given by

\displaystyle\delta A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\delta{\bar{A}}_{\mu}=-\zeta\gamma_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\gamma_{\mu}\lambda\bigg.

(4.165)

\displaystyle\delta\sigma

\displaystyle=i\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\lambda\bigg.

(4.166)

\displaystyle\delta\lambda

\displaystyle=\zeta\bigg[iD+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}({\bar{F}}-F)_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+[{\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}},\sigma]\bigg]\bigg.

(4.167)

\displaystyle\delta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\bigg[iD-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}({\bar{F}}-F)_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+[{\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}},\sigma]\bigg]\bigg.

(4.168)

\displaystyle\delta D

\displaystyle=-\zeta[{\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}},{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}]-{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[{\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}},\lambda]\bigg.

(4.169)

Originally these only included the
\zeta
components, since this scheme only cared about a
\delta
-localisation (for good reasons). It is worth noting that these transformations don’t resemble standard supersymmetry transformations as much anymore, due to the fact that they don’t contain derivatives anymore except for the field strengths.

Let us verify whether the action is eliminated by these supersymmetry transformations. First and foremost we note that

\displaystyle\delta A

\displaystyle=\delta{\bar{A}}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\delta S_{\text{mass}}[{\bar{A}}-A]

\displaystyle=0.

(4.170)

We further also note that in close analogy to computations
4.107
-
4.111
one finds

\begin{split}\delta\Big\{S_{\text{CS}}[{\bar{A}}]-S_{\text{CS}}[A]\Big\}&=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}({\bar{F}}-F)_{\mu\nu}\zeta\gamma_{\rho}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}({\bar{F}}-F)_{\mu\nu}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\gamma_{\rho}\lambda\bigg]\end{aligned}\\
&=-\delta\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[\lambda{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}-\sigma D\Big]\end{split}

(4.171)

We hence conclude that this action is supersymmetric.

Localisation

The localisation scheme for this procedure is the
\delta
-localisation scheme. The reason for this is that due to the unconventional form of the supersymmetries the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation scheme is hopeless in yielding any results. The fermionic localising functional is again given by

{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{A}}]=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\Big[(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda\Big].

(4.172)

Working out the
\delta
-localisation scheme we find in analogy to computation
4.90
that

S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{A}}]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\Big({\bar{F}}_{\mu\nu}-F_{\mu\nu}+\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}[({\bar{A}}-A)^{\rho},\sigma]\Big)\\
\times\Big({\bar{F}}{{}^{\mu\nu}}-F^{\mu\nu}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}[({\bar{A}}-A)_{\rho},\sigma]\Big)\bigg]

(4.173)

It is clear now that we don’t have any Bianchi identities at our disposal to make meaningful simplifications. As such we arrive at a localisation locus

\displaystyle{\bar{F}}_{\mu\nu}-F_{\mu\nu}+\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}[({\bar{A}}-A)^{\rho},\sigma]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=0.

(4.174)

This localises the action to the off-shell sourced diagonal field equations and the
\sigma
field equation.

4.5.3
The Super-Third Way Theory

We now formulate the super-Third Way Theory. The construction of this theory follows in close analogy to super-Proca-Chern-Simons theory. Again we deform the supersymmetry transformations in such a way that a mass term is forced upon us. In strong analogy to the super-Proca-Chern-Simons action we find

The Super-Third Way Action

S^{\text{super}}_{\text{3\textsuperscript{rd}Way}}[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]=\frac{k}{2\pi}S_{\text{SCS}}[{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi}S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]-\frac{k{\bar{k}}}{4\pi^{2}}S_{\text{mass}}[{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}-{\mathcal{V}}]

(4.175)

the supersymmetrisation of the Third Way Action. Its field content consists of two vector multiplets
{\mathcal{V}}=(A,\sigma,\lambda,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}},D)
and
{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}=({\bar{A}},{\bar{\sigma}},{\bar{\lambda}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}},{\bar{D}})
. Its super-Chern-Simons component actions are given by
4.106
and the mass term is in this case given by

S_{\text{mass}}[{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}-{\mathcal{V}}]=\int\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\frac{1}{2\ell}({\bar{A}}-A)\ast({\bar{A}}-A)+\frac{1}{2\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\ast({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\bigg]

(4.176)

in close analogy to the super-Proca-Chern-Simons case
4.126
. Its field equations are given by

\displaystyle F+\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}\ast({\bar{A}}-A)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\bar{F}}+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}\ast({\bar{A}}-A)

\displaystyle=0\bigg.

(4.177)

\displaystyle D+\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\bar{D}}+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)

\displaystyle=0\bigg.

(4.178)

\displaystyle\sigma=\lambda={\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle{\bar{\sigma}}=\lambda={\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}

\displaystyle=0\bigg.

(4.179)

Its supersymmetries are also derived in close analogy to super-Proca-Chern-Simons theory. We recall that the criteria which were used to derive these supersymmetry transformations were given by

•

Supersymmetrising the Third Way Theory.

•

Yielding nilpotent supersymmetry transformations.

•

Ideally localising the action on on-shell field configuration.

These conditions yield the following supersymmetry transformations:

Third Way Supersymmetry Transformations

We denote the Third Way supersymmetry transformation by

\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}},{\bar{\zeta}},{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}}=\delta_{\zeta}+{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}+{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}}+{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}

(4.180)

this time parametrised by
four
independent constant 2-spinors
\zeta
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}
,
{\bar{\zeta}}
,
{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}
. The supersymmetry transformations read

\displaystyle\delta A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\zeta\gamma_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\gamma_{\mu}\lambda\bigg.

(4.181)

\displaystyle\delta{\bar{A}}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-{\bar{\zeta}}\gamma_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}+{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\gamma_{\mu}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg.

(4.182)

\displaystyle\delta\sigma

\displaystyle=i\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\lambda\bigg.

(4.183)

\displaystyle\delta{\bar{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=i{\bar{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg.

(4.184)

\displaystyle\delta\lambda

\displaystyle=\zeta\bigg[iD+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}})+\frac{ik}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)}\bigg]\bigg.

(4.185)

\displaystyle\delta{\bar{\lambda}}

\displaystyle={\bar{\zeta}}\bigg[i{\bar{D}}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}{\bar{F}}_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\bar{{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}}}{\bar{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}})+\frac{i{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)}\bigg]\bigg.

(4.186)

\displaystyle\delta{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\bigg[iD-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}})+\frac{ik}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)}\bigg]\bigg.

(4.187)

\displaystyle\delta{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}

\displaystyle={\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\bigg[i{\bar{D}}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}{\bar{F}}_{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\rho}+{\bar{{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}}}{\bar{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}})+\frac{i{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)}\bigg]\bigg.

(4.188)

\displaystyle\delta D

\displaystyle=i\zeta\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}+\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma\big)\lambda\bigg.

(4.189)

\displaystyle\delta{\bar{D}}

\displaystyle=i{\bar{\zeta}}\big(i{\bar{{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}}}+\operatorname{ad}{\bar{\sigma}}\big){\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}+i{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\big(i{\bar{{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}}}-\operatorname{ad}{\bar{\sigma}}\big){\bar{\lambda}}\bigg.

(4.190)

where the highlighted terms are the new terms which diverge from the standard supersymmetry transformations of the vector multiplet. Note that these terms indeed vanish as
\ell\to\infty
.

Let us now verify that these indeed leave the super-Third Way action invariant. We find that the new contributions are given by

\displaystyle\frac{k}{2\pi}\delta S_{\text{SCS}}[{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]\Big|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{new}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{k{\bar{k}}}{4\pi^{2}\ell}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &{\bar{\zeta}}\Big(-({\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}})+i({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big){\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}\\
+~&{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\Big(+({\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}})+i({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big){\bar{\lambda}}\bigg]\end{aligned}

(4.191)

\displaystyle-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi}\delta S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\Big|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{new}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{k{\bar{k}}}{4\pi^{2}\ell}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &\zeta\Big(+({\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}})-i({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\\
+~&{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\Big(-({\bar{\not{A}}}-{\not{A}})-i({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big)\lambda\bigg]\end{aligned}

(4.192)

These cancel precisely against the supersymmetry transformations of the mass term:

-\frac{k{\bar{k}}}{4\pi^{2}}\delta S_{\text{mass}}[{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}-{\mathcal{V}}]=-\frac{k}{2\pi}\delta S_{\text{SCS}}[{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]\Big|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{new}}}+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi}\delta S_{\text{SCS}}[{\mathcal{V}}]\Big|_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{new}}}

(4.193)

hence proving that the super-Third Way Theory is supersymmetric

\delta_{\zeta,{\tilde{\zeta}},{\bar{\zeta}},{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}}S^{\text{super}}_{\text{3\textsuperscript{rd}Way}}[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]=0.

(4.194)

As for the nilpotency of these transformations, these follow from precisely the same steps as equations
4.135
-
4.138
, thus resulting in

\delta^{2}_{\zeta}={\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}{{}^{2}_{\tilde{\zeta}}}={\bar{\delta}}{{}^{2}_{\bar{\zeta}}}={\bar{\delta}}{{}^{2}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}}=0.

(4.195)

That is, the Third Way supersymmetries are nilpotent.

4.5.4
The Third Way Supersymmetry Algebra

Four Killing Spinors

Let us now take a closer look at the supersymmetry algebra of the super-Third Way Theory. This algebra will take on a significantly more complicated form, since now we have split it up into four different supersymmetries. We split these up into three different cathegories:

•

\delta^{2}
,
{\bar{\delta}}{{}^{2}}
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}{{}^{2}}
,
{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}{{}^{2}}
: These all vanish generalising the nilpotency of the transformations as

\{\delta_{\zeta},\delta_{\eta}\}=\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\eta}}\}=\{{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}},{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\eta}}\}=\{{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\eta}}}}\}=0

(4.196)

•

\delta{\bar{\delta}}
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}{\bar{\delta}}
,
\delta{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}
,
{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}
: These interestingly —or perhaps alarmingly— show some non-vanishing results:

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}}\}\lambda^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{\pi\ell}(\zeta{\bar{\zeta}}){\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}

(4.197)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}}\}{\bar{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}(\zeta{\bar{\zeta}}){\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}}\}\big|_{\text{other}}

\displaystyle=0

(4.198)

\displaystyle\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k}{\pi\ell}{\bar{\zeta}}{{}^{\alpha}}({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}})

(4.199)

\displaystyle\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}}\}{\bar{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{{}^{\alpha}}({\bar{\zeta}}\lambda)

\displaystyle\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}}\}\big|_{\text{other}}

\displaystyle=0

(4.200)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}\lambda^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k}{\pi\ell}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}(\zeta{\bar{\lambda}})

(4.201)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}\zeta^{\alpha}({\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}\big|_{\text{other}}

\displaystyle=0

(4.202)

\displaystyle\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{k}{\pi\ell}({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}){\bar{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

(4.203)

\displaystyle\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}})\lambda^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}\big|_{\text{other}}

\displaystyle=0

(4.204)

•

\delta{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}
,
{\bar{\delta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}
: These are the Third Way analogues of the non-trivial relations:

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\nu}\Big(F_{\nu\mu}-\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}\epsilon_{\nu\mu\rho}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\rho}\Big)+2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma

(4.205)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\sigma

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma

(4.206)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\lambda^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\lambda^{\alpha}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,\lambda^{\alpha}]+\frac{k}{\pi\ell}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{{}^{\alpha}}(\zeta\lambda)

(4.207)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}]+\frac{k}{\pi\ell}\zeta^{\alpha}({\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})

(4.208)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}D

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\Big(D+\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big)-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,D]\\
&+\frac{k}{\pi\ell}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\end{aligned}

(4.209)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}

\displaystyle=0

(4.210)

and similarly

\displaystyle\{{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}{\bar{A}}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-2i{\bar{K}}{{}^{\nu}}\Big({\bar{F}}_{\nu\mu}-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}\epsilon_{\nu\mu\rho}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\rho}\Big)+2{\bar{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}{\bar{\sigma}}

(4.211)

\displaystyle\{{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}{\bar{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=-2i{\bar{K}}{{}^{\mu}}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}{\bar{\sigma}}

(4.212)

\displaystyle\{{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}{\bar{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-2i{\bar{K}}{{}^{\mu}}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}{\bar{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}-2{\bar{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}[{\bar{\sigma}},{\bar{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}]-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}({\bar{\zeta}}{\bar{\lambda}})

(4.213)

\displaystyle\{{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-2i{\bar{K}}{{}^{\mu}}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}-2{\bar{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}[{\bar{\sigma}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}]-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}{\bar{\zeta}}{{}^{\alpha}}({\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}})

(4.214)

\displaystyle\{{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}{\bar{D}}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &-2i{\bar{K}}{{}^{\mu}}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}\Big({\bar{D}}+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big)-2{\bar{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}[{\bar{\sigma}},{\bar{D}}]\\
&+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}{\bar{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\end{aligned}

(4.215)

\displaystyle\{{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}\}{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle=0

(4.216)

where by
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}
we denoted the collective field content of the respective vector multiplets and where we defined the Killing vectors

\displaystyle K^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\zeta\gamma^{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}},

\displaystyle{\bar{K}}{{}^{\mu}}

\displaystyle={\bar{\zeta}}\gamma^{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}.

(4.217)
