Two Killing Spinors

We note that due to the relations
4.197
-
4.204
, and in particular equations
4.197
,
4.198
,
4.203
and
4.204
the algebra takes on quite an unusual form. However, we note that these are all proportional to either
\zeta{\bar{\zeta}}
or
{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}
. Hence this suggests that to make these vanish we could take
\zeta={\bar{\zeta}}
and
{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}={\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\zeta}}}}
and redefine

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta},{\bar{\delta}}_{\bar{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\to\delta_{\zeta}:=\delta_{\zeta,0,\zeta,0}

\displaystyle{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\delta}}}}_{\tilde{{\bar{\zeta}}}}

\displaystyle\to{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}:=\delta_{0,{\tilde{\zeta}},0,{\tilde{\zeta}}}.

(4.218)

The algebra then takes on the more familiar form

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},\delta_{\eta}\}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\{{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\eta}}\}

\displaystyle=0

(4.219)

with non-trivial anticommutators

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\nu}\Big(F_{\nu\mu}-\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}\epsilon_{\nu\mu\rho}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\rho}\Big)+2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma\bigg.

(4.220)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\bar{A}}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\nu}\Big({\bar{F}}_{\nu\mu}-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}\epsilon_{\nu\mu\rho}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\rho}\Big)+2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}{\bar{\sigma}}\bigg.

(4.221)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\sigma

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\sigma\bigg.

(4.222)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\bar{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}{\bar{\sigma}}\bigg.

(4.223)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}\lambda^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\lambda^{\alpha}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,\lambda^{\alpha}]-\frac{k}{\pi\ell}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{{}^{\alpha}}\zeta({\bar{\lambda}}-\lambda)\bigg.

(4.224)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\bar{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}{\bar{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[{\bar{\sigma}},{\bar{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}]-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}{{}^{\alpha}}\zeta({\bar{\lambda}}-\lambda)\bigg.

(4.225)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{{}^{\alpha}}]-\frac{k}{\pi\ell}\zeta^{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}({\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})\bigg.

(4.226)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-2iK^{\mu}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[{\bar{\sigma}},{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}{{}^{\alpha}}]-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}\zeta^{\alpha}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}({\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})\bigg.

(4.227)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}D

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &-2iK^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\Big(D+\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big)-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[\sigma,D]\\
&+\frac{k}{\pi\ell}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\end{aligned}\bigg.

(4.228)

\displaystyle\{\delta_{\zeta},{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\tilde{\zeta}}\}{\bar{D}}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &-2iK^{\mu}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}\Big({\bar{D}}+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big)-2\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}[{\bar{\sigma}},{\bar{D}}]\\
&+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{\pi\ell}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}\zeta{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}\end{aligned}\bigg.

(4.229)

where we now only defined a single Killing vector

K^{\mu}=\zeta\gamma^{\mu}{\accentset{\sim}{\zeta}}.

(4.230)

In analogy to the Proca-Chern-Simons case this transformation takes on the form

\text{SUSY}^{2}=\text{transl}+\text{gauge}+\text{mass}.

(4.231)

However, an aspect in which it is different from Proca-Chern-Simons theory is that the field dependent gauge transformation are different between the two multiplets
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}
. Aside from the issues we incountered in Proca-Chern-Simons theory for the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation argument this also will hinder it.

4.5.5
Localising the Super-Third Way Theory

Finally, we move on to perform the localisation procedure of the super-Third Way Theory. This will follow in close analogy to the Proca-Chern-Simons case.

{\mathcal{Q}}
-Localisation Scheme

Let us now start with the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-localisation scheme instead of the
\delta
-localisation scheme, because this is the less promising one for similar reasons as the Proca-Chern-Simons case. In this case the fermionic localising functional is given by

{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]=\frac{1}{4|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda+({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}+({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\bar{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\bar{\lambda}}+({\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}})^{\dagger}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}\bigg]

(4.232)

where we defined a localising supersymmetry

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}=\delta_{\zeta}+{\accentset{\sim}{\delta}}_{\zeta}=\delta_{\zeta,\zeta,\zeta,\zeta}

(4.233)

again following the definition
4.218
. However, for reasons similar to the Proca-Chern-Simons case it sadly doesn’t work, since

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\zeta}S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]\neq 0.

(4.234)

The explicit expression isn’t given because as one might suspect it’s quite a mess, and it doesn’t seem to vanish on-shell, suggesting that adding auxiliary field content won’t obviously help. However, this doesn’t stop one from trying and multiple ways of adjusting the supersymmetries were tried out, though all being unsuccessful. Most notably a way to come up with the ‘right’ supersymmetries was starting from superspace, and breaking the group of supergauge transformations. This would lead to supersymmetries somewhat resembling the Third Way supersymmetries but which didn’t seem fit for constructing a supersymmetric action.

\delta
-localisation scheme

Now, we treat the more promising
\delta
-localisation scheme. Here the fermionic localising functional is given by

{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]=\frac{1}{2|\zeta|^{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[(\delta_{\zeta}\lambda)^{\dagger}\lambda+(\delta_{\zeta}{\bar{\lambda}})^{\dagger}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg]

(4.235)

where we defined the supersymmetry transformation
\delta_{\zeta}=\delta_{\zeta,0,\zeta,0}
as in equation
4.218
. The systematics of the computations are identitcal to Proca-Chern-Simons theory but the equations are over twice as long. Hence, we will only present the reader with the results, these are given by:

\delta
-Localisation of the Super-Third Way Theory

\begin{split}&S_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]=\delta_{\zeta}{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{loc}}[{\mathcal{V}},{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}]\\
&=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{3}x~\operatorname{tr}\bigg[\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{4}\Big(F_{\mu\nu}-\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\rho}+\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}{\mathcal{D}}^{\rho}\sigma\Big)\\
&\times\Big(F^{\mu\nu}-\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}({\bar{A}}-A)_{\rho}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}{\mathcal{D}}_{\rho}\sigma\Big)\\
&-\frac{1}{4}\Big({\bar{F}}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\rho}+\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}{{}^{\rho}}{\bar{\sigma}}\Big)\\
&\times\Big({\bar{F}}{{}^{\mu\nu}}-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}({\bar{A}}-A)_{\rho}+\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\rho}{\bar{\sigma}}\Big)\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\Big(D+\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Big({\bar{D}}+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)\Big)^{2}\\
&-{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}\Big(i{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}-\operatorname{ad}\sigma+\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}\Big)\lambda+\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}\lambda\\
&-{\accentset{\sim}{{\bar{\lambda}}}}\big(i{\bar{{\not{{\mathcal{D}}}}}}-\operatorname{ad}{\bar{\sigma}}-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}\big){\bar{\lambda}}-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}{\accentset{\sim}{\lambda}}{\bar{\lambda}}\bigg]\end{aligned}\end{split}

(4.236)

which yields a localisation locus

\displaystyle F-\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}\ast({\bar{A}}-A)

\displaystyle=-\ast{\mathcal{D}}\sigma

\displaystyle D+\frac{k}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)

\displaystyle=0

(4.237)

\displaystyle{\bar{F}}-\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}\ast({\bar{A}}-A)

\displaystyle=-\ast{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}{\bar{\sigma}}

\displaystyle{\bar{D}}+\frac{{\bar{k}}}{2\pi\ell}({\bar{\sigma}}-\sigma)

\displaystyle=0

(4.238)

where the Third Way field equations become sourced by the scalar fields.

In analogy to the Proca-Chern-Simons case, we note that for the covariant flatness
{\mathcal{D}}\sigma={\bar{\mathcal{D}}}{\bar{\sigma}}=0
to seperate from the
A
,
{\bar{A}}
field equations the condition
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}\equiv{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}=0
would have to be satisfied. The deeper reason why this isn’t the case can again be traced back to the violation of Noether’s second theorem.
Due to the fact that the action isn’t invariant under a full
G\times G
but rather the diagonal subgroup
G
, Noether’s second theorem doesn’t have to hold anymore:

\delta_{\theta,{\bar{\theta}}}S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}~\not{\equiv}~0

(4.239)

where we now have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\frac{\delta S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}}{\delta A_{\mu}}

\displaystyle~\not{\equiv}~0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}\frac{\delta S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}}{\delta{\bar{A}}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle~\not{\equiv}~0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}\equiv{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}

\displaystyle~\not{\equiv}~0.

(4.240)

However, if the fields are taken to be on-shell, this requirement is satisfied:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}}{\delta A_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S^{\text{3rd}}_{\text{Way}}}{\delta{\bar{A}}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=0

(4.241)

Again it is worth noting that the appearance of terms which vanish on-shell could be an indicator for auxiliary fields which have been integrated away. Perhaps there exists some addition of auciliary fields, which could after integrating them away impose the condition
{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}\equiv{\bar{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}({\bar{A}}-A)^{\mu}=0
. Attempts have been made to do this consistently, and while the possibility of this isn’t excluded, no such addition of additional auxiliary fields is currently known.

Conclusion

Let us now conclude this thesis. The main goal of this thesis was to localise the Third Way Theory. However, a lot of interesting original computations also emerged from our treatment of the Witten index in chapter 2.

For the Witten index, a number of original computations have been performed. These derived already established results which —to the best of our knowledge— haven’t been applied elsewhere in the literature. For starters, the
\beta
-independence of the Witten index is typically only derived using the Hilbert space formalism. Instead in this thesis we used the path integral formalism to derive this result. We arrived at this result by using the superspace formalism to note that the action is
Q
-exact. Similarly, the ‘kinetic’ and ‘potential’ rescalings were shown to be symmetries of the Witten index, again making use of superspace methods. These methods had the advantage of showing that a term is manifestly
Q
-exact, rather than forcing us to engage in educated guess work. Finally, the computations were overall more thorough than those in the source material (particularly
[
13
]
).

As for the Third Way Theory, let us remind the reader of the significance of localising this theory again: On-shell, the Third Way Theory is a continuous deformation of Yang-Mills theory. However, the action which describes this theory consists of two independent Chern-Simons theories over the same gauge group and a mass term which breaks the two copies of the gauge group to its diagonal subgroup. Combined with the observation that
{\mathcal{N}}=0
Chern-Simons theory can be localised due to its equivalence with
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Chern-Simons theory, one is lead to hypothesise that the
{\mathcal{N}}=0
and
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Third Way theories are equivalent. As a corollary to this, it would then follow that localising the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Third Way Theory is equivalent to localising the
{\mathcal{N}}=0
Third Way Theory, thus achieving a method of doing non-perturbative computations in a highly non-trivial deformation of
d=3
Yang-Mills theory.

Progress to this end has been made in various ways: Significant progress has been made into understanding Proca-Chern-Simons theory, the toy model which leads to the Third Way localisation. A new kind of supersymmetry transformation has been introduced to supersymmetrise this theory, which we coined ‘massive supersymmetry’. Using this supersymmetry, Proca-Chern-Simons theory was then superymmetrised, such that its superanalogue is physically equivalent to the original theory. After this, the theory was localised on its field equations, sourced by auxiliary fields. Then, a proposed localisation scheme by Arvanitakis was reviewed which localised the Third Way Theory on its diagonal field equation, sourced by auxiliary fields. Finally, the results of Proca-Chern-Simons theory were generalised to the Third Way Theory, leading to a supersymmetrisation of the Third Way Theory which is physically equivalent to the original theory. Using this supersymmetrisation, the Third Way Theory was then localised on both its field equations, sourced by auxiliary fields.

In conclusion, significant progress has been made towards localising the Third Way Theory. We found a localising action which localises the theory on sourced field equations. One assuption which has been made throughout the thesis though is that the path integral measure is supersymmetric. This is at this moment the only barrier between definitively being able to say that the theory is localised.

A.1
Differential Geometry

A.1.1
Riemannian Geometry

We denote the flat space metric as

\eta_{ab}=\operatorname{diag}(-1,\dots,-1,+1,\dots,+1).

(A.1)

The curved space metric is given by

g_{\mu\nu}=e_{\mu}{{}^{a}}e_{\nu}{{}^{b}}\eta_{ab}

(A.2)

where
e_{\mu}{{}^{a}}
the vielbeins with inverses
e^{\mu}{{}_{a}}
which we take such that

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle:=\det(\eta_{ab})=(-)^{\text{\# time directions}}

(A.3)

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle:=\det(e_{\mu}{{}^{a}})>0

(A.4)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle:=\det(g_{\mu\nu})=\eta e^{2}

(A.5)

We denote the Christoffel symbol by
\Gamma_{\mu\sigma}{{}^{\rho}}
and the spin connection by
\omega_{\mu}{{}^{ab}}

[
14
]
[
27
]
.

A.1.2
Lie algebras and Connections

Lie algebras

We consider a simple compact Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
. The Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is taken to be generated by
T_{I}
with structure constants
f_{IJ}{{}^{K}}
and Killing form
\kappa_{IJ}
defined through

\displaystyle[T_{I},T_{J}]

\displaystyle=f_{IJ}{{}^{K}}T_{K}

\displaystyle\kappa_{IJ}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}\big[T_{I}T_{J}\big]

(A.6)

in our conventions
f_{IJ}{{}^{K}}
are real and
\kappa_{IJ}
non-degenerate and negative semi-definite. Let
\omega_{p}
and
\eta_{q}
be
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued forms. Their commutator is then defined as

\displaystyle[\omega_{p},\eta_{q}]

\displaystyle:=\omega_{p}\eta_{q}-(-)^{pq}\eta_{q}\omega_{p}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle[\omega_{p},\eta_{q}]^{I}

\displaystyle:=f_{JK}{{}^{I}}\omega_{p}^{J}\eta_{q}^{K}.

(A.7)

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded Jacobi identity here takes on the form

[\alpha_{r},[\omega_{p},\eta_{q}]]=[[\alpha_{r},\omega_{p}],\eta_{q}]+(-)^{rp}[\omega_{p},[\alpha_{r},\eta_{q}]].

(A.8)

One should think of this as analogous to the super-Jacobi identities for Lie superalgebras
[
9
]
[
14
]
.

Connections

Connections of this group are
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued 1-forms
A
which transform as

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\to e^{\theta}(A+{\mathrm{d}})e^{-\theta}

\displaystyle\delta_{\theta}A

\displaystyle=-{\mathrm{d}}\theta-[A,\theta]=:-{\mathcal{D}}\theta

(A.9)

and field strengths are denoted as

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle:={\mathrm{d}}A+A^{2}

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\to e^{\theta}Fe^{-\theta}

\displaystyle\delta_{\theta}F

\displaystyle=[\theta,F]

(A.10)

where
\theta=\theta^{I}T_{I}
a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued local gauge parameters. Field strengths satisfy the Bianchi identity

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}F

\displaystyle\equiv 0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{[\mu}F_{\rho\sigma]}

\displaystyle\equiv 0.

(A.11)

where we defined
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathrm{d}}+\operatorname{ad}A

[
14
]
.

A.1.3
Levi-Civita Symbol and Hodge Dualities

Levi-Civita symbol and tensor

The Levi-Civita
symbol
is characterised by

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{[a_{1}\dots a_{d}]}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{d}}

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0\dots(d-1)}

\displaystyle=1

(A.12)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{[a_{1}\dots a_{d}]}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{a_{1}\dots a_{d}}

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{0\dots(d-1)}

\displaystyle=1

(A.13)

Similarly, we define the Levi-Civita
tensor
for flat indices as

\displaystyle\epsilon_{[a_{1}\dots a_{d}]}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{d}}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0\dots(d-1)}

\displaystyle=1

(A.14)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{[a_{1}\dots a_{d}]}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{a_{1}\dots a_{d}}

\displaystyle\epsilon^{0\dots(d-1)}

\displaystyle=\eta

(A.15)

and the Levi-Civita tensor for curved indices is obtained as

\epsilon_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{d}}:=e_{\mu_{1}}{{}^{a_{1}}}\dots e_{\mu_{d}}{{}^{a_{d}}}\varepsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{d}}=e\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{d}}.

(A.16)

Contractions between Levi-Civita symbols are computed as

\varepsilon^{a_{1}\dots a_{p}c_{p+1}\dots c_{d}}\varepsilon_{b_{1}\dots b_{p}c_{p+1}\dots c_{d}}=p!(d-p)!\delta^{a_{1}\dots a_{p}}_{b_{1}\dots b_{p}}

(A.17)

where the generalised Kronecker delta symbol is defined as

\delta^{a_{1}\dots a_{p}}_{b_{1}\dots b_{p}}=\delta^{[a_{1}}{{}_{b_{1}}}\dots\delta^{a_{d}]}{{}_{b_{d}}}.

(A.18)

For the Levi-Civita tensor this gives

\displaystyle\epsilon^{a_{1}\dots a_{p}c_{p+1}\dots c_{d}}\epsilon_{b_{1}\dots b_{p}c_{p+1}\dots c_{d}}

\displaystyle=p!(d-p)!\cdot\eta\cdot\delta^{a_{1}\dots a_{p}}_{b_{1}\dots b_{p}}

(A.19)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}\rho_{p+1}\dots\mu_{d}}\epsilon_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{p}\rho_{p+1}\dots\mu_{d}}

\displaystyle=p!(d-p)!\cdot\eta\cdot\delta^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{p}}.

(A.20)

with additional factors
\eta

[
14
]
[
27
]
.

Hodge dualities

We define the Hodge star operator
\ast:\Omega^{p}(M)\to\Omega^{d-p}(M)
as

\ast\omega_{\mu_{p+1}\dots\mu_{d}}:=\frac{1}{p!}\epsilon_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{d}}\omega^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}

(A.21)

The Hodge star satisfies

\displaystyle\ast_{d-p}\ast_{p}

\displaystyle=\eta(-)^{p(d-p)}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\ast_{p}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\eta(-)^{p(d-p)}\ast_{d-p}

(A.22)

giving us an expression for inverse Hodge operators
[
14
]
[
27
]
.

Inner products between forms

Making use of the Hodge star we can construct inner products

\displaystyle(\bullet|\bullet)

\displaystyle:\Omega^{p}(M)^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle(\bullet|\bullet)

\displaystyle:(\Omega^{p}(M)\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbb{R}

(A.23)

between (
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued) forms as

(\omega|\eta):=\int\operatorname{tr}\big[\omega\ast\eta\big]=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{d}x~\frac{e}{p!}\kappa_{IJ}\omega^{I}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\eta^{J\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}.

(A.24)

The non-
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued case is simply obtained by not taking the trace as it isn’t defined. This product is symmetric and non-degenerate. On Riemannian manifolds it’s also negative semi-definite in the
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued case and positive semi-definite in the non-
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued case
[
14
]
.

Adjoint exterior derivatives

Using the aforementioned inner product we can now define adjoints of exterior derivatives. Let
\omega_{p}
and
\eta_{p+1}
be respectively
p
- and
(p+1)
-forms. We define the adjoint
{\mathrm{d}}^{\dagger}_{p+1}:\Omega^{p+1}(M)\to\Omega^{p}(M)
of the exterior derivative
[
14
]
[
27
]
through the relation

\displaystyle({\mathrm{d}}_{p}\omega_{p}|\eta_{p+1})=(\omega_{p}|{\mathrm{d}}^{\dagger}_{p+1}\eta_{p+1})

(A.25)

\displaystyle\Downarrow

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}^{\dagger}=-\eta(-)^{p(d-p)}\ast{\mathrm{d}}\ast=-(-)^{d+p}\ast{\mathrm{d}}{\ast^{-1}}=(-)^{p}{\ast^{-1}}{\mathrm{d}}\ast=-{\mathrm{d}}\operatorname{\lrcorner}

(A.26)

where we defined the contraction between forms as

\displaystyle\eta_{p}\operatorname{\lrcorner}\omega_{p+q}=\omega_{p+q}\operatorname{\llcorner}\eta_{p}:=(-)^{r(d-r+s)}{\ast^{-1}}(\eta_{s}\ast\omega_{r+s})\Big.

(A.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{m}\Big.

\displaystyle(\eta_{p}\operatorname{\lrcorner}\omega_{p+q})_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}=(\omega_{p+q}\operatorname{\llcorner}\eta_{p})_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}:=\frac{1}{q!}\eta^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}}\omega_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}

(A.28)

Similarly it follows that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{D}}\operatorname{\lrcorner}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{ad}A)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{ad}A\operatorname{\lrcorner}

(A.29)

A.1.4
Matrix Exponentiation and BCH Formula

Let us mention some standard formulas related to matrix exponentiation. Let
X,Y
be arbitrary matrices. One could verify that

\displaystyle e^{X}Ye^{-X}

\displaystyle=e^{\operatorname{ad}X}Y=Y+[X,Y]+\frac{1}{2}[X,[X,Y]]+\dots

(A.30)

\displaystyle e^{X}\delta e^{-X}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-e^{-\operatorname{ad}X}}{\operatorname{ad}X}\delta X=\delta X-\frac{1}{2}[X,\delta X]+\frac{1}{6}[X,[X,\delta X]]-\dots

(A.31)

where
\delta
is an arbitrary derivative with respect to matrix multiplication
[
28
]
.

Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula

For matrices which don’t commute the identity
e^{x}e^{y}=e^{x+y}
doesn’t generally hold anymore. Instead, it is replaced by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula
[
29
]

e^{X}e^{Y}=e^{X\star Y}

(A.32)

where
X\star Y
is formally expressed as

X\star Y=\ln(e^{X}e^{Y})=Y+\int_{0}^{1}{\mathrm{d}}t~g\big(e^{t\operatorname{ad}X}e^{\operatorname{ad}Y}\big)X=-(-Y)\star(-X)

(A.33)

with
g(z)
given by

\displaystyle g(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\ln z}{1-z}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(1-z)^{n}}{n+1},

\displaystyle|1-z|

\displaystyle<1.

(A.34)

Some of the leading order terms are given by

X\star Y=X+Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]+\frac{1}{12}[[X,Y],Y]+\frac{1}{12}[X,[X,Y]]+{\mathcal{O}}(X^{p}Y^{q})_{p+q\geq 4}

(A.35)

Another interesting way to expand
X\star Y
is as

\begin{split}X\star Y&=X+\frac{\operatorname{ad}X}{1-e^{-\operatorname{ad}X}}Y+{\mathcal{O}}(Y^{2})\\
&=Y-\frac{\operatorname{ad}Y}{1-e^{+\operatorname{ad}Y}}X+{\mathcal{O}}(X^{2})\end{split}

(A.36)

to leading orders in respectively
Y
and
X

[
9
]
. Interestingly, it is the case that

\frac{z}{e^{z}-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{B_{n}}{n!}z^{n}=1-\frac{1}{2}z+\frac{1}{12}z^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(z^{4})

(A.37)

where
B_{n}
the
Bernoulli numbers

[
30
]
recursively defined as

\displaystyle B_{0}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle B_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}\binom{n+1}{k}B_{k}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle B_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{n+1}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n+1}{k}B_{k}.

(A.38)

Some of the leading values are given by

\displaystyle B_{0}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle B_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2},

\displaystyle B_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6},

\displaystyle B_{3}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle B_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{30},

\displaystyle\dots

(A.39)

A.2
d=0+3
,
{\mathcal{N}}=2
Supersymmetry

A.2.1
Spinors and Gamma Matrices

The gamma matrices are
2\times 2
matrices given by

\gamma^{\mu}=(\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}})=(\sigma^{3},~-\sigma^{1},~-\sigma^{2})

(A.40)

with
\mu=1,2,3
and
\alpha,\beta=1,2
, where lower spinorial indices are column indices and upper spinorial indices are row indices, and
\sigma^{\mu}
are the Pauli matrices
[
11
]
[
26
]
. These satisfy an algebra

\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}{\mathbbm{1}}+i\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\gamma_{\rho}

(A.41)

where we normalised the
x
-space Levi-Civita symbol as
\epsilon_{012}=1
and the metric is simply given by
\eta_{\mu\nu}=\delta_{\mu\nu}
.
Indices are raised and lowered using the charge conjugation matrix which in this case is just the Levi-Civita symbol
\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}
in spinor space, where we follow NW-SE conventions:

\displaystyle\psi^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\psi_{\beta},

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\psi^{\beta}\varepsilon_{\beta\alpha}.

(A.42)

where we normalised
\varepsilon_{12}=\varepsilon^{12}=1
. Let us name a few interesting properties of the tensors
\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}
and
\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}}
:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{\beta}{{}_{\alpha}}=\delta_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\mu\beta}{{}_{\alpha}}

etc.

(A.43)

Similarly, we have for example

\displaystyle\psi\chi

\displaystyle:=\psi^{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}=(-)^{|\psi||\chi|+1}\chi\psi

(A.44)

\displaystyle\psi\gamma^{\mu}\chi

\displaystyle:=\psi^{\alpha}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}}\chi_{\beta}=(-)^{|\psi||\chi|}\chi\gamma^{\mu}\psi

(A.45)

where
|\bullet|
denotes the Grassmann parity of an object. In case there is no(t much) room for confusion, contracted indices will usually be dropped, with always an assumption of NW-SE contraction. Some more advanced identities of the gamma matrices include the Fierz identity

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbbm{1}}\operatorname{tr}M+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\mu}\operatorname{tr}(M\gamma_{\mu})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle M_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}M_{\gamma}{{}^{\gamma}}\varepsilon_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2}M_{\gamma}{{}^{\delta}}\gamma_{\mu\delta}{{}^{\gamma}}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}}

(A.46)

as well as the following few lemmas
[
11
]
[
26
]
:

A.2.2
Some identities

Lemma:

\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha\beta}}\gamma_{\mu}{{}^{\gamma\delta}}=-2\varepsilon_{(\alpha}{{}^{\gamma}}\varepsilon_{\beta)}{{}^{\delta}}

(A.47)

Proof:
We start off by noting that

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\ref{Clifford algebra}}}{=}\delta^{\mu}{{}_{\mu}}{\mathbbm{1}}=3{\mathbbm{1}},

(A.48)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\ref{Clifford algebra}}}{=}\gamma^{\mu}+i\epsilon^{\nu\mu\rho}\gamma_{\rho}\gamma_{\nu}\overset{\text{\ref{Clifford algebra}}}{=}\gamma^{\mu}-\epsilon^{\nu\mu\rho}\epsilon_{\rho\nu\sigma}\gamma^{\sigma}=-\gamma^{\mu}.

Applying the Fierz identity to respectively
{}_{\alpha}{{}^{\gamma}}
and
{}_{\beta}{{}^{\gamma}}
we now find that

\begin{split}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha\beta}}\gamma_{\mu}{{}^{\gamma\delta}}&\overset{\text{\ref{Fierz identity}}}{=}-\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{\mu(\beta}{{}^{\delta}}\varepsilon_{\alpha)}{{}^{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\mu(\beta}{{}^{\delta}}\gamma^{\nu}{{}_{\alpha)}}{{}^{\gamma}}\\
&\overset{\text{\ref{tussenstap}}}{=}-\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon_{(\alpha}{{}^{\gamma}}\varepsilon_{\beta)}{{}^{\delta}}-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{\mu(\alpha}{{}^{\gamma}}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\beta)}}{{}^{\delta}}\\
&\overset{\text{\ref{Fierz identity}}}{=}-\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon_{(\alpha}{{}^{\gamma}}\varepsilon_{\beta)}{{}^{\delta}}-\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma_{\mu\gamma\delta}\gamma_{\nu}{{}^{\alpha\beta}}\\
&\overset{\text{\ref{tussenstap}}}{=}-\frac{3}{2}\varepsilon_{(\alpha}{{}^{\gamma}}\varepsilon_{\beta)}{{}^{\delta}}+\frac{1}{4}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha\beta}}\gamma_{\mu}{{}^{\gamma\delta}}\end{split}

(A.49)

which is clearly equivalent to the desired identity.∎

Lemma:

\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\gamma_{\rho\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}\gamma_{\sigma\gamma}{{}^{\delta}}=i\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\delta}}\varepsilon_{\gamma}{{}^{\beta}}-i\varepsilon_{\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\gamma}}{{}^{\beta}}

(A.50)

Proof:
Application of the Fierz identity on
{}^{\beta}{{}_{\gamma}}
yields

\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\gamma_{\rho\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}\gamma_{\sigma\gamma}{{}^{\delta}}\overset{\text{\ref{Fierz identity}}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\Big(\gamma_{\rho}\gamma_{\sigma\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}\varepsilon_{\gamma}{{}^{\beta}}+\gamma_{\rho}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}\gamma^{\nu}{{}_{\gamma}}{{}^{\beta}}\Big).

(A.51)

For the first term we find

\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\gamma_{\rho}\gamma_{\sigma\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}\overset{\text{\ref{Clifford algebra}}}{=}\frac{i}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\epsilon_{\rho\sigma\nu}\gamma^{\nu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\delta}}=i\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\delta}}.

(A.52)

As for the second term we find

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\gamma_{\rho}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}&\overset{\text{\ref{Clifford algebra}}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\big(\eta_{\rho\nu}+i\epsilon_{\rho\nu\tau}\gamma^{\tau}\big)\gamma_{\sigma\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}\\
&\overset{\text{\ref{Clifford algebra}}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu}{{}_{\nu\sigma}}\gamma^{\sigma}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\delta}}-i\delta^{\mu\sigma}_{\nu\tau}\big(\delta^{\tau}{{}_{\sigma}}\varepsilon_{\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}+i\epsilon^{\tau}{{}_{\sigma\kappa}}\gamma^{\kappa}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\delta}}\big)\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu}{{}_{\nu\sigma}}\gamma^{\sigma}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\delta}}-i\delta^{\mu}{{}_{\nu}}\varepsilon_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu}{{}_{\nu\sigma}}\gamma^{\sigma}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\delta}}\\
&=-i\delta^{\mu}{{}_{\nu}}\varepsilon_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}\end{split}

(A.53)

Combining these results we find

\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\gamma_{\rho\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}\gamma_{\sigma\gamma}{{}^{\delta}}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\rho\sigma}\Big(\gamma_{\rho}\gamma_{\sigma\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}\varepsilon_{\gamma}{{}^{\beta}}+\gamma_{\rho}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}\gamma^{\nu}{{}_{\gamma}}{{}^{\beta}}\Big)=i\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\delta}}\varepsilon_{\gamma}{{}^{\beta}}-i\varepsilon_{\alpha}{{}^{\delta}}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\gamma}}{{}^{\beta}}

(A.54)

thus proving our lemma.∎

Finally, an interesting identity which could prove useful is

Lemma:
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
be any spinor space tensor. Then

{\mathcal{O}}^{\beta}{{}_{\alpha\beta}}={\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}{{}^{\beta}}{{}_{\beta}}+{\mathcal{O}}^{\beta}{{}_{\beta\alpha}}.

(A.55)

Proof:
Since antisymmetric 3-tensors in spinor space always vanish, we find that

0\equiv 3{\mathcal{O}}_{[\alpha\beta\gamma]}={\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha[\beta\gamma]}+{\mathcal{O}}_{[\gamma|\alpha|\beta]}+{\mathcal{O}}_{[\beta\gamma]\alpha}.

(A.56)

Contracting
\beta
and
\gamma
then yields the desired identity.∎

A.2.3
Hermitian Conjugation

Let us now make a few comments on Hermitian conjugation. Throughout this thesis we will repeatedly make use of norms of vectors, which arise from Hilbert space inner products, as opposed to the contractions of indices through the charge conjugation matrix
\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta}
. The former is more easy to understand using column matrices and the other in index notation. In this section of the appendix we wish to shed some light on this potentially confusing tension between these two notations.

To be specific, we note that

\displaystyle\zeta\eta

\displaystyle:=\zeta^{\alpha}\eta_{\alpha}=\zeta_{\alpha}\eta_{\beta}\varepsilon^{\beta\alpha}

\displaystyle(\zeta|\eta)

\displaystyle:=(\zeta_{\alpha})^{\ast}\eta_{\alpha}

\displaystyle|\zeta|^{2}

\displaystyle:=(\zeta_{\alpha})^{\ast}\zeta_{\alpha}

(A.57)

These are covariant expressions, however, the way the indices are contracted may be confusing to readers. Now looking at the column vector notation we find that

\displaystyle(\zeta|\eta)

\displaystyle:=\zeta^{\dagger}\eta

\displaystyle|\zeta|^{2}

\displaystyle:=\zeta^{\dagger}\zeta

(A.58)

interpreting now
\zeta
as a column matrix and
\zeta^{\dagger}
as a row matrix. As it might be regarded as rather confusing that there are two ways to look at these inner products we harmonise the two by introducing the spinor notation

\zeta^{\dagger\alpha}:=(\zeta_{\alpha})^{\ast}

(A.59)

in terms of representations this also makes sense, since if one is in the defining representation of SU(2) the other will be in the conjugate representation, which makes sense index-wise. We can now harmoniously write

\displaystyle(\zeta|\eta)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\dagger}\eta=\zeta^{\dagger\alpha}\eta_{\alpha}

\displaystyle|\zeta|^{2}

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\dagger}\zeta=\zeta^{\dagger\alpha}\zeta_{\alpha}

(A.60)

Let us now move on to the gamma matrices. We note that

\displaystyle(\gamma^{\mu})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\mu}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle(\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}})^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\beta}}{{}^{\alpha}}

(A.61)

As an example we find that

(\gamma^{\mu}\zeta)^{\dagger\alpha}=(\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}}\zeta_{\beta})^{\ast}=(\zeta_{\beta})^{\ast}(\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\alpha}}{{}^{\beta}})^{\ast}=\zeta^{\dagger\beta}\gamma^{\mu}{{}_{\beta}}{{}^{\alpha}}=\zeta^{\dagger}\gamma^{\mu\alpha}

(A.62)

which is nicely in agreement with the results one would expect from using matrices instead of index notation
[
11
]
.
